, even though strictly speaking the location of the indeces in this case could be take to mean that
\bm{V}
is covariant. The cases where such somewhat misleading notation is used are few and far in between, and we never use it in contexts where it could lead to any confusion or misconception.

The scalar product of two 4-vectors is given by

\displaystyle V\cdot U=g_{\mu\nu}V^{\mu}U^{\nu}=V_{\nu}U^{\nu}=V_{0}U^{0}+V_{i}U^{i}=V^{0}U^{0}-\bm{V}\cdot\bm{U}~.

(9)

In consequence

\displaystyle\bm{V}\cdot\bm{U}=-V_{k}U^{k}~.

(10)

The four-gradient is given by

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{x^{\mu}}}=\left(\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{x^{0}}},\bm{\nabla}\right)~.

(11)

where
x^{\mu}
is the position four-vector. In particular,

\displaystyle(\bm{\nabla})_{i}=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{x^{i}}}=-\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{x_{i}}}~.

(12)

A specific example of the consequences of the adopted notation is that the expression for the kinetic energy becomes

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\textrm{kinetic}}=\sqrt{\bm{p}^{2}+m^{2}}=\sqrt{-p_{k}p^{k}+m^{2}}~.

(13)

As another example, note that the conservation of current is given by

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{j^{0}}}{\partial{t}}+\bm{\nabla}\cdot\bm{j}=\frac{\partial{j^{0}}}{\partial{x^{0}}}+\sum_{i}\big(\bm{\nabla})_{i}j^{i}=\frac{\partial{j^{0}}}{\partial{x^{0}}}+\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{x^{i}}}j^{i}=\partial_{\mu}j^{\mu}=0~.

(14)

Chapter 8
Phase transitions

This appendix is largely based on Ref.
[
186
]
.

Phase transitions are processes involving a change between two states (phases) of a given medium characterized by different properties, and they can be largely divided into two types.

A first-order phase transition is characterized by a discontinuity of the first derivative of the thermodynamic potential (the Ehrenfest classification) and involves a latent heat (modern classification), that is, a finite amount of energy has to be released or absorbed for the system to undergo the phase transition. A canonical example of a first-order phase transition is the melting of ice: In order for this transition to occur, heat must be absorbed by the ice, and during this process of absorption there exist parts of the system that have already melted (parts that have completed the transition) and parts which are still frozen; in other words, there is a coexistence of phases. Note that the coexistence of the frozen and the melted phase means that different parts of the system are characterized by different densities corresponding to the densities of ice and water. This can be reformulated as the discontinuity of the first derivative of the pressure (the thermodynamic potential) with respect to the chemical potential,
\partial{P}/\partial{\mu}=n
.

A second-order phase transition is characterized by a discontinuity in the second derivative of the thermodynamic potential (the Ehrenfest classification) and involves a divergent susceptibility and an infinite correlation length (modern classification). A prominent example here is the ferromagnetic phase transition, within which the entire volume of a ferromagnet, when cooled below the transition temperature (known as the Curie temperature), develops a spontaneous magnetization, that is the magnetic moments in the system align with each other. Here the magnetization, which is the first derivative of the thermodynamic potential with respect to the external magnetic field, is zero above the Curie temperature and increases continuously from zero below the Curie temperature; consequently, the magnetic susceptibility, which is the second derivative of the thermodynamic potential with respect to the external magnetic field, is discontinuous.

It is known that the nuclear liquid-gas phase transition in the ordinary nuclear matter is of the first order, and effective models suggest that the phase transition from the hadronic to quark and gluon degrees of freedom may also be of first order in the region of the QCD phase diagram characterized by high values of the baryon chemical potential. Let us therefore discuss further certain features of this type of phase transitions.

Figure 1:
Phase diagrams in the temperature and chemical potential
(T,\mu)
, plane (left panel) and the temperature and density
(T,n)
plane (right panel), drawn for a hypothetical system with a first-order phase transition. Orange dots mark points at which the system is characterized by a given temperature
T_{1}
, chemical potential
\mu_{1}
, and pressure
P_{1}
(not shown); note that there are two such points in the
(T,n)
plane, indicating a coexistence of two phases with different densities,
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
. A continuum of coexistence points forms the coexistence curves, shown by the solid blue lines; note that in the
(T,n)
plane, the coexistence curves bound a region where a mixed phase occurs. The coexistence lines terminate either on another phase transition line, or on a critical point; these possibilities are not depicted here.

As already mentioned, first-order phase transitions are characterized by the occurrence of states with mixed phases, like melting ice floating in water or a liquid and its vapor. These coexisting phases are in mutual equilibrium, which means that they are characterized by the same temperature, pressure, and chemical potential,

\displaystyle T_{1}=T_{2}~,\hskip 28.45274ptP_{1}=P_{2}~,\hskip 28.45274pt\mu_{1}=\mu_{2}~.

(1)

If we plot the points at which two phases of a given system coexist, we obtain a phase diagram of a given substance. Phase diagrams can be drawn using different primary variables. For example in the case of the liquid-gas phase transition, it can be convenient to draw a phase diagram in the temperature and chemical potential
(T,\mu)
or temperature and density
(T,n)
planes, see Fig.
1
. Note that the coexistence of phases is given by a single line in the
(T,\mu)
plane, while the phase diagram of the same system in the
(T,n)
plane is characterized by a coexistence region bounded by two coexistence lines; this is because for every set of values
(T,P,\mu)
at which the two phases coexist, there are two corresponding coexistence densities,
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
. As can be clearly seen from the diagrams in Fig.
1
, the coexistence chemical potential or, alternatively, the coexistence densities are functions of temperature. Importantly, for a given temperature
T=T_{1}
at which the coexistence densities are given by
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
, an equilibrated system of
N
particles in volume
V
such that the density
n=N/V
satisfies
n_{1}<n<n_{2}
cannot exist. This is because the coexistence region of the phase diagram is thermodynamically and, in a specific subregion, also mechanically unstable (we will elaborate on this below). If such a system is obtained as a result of a non-equilibrium evolution (for example as a result of an adiabatic quench), the system will spontaneously separate into two phases occupying volumes
V_{1}=\alpha V
and
V_{2}=(1-\alpha)V
, where
\alpha={(n-n_{1})}/{(n_{2}-n_{1})}
(the latter is a simple consequence of the fact that the numbers of particles in each of the phases must satisfy
N_{1}+N_{2}=N
).

Let us now discuss isotherms of pressure as a function of density, see left panel in Fig.
2
. At a given temperature, for densities smaller than the lower (or “left”) coexistence density
n_{L}=n_{L}(T)
, the pressure is a monotonously rising function of the density
n
. Likewise, for densities larger than the upper (or “right”) coexistence density
n_{R}=n_{R}(T)
, the pressure is also monotonic and rising. That the pressure satisfies
dP/dn>0
is in fact a condition for the system to be mechanically stable (this can be easily seen by noticing that if the pressure decreased with density, the system would have a tendency to collapse in on itself).

Figure 2:
Isotherms of pressure as functions of the density
n
(left panel) and the chemical potential
\mu
(right panel), drawn for a hypothetical system with a first-order phase transition. Points on the pressure isotherms where two phases coexist are marked with orange dots, the coexistence regions are denoted with dashed blue lines, and the thermodynamically unstable parts of the pressure curves are denoted with purple lines; in particular, in the left panel, the solid purple lines show the functional form of the pressure, while the dashed purple lines show the Maxwell construction.

However, in order to have
P(n_{L})=P(n_{R})
the pressure must be a non-monotonic function of
n
for
n_{L}(T)<n<n_{R}(T)
, as depicted. In particular, this means that there exist densities for which
dP(T)/dn<0
. This shows that the matter in this region, called the spinodal region, is mechanically unstable: any system brought into the spinodal region (e.g., through a sudden quench) will undergo a spontaneous and violent separation into two coexisting phases with densities given by
n_{L}(T)
and
n_{R}(T)
. The boundaries of the spinodal region at a given temperature
T
, here denoted by
\eta_{L}
and
\eta_{R}
, are given by the condition
dP(\eta_{L,R},T)/dn=0
. The ranges of densities satisfying
n_{L}<n<\eta_{L}
and
\eta_{R}<n<n_{R}
, are thermodynamically, but not mechanically unstable; this means that the states of the system in these regions are metastable, as is the case for example for overheated liquid or supercooled vapor. For the isotherms of pressure shown as functions of the chemical potential, see right panel of Fig.
2
, the metastable and unstable portions of the isotherms are the ones that form a closed curve beginning and ending on the crossing point.

Figure 3:
Phase diagram in the temperature and density
(T,n)
plane, drawn for a hypothetical system with a first-order phase transition ending in a critical point. It is possible to devise a path in the phase diagram such that the system will go from phase 1 to phase 2 without experiencing the coexistence of phases.

We can further see in the left panel of Fig.
2
that as the temperature is increased, the width of the coexistence region (and likewise the width of the spinodal region) becomes smaller, until finally at a temperature
T=T_{c}
, known as the critical temperature, the coexistence lines merge at
n=n_{c}
, known as the critical density. The point
(T_{c},n_{c})
is known as the critical point. Importantly, for temperatures higher than the critical temperature
T_{c}
, the notion of the two phases of the studied substance does not have a well-defined sense: the system is neither in phase 1, nor in phase 2 (see Fig.
3
). Therefore, it is possible to devise a path in the phase diagram following which brings the system from phase 1 into phase 2 without matter ever separating into two phases. This underscores the notion that the concept of phases is somewhat arbitrary and is only really well-defined when the two phases coexist and are in contact. In the case of the liquid-gas phase transition, while below
T_{c}
the system may be described as being either in the liquid or the gas phase, above
T_{c}
, to stress the fact that these two descriptors do not apply anymore, it is usually referred to as a “fluid”.

An in-depth review of the thermodynamics related to the first-order phase transitions in general, and to the spinodal region in particular, can be found in Ref.
[
187
]
.

Chapter 9
Kinematic variables, collision geometry, and baryon transport in heavy-ion collisions

Appendix 9.A
Kinematic variables

This section is based on Ref.
[
223
]
.

The kinematics of relativistic particles in heavy-ion collisions is often described using two notable variables. Customarily, the direction of the heavy-ion beam defines the
z
-axis of the collision systems. If we then consider a relativistic particle with a 4-momentum
p^{\mu}=(E,\bm{p})
, where
\bm{p}=(p_{x},p_{y},p_{z})
, then the transverse mass,
m_{T}
, can be defined as

\displaystyle m_{T}^{2}\equiv E^{2}-p_{z}^{2}=m^{2}+p_{T}^{2}~,

(1)

where
p_{z}=|\bm{\hat{p}}_{z}|
is the component of the particle’s momentum along the beam axis, often called the longitudinal momentum,
m
is the particle’s rest mass, and
p_{T}=|\bm{p}_{T}|=|p_{x}\bm{\hat{x}}+p_{y}\bm{\hat{y}}|
is the component of the particle’s momentum transverse to the beam direction, called the transverse momentum. It is easy to see that the transverse mass is invariant under Lorentz transformations along the beam direction.

The rapidity of the particle,
y_{r}
, is defined as

\displaystyle y_{r}\equiv\frac{1}{2}\ln\frac{E+p_{z}}{E-p_{z}}=\frac{1}{2}\ln\frac{1+\beta}{1-\beta}~,

(2)

where the second equality uses the longitudinal velocity of the particle
\beta=p_{z}/E
. (Here we note that while in this appendix we denote rapidity as
y_{r}
to avoid confusion with the
y
-axis in the coordinate space, it is customarily denoted with just
y
; the distinction between the two variables is usually clear from the context.) The rapidity can be thought of as a measure of the contribution of the particle’s velocity in the beam direction to the particle’s total energy. Note that if
p_{z}=0
, then
y_{r}=0
.

The advantage of
y_{r}
, as compared to the velocity
\beta
, is that it transforms more easily under Lorentz boosts. Consider two observers,
A
and
B
, where the observer
B
is moving with a constant velocity
\beta_{0}
with respect to the observer
A
(to simplify, let us consider only one spatial dimension). If
B
observes some object
C
moving with velocity
\beta^{\prime}
, then
A
sees the same object moving with velocity

\displaystyle\beta\equiv\frac{dx}{dt}=\frac{\gamma_{0}\big(dx^{\prime}+\beta_{0}dt^{\prime}\big)}{\gamma_{0}\big(dt^{\prime}+\beta_{0}dx^{\prime}\big)}=\frac{\beta^{\prime}+\beta_{0}}{1+\beta_{0}\beta^{\prime}}~,

(3)

that is the velocities in the two frames are related by a composition law. On the other hand, the rapidities reported by the two observers are simply related by

\displaystyle y_{r}=\frac{1}{2}\ln\frac{\gamma_{0}\big(E^{\prime}+\beta_{0}p_{z}^{\prime}\big)+\gamma_{0}\big(p_{z}^{\prime}+\beta_{0}E^{\prime}\big)}{\gamma_{0}\big(E^{\prime}+\beta_{0}p_{z}^{\prime}\big)-\gamma_{0}\big(p_{z}^{\prime}+\beta_{0}E^{\prime}\big)}=\frac{1}{2}\ln\frac{(E^{\prime}+p_{z}^{\prime})(1+\beta_{0})}{(E^{\prime}-p_{z}^{\prime})(1-\beta_{0})}=y_{r}^{\prime}+y_{0}~,

(4)

where
y_{0}
is the rapidity of
B
with respect to
A
, so that the rapidities in the two frames are additive. In particular, this means that a difference of rapidities,
\Delta y_{r}=y_{r}^{(1)}-y_{r}^{(2)}
, is a Lorentz-invariant quantity (which is not the case for the difference of velocities). This is especially useful in the context of heavy-ion collisions, where many observables either depend on the difference in rapidities of two observed particles (e.g., two-particle correlations), or are calculated within a certain chosen rapidity window
\Delta y
(e.g., cumulants of baryon number).

Figure 1:
A plot showing the relation between values of the pseudorapidity
\eta
and the polar angle
\theta=\measuredangle\big(\bm{\hat{p}},\bm{\hat{z}}\big)
. Note that
\eta\big(\theta=\frac{\pi}{2}\big)=0
and
\eta\big(\theta=0\big)=+\infty
.

In experiment, it is not always possible to identify the mass of a particle, necessary to calculate the rapidity, Eq. (
2
). For particles with very high momentum, however, the mass contribution to the energy can often be neglected,
E\approx p
, where
p=|\bm{p}|
. In that case the rapidity is approximately equal to the pseudorapidity
\eta
, which is defined as

\displaystyle\eta\equiv\frac{1}{2}\ln\frac{p+p_{z}}{p-p_{z}}=\frac{1}{2}\ln\frac{1+\frac{p_{z}}{p}}{1-\frac{p_{z}}{p}}=\frac{1}{2}\ln\frac{1+\cos\theta}{1-\cos\theta}=-\ln\tan\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)~,

(5)

where
\theta
is the angle between the direction of the particle’s momentum and the beam axis,
\theta=\measuredangle\big(\bm{\hat{p}},\bm{\hat{z}}\big)
. Being a function of
\theta
only, pseudorapidity has a clear geometrical interpretation. In particular, a detector often covers a well-defined range of
\theta
with respect to the beam axis, and it is not unusual for particles with high values of pseudorapidity to fall beyond the region where detection is possible. Note that a pseudorapidity window
\Delta\eta=1
around
\eta=0
covers about a quarter of the entire range of the angle
\theta
, while for example the same rapidity window about
\eta=4
covers only a small angle, as shown in Fig.
1
. This is a consequence of the non-linear dependence of
\eta
on
\theta
. For the same reason, a particle distribution that is isotropic in the coordinate space (that is a distribution uniform in
\sin\theta d\theta~d\phi
) is, when expressed in terms of
\eta
, a distribution with a peak at small values of
\eta
. The same conclusions can be approximately applied to the rapidity. In consequence, experimental observables are often considered at small (relative to the beam rapidity) values of rapidity or pseudorapidity; the corresponding range of considered values of
y_{r}
or
\eta
is referred to as “midrapity” or “central rapidity region”.

Notably, using the transverse mass and rapidity, it is possible to write the energy and longitudinal momentum of a particle as

\displaystyle E=m_{T}\cosh y_{r}\hskip 15.0pt{\textrm{and}}\hskip 15.0ptp_{z}=m_{T}\sinh y_{r}~,

(6)

respectively.

Using the center-of-mass frame for expressing the beam energy allows one to make easy comparisons between collisions in the collider mode and collisions in the target mode. Conveniently, for collisions of identical nuclei of mass
M
moving with velocities of equal magnitudes and opposite directions, the lab frame is the center-of-mass frame. If one denotes the 4-momentum of one of the nuclei as
p_{1}^{\mu}=(E,0,0,p_{z})
, then the 4-momentum of the other nucleus is
p_{2}^{\mu}=(E,0,0,-p_{z})
, and the Lorentz invariant Mandelstam variable
s
satisfies
\sqrt{s}=\sqrt{\big(p_{1}^{\mu}+p_{2}^{\mu}\big)^{2}}=2E
. For collisions in the target mode, in which one of the nuclei is at rest while the other nucleus is moving with energy
E
(in the lab frame), one has
\sqrt{s}=\sqrt{2M(M+E)}=\sqrt{2M(2M+E_{\textrm{kin}})}
, where
E_{\textrm{kin}}\equiv E-M
is the kinetic energy of the projectile nucleus. To enable comparisons between collisions of nuclei with different mass numbers
A
, one further defines
\sqrt{s_{NN}}
as the center-of-mass energy of two protons colliding with kinetic energy
E_{\textrm{kin},NN}=E_{\textrm{kin}}/A
. Up to a slight discrepancy due to neglecting the binding energy of the nuclei,
\sqrt{s_{NN}}
can be thought of as the center-of-mass energy per nucleon pair in the system,
\sqrt{s_{NN}}\approx\sqrt{s}/A
.

Appendix 9.B
Collision geometry

Figure 2:
A sketch of a heavy-ion collision geometry in the collider mode. Note that two nuclei moving at relativistic speeds should be Lorentz contracted, but the sketch does not include the contraction for clarity. The outer cylinder on the sketch, indicated with a solid gray line, is supposed to indicate the geometry of a typical detector such as STAR, but its relative dimensions are not to scale to make the figure more clear. The nuclei are moving along the beam pipe, coincident with the
z
-axis, indicated with a long-dashed gray line. The short-dashed red line indicates the reaction plane, the dotted blue line indicates the transverse plane, and the green dash-dotted line indicates the intersection of the reaction and transverse planes, where
y_{r}=0
. The insert shows the geometry of the collision when looking along the beam pipe. See text for more details.

Fig.
2
shows a simplified sketch of a heavy-ion collision in the collider mode. Two heavy ions are moving along the beam pipe, which is coincident with the
z
-axis, with equal energies
E=\sqrt{m^{2}+p_{z}^{2}}
. In the figure, the orange heavy ion moves with momentum
\bm{p}_{1}=+p_{z}\bm{\hat{z}}
, while the turquoise heavy ion moves with momentum
\bm{p}_{2}=-p_{z}\bm{\hat{z}}
. The impact parameter
b
of the collision is defined as the length of the vector
\bm{b}
connecting the centers of the colliding nuclei at the point of closest approach (see the insert in Fig.
2
). The
z
-axis together with
\bm{b}
create a plane called the reaction plane, indicated in the figure by short-dashed red lines. One can also define the transverse plane, which is a plane perpendicular to the beam axis, indicated in the figure with a blue dotted line; usually, the intersection of the beam axis and the transverse plane is set at a point where the rapidity is zero,
y_{r}=0
; in the figure, the intersection of the reaction and the transverse planes is indicated by the green dash-dotted line. The directions in the transverse plane are given by the
x
-axis and the
y
-axis, as indicated on the insert in Fig.
2
.

Figure 3:
A plot of different collision centralities, corresponding to a series of impact parameters
b
, shown in a view along the beam axis. The concentric rings indicate regions of the same density. The sketch does not include the intrinsic fluctuations in the positions of nucleons inside the nuclei. Figure from
[
224
]
.

Collisions can occur at different values of the impact parameter
b
, see Fig.
3
. Nucleons from the two nuclei which overlap in the transverse plane are called participants. The remaining nucleons, which “detach” from the colliding nucleus at the moment of the collision and then continue to move with the beam momentum, are called spectators. Different impact parameters will lead to different geometries of the initial collision region, numbers of participants, and initial energy depositions. Therefore, to enable meaningful comparisons between different geometries of the initial systems, collisions are sorted into classes corresponding to ranges of impact parameters. Because measuring the impact parameter itself is impossible in experiment, the collisions are instead characterized by a parameter known as centrality. There are many ways of establishing the centrality of a collision, varying between detectors, however, the intuitive picture for most of these methods is the following: Collisions with a small impact parameter will lead to a greater amount of energy deposited in the collision region and through that to a larger number of produced particles and a larger number of detected charged particles, or charged particle multiplicity,
N_{\textrm{ch}}
. When the collisions are sorted by their respective values of
N_{\textrm{ch}}
, one can define specific ranges of
N_{\textrm{ch}}
as corresponding to “more central” or “less central” collisions. And so, for example, one can define the most central collisions as the top 10% of all collisions with respect to the number of detected charged particles
N_{\textrm{ch}}
; such collisions are then said to be characterized by a 0–10% centrality. Correspondingly, one can define mid-central collisions as those falling between the top 10% and the top 40% of all collisions, and peripheral collisions as those falling in the top 40%–80%. Typically, the most peripheral collisions, in this case the bottom 20%, are discarded to avoid biases due to detector trigger inefficiencies. The entire range of used centralities, here 0–80%, is referred to as “minimum bias”. We note that the ranges defining the centralities are chosen to best suit a specific analysis and a particular experiment, and are by no means set to the example values provided above.

Appendix 9.C
Baryon transport

The initial rapidity of the beam, or equivalently the collision energy, influences the rapidity distributions of detected particles. Initially, the transverse momentum of the entire system as well as of each of the nucleons inside the colliding nuclei is zero (here, we neglect the Fermi momenta of the nucleons inside the nuclei). Because momentum is conserved, the total transverse momentum after the collision is also zero, but individual particles (both transported as well as produced) develop non-zero values of
p_{T}
through scattering or collective motion of the system (e.g., collective flow). At the same time, on average, there is only so much transverse momentum that a particle can plausibly develop through these means. Indeed, head-on collisions of quarks or gluons can result in scattering products with almost zero longitudinal momentum and a large fraction of their initial energy converted into the transverse momenta; for example, at
\sqrt{s_{NN}}=200\ \textrm{GeV}
, which corresponds to
E_{\textrm{kin},NN}\approx 100\textrm{GeV}
, the individual quarks inside the proton may typically have
p_{z}\approx 10
–
20\ \textrm{GeV}
, and so, if two such quarks collide, there is a finite chance to produce a
10
–
20\ \textrm{GeV}
transverse jet (where by a jet we understand a collection of final state particles originating from a single hard scattering at the initial stage of the collision). However, aside from these somewhat exceptional events, ordinarily the final transverse momentum of a detected particle is much smaller, on the order of
p_{T}\approx 0.5\ \textrm{GeV}
(the average transverse momentum of protons tends to be
p_{T}\approx 1.0\ \textrm{GeV}
, see below for more details).

A typical detector can detect particles with pseudorapidities
|\eta|\leq 1.5
(and therefore, approximately, rapidities
|y_{r}|\leq 1.5
), while the beam rapidity
y_{\textrm{beam}}
(that is the rapidity of a single proton moving with
p_{\textrm{beam},NN}
) characterizing the collisions is often much higher. It is evident that a large change in the rapidity,
\Delta y_{r}=y_{r}^{\prime}-y_{r}
, requires a correspondingly large change in the longitudinal momentum,
\Delta p_{z}
, where the latter will also grow with the magnitude of
y_{r}^{\prime}
. Changes in the rapidity of baryons occurring during the collisions can be established by measuring the rapidity distribution of net protons
dN_{p-\bar{p}}/dy_{r}
at a given collision energy and centrality. Fig.
5
in Chapter
Density Functional Equation of State

and Its Application to the Phenomenology of Heavy-Ion Collisions
shows
dN_{p-\bar{p}}/dy_{r}
as measured in collisions with 0–5% centrality at the AGS (Au+Au collisions at
\sqrt{s_{NN}}=5\ \textrm{GeV}
,
y_{\textrm{beam}}=1.64
)
[
42
]
, SPS (Pb+Pb collisions at
\sqrt{s_{NN}}=17.2\ \textrm{GeV}
,
y_{\textrm{beam}}=2.91
)
[
43
]
, and RHIC (Au+Au collisions at
\sqrt{s_{NN}}=200\ \textrm{GeV}
,
y_{\textrm{beam}}=5.36
)
[
41
]
. We see that for the lowest collision energies the net proton distribution is peaked around
y_{r}\approx 0
, and the mean absolute value of rapidity is
\langle|y_{r}|\rangle\approx 0.7
(note that mean rapidity
\langle y_{r}\rangle
is always 0 due to the geometry of symmetric collisions in the center-of-mass frame), while for collisions at the SPS the distribution has two peaks around
y_{r}\approx\pm 1.3
, with the mean absolute rapidity of about
\langle|y_{r}|\rangle\approx 1.22
. For collisions at RHIC the peaks of the distribution are beyond the reach of the detector, but numerical fits suggest the peaks at about
y_{r}\approx 4.25
, and the corresponding mean absolute rapidity is
\langle|y_{r}|\rangle\approx 3.32
. The average rapidity loss, defined as
\langle\delta y_{r}\rangle\equiv y_{\textrm{beam}}-\langle|y_{r}|\rangle
, for the three experiments is then approximately
1.0
,
1.7
, and
2.0
, respectively.

It is instructive to ask what is the change in the longitudinal momentum (between the initial and final state) of a typical proton measured within a given rapidity window, assuming that the proton has remained intact throughout the collision. (Here one has to bear in mind that this exercise only serves to develop a rough intuition about possible final longitudinal and transverse momenta in the system, as in a true collision any given final state particle will not only be a product of multiple scatterings and decays, but will also most probably originate in the deconfined QGP. One can hope, however, that final state protons can be used as a good proxy for the effects of the complex evolution of the baryon number, itself originating in the protons and neutrons from the colliding nuclei.) We consider protons with transverse momentum
p_{T}=0.9\ \textrm{GeV}
. This value is chosen based on the BRAHMS experiment analysis of Au+Au collisions at
\sqrt{s_{NN}}=200\ \textrm{GeV}
and 0–5% centrality, which measured
\langle p_{T}\rangle=1.01\pm 0.01(\textrm{stat})\ \textrm{GeV}
at
y_{r}\approx 0
and
\langle p_{T}\rangle=0.84\pm 0.01(\textrm{stat})\ \textrm{GeV}
at
y_{r}\approx 3

[
41
]
. For comparison, at lower energies, for example in Pb+Pb collisions at
\sqrt{s_{NN}}=17.2\ \textrm{GeV}
and 0–5% centrality, the mean transverse momentum of protons is
\langle p_{T}\rangle=0.825\pm 37\ \textrm{GeV}
at
y_{r}=0
and
\langle p_{T}\rangle=0.600\pm 17\ \textrm{GeV}
at
y_{r}=2.4

[
43
]
.

Table
1
lists values of
p_{\textrm{beam},NN}
and the beam rapidity
y_{\textrm{beam}}
corresponding to different values of the center-of-mass energy
\sqrt{s_{NN}}
. Also listed are values of the longitudinal momentum difference
\Delta p_{z}\equiv p_{\textrm{beam},NN}-p_{z,\textrm{final}}
required for the final state proton to have a transverse momentum of
p_{T}=0.9\ \textrm{GeV}
and a rapidity of
y_{0}
, where we consider
y_{0}=\{1,2,3,4\}
. Negative values of
\Delta p_{z}
signify a longitudinal momentum loss (
y_{0}<y_{\textrm{beam}}
), while positive values correspond to a gain in longitudinal momentum (
y_{0}>y_{\textrm{beam}}
). In experiment, collision participants tend to lose momentum due to the initial energy deposition in the collision region and scattering, although momentum gains are also possible through either collective effects or as excited remnants of the sheared off nuclei emit particles.

From the results of our simple calculation, displayed in Table
1
, we see that decreasing the rapidity of a proton moving with
p_{\textrm{beam},NN}
at
\sqrt{s_{NN}}=200.0\ \textrm{GeV}
by about 2 units of rapidity requires a momentum loss of
65
–
85\ \textrm{GeV}
. We can compare this value with the experiment, where an average energy of measured baryons can be calculated through

\displaystyle\langle E\rangle=\frac{1}{N_{\textrm{part}}}\int_{-y_{\textrm{beam}}}^{+y_{\textrm{beam}}}dy_{r}~\Big\langle E(y_{r})\Big\rangle~\frac{dN_{B-\bar{B}}}{dy_{r}}~.

(7)

The difference
\delta E\equiv E_{\textrm{beam}}-\langle E\rangle
is the average energy loss, and in
\sqrt{s_{NN}}=200.0\ \textrm{GeV}
Au+Au collisions at 0–5% centrality it has been established to be equal
\Delta E=73\pm 6\ \textrm{GeV}

[
41
]
, which agrees with our basic estimate. Importantly,
\Delta E
can be thought of as the energy deposited in the collision region, available for, e.g., particle production.

We stress that although the values of
\Delta p_{z}(y_{0})
listed in Table
1
may aid in building an intuition about the kinematics of a heavy-ion collision, one should remember that the evolution of heavy-ion collision is much more complicated than the simple picture suggested here and includes, among others, formation of the fireball, deconfinement, evolution of a strongly-interacting QGP, and hadronization.

The energy dependence of net proton rapidity distributions (see Fig.
5
in Chapter
Density Functional Equation of State

and Its Application to the Phenomenology of Heavy-Ion Collisions
) shows that although in very high-energy collisions a significant amount of longitudinal momentum is converted into energy available for particle production, the midrapidity region is mostly baryon-free. The opposite is true at the lowest energies, where almost all baryon number participating in the collision is found within one unit of rapidity around
y_{r}=0
. Models explaining this behavior exist, however, the exact mechanism of transporting baryon number to smaller absolute values of rapidity, also known as “baryon stopping”, is unknown and continues to be a subject of ongoing research.

Chapter 10
Cumulants in terms of derivatives of the pressure with respect to the net baryon number density

We start by noting that, based on Eq. (
27
), the cumulants satisfy

\displaystyle\kappa_{j+1}\equiv T({d\kappa_{j}}/{d\mu_{B}})_{T}~.

(1)

The first cumulant is given by

\displaystyle\kappa_{1}\equiv V\left(\frac{dP}{d\mu_{B}}\right)_{T}=Vn_{B}~.

(2)

Following Eq. (
1
), the second cumulant is given by

\displaystyle\kappa_{2}\equiv VT\left(\frac{d{n_{B}}}{d{\mu_{B}}}\right)_{T}~.

(3)

From
dP=Tds+n_{B}d\mu_{B}
we know that

\displaystyle\left(\frac{d{\mu_{B}}}{d{n_{B}}}\right)_{T}=\frac{1}{n_{B}}\left(\frac{d{P}}{d{n_{B}}}\right)_{T}~,

(4)

so that Eq. (
3
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\kappa_{2}=\frac{VTn_{B}}{\left(\frac{d{P}}{d{n_{B}}}\right)_{T}}~.

(5)

Similarly, the third cumulant is given by

\displaystyle\kappa_{3}\equiv T\left(\frac{d{\kappa_{2}}}{d{\mu_{B}}}\right)_{T}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle VT^{2}~\left(\frac{dn_{B}}{d\mu_{B}}\right)_{T}\frac{d}{dn_{B}}\bigg|_{T}\left[\frac{n_{B}}{\left(\frac{dP}{dn_{B}}\right)_{T}}\right]

(6)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{VT^{2}n_{B}}{\left(\frac{dP}{dn_{B}}\right)^{2}}\left[1-\frac{n_{B}}{\left(\frac{dP}{dn_{B}}\right)_{T}}\left(\frac{d^{2}P}{dn_{B}^{2}}\right)_{T}\right]~.

where in the second equality we have used Eq. (
5
) and in the third equality we again utilized Eq. (
4
). Finally, the fourth cumulant can be obtained in the same way, yielding

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\kappa_{4}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle T\left(\frac{d\kappa_{3}}{d\mu_{B}}\right)_{T}=T\left(\frac{d{n_{B}}}{d{\mu_{B}}}\right)_{T}\left(\frac{d\kappa_{3}}{dn_{B}}\right)_{T}\hskip 227.62204pt

(7)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{VT^{3}n_{B}}{\left(\frac{dP}{dn_{B}}\right)_{T}^{3}}\left[1-\frac{4n_{B}}{\left(\frac{dP}{dn_{B}}\right)_{T}}\left(\frac{d^{2}P}{dn_{B}^{2}}\right)_{T}+\frac{3n_{B}^{2}}{\left(\frac{dP}{dn_{B}}\right)_{T}^{2}}\left(\frac{d^{2}P}{dn_{B}^{2}}\right)_{T}^{2}-\frac{n_{B}^{2}}{\left(\frac{dP}{dn_{B}}\right)_{T}}\left(\frac{d^{3}P}{dn_{B}^{3}}\right)_{T}\right]~.

Chapter 11
The static limit of the Walecka model

This appendix is based on Ref.
[
225
]
.

Appendix 11.A
A simple case of a scalar interaction field

Let us consider a simple 2-nucleon scattering as shown in Fig.
1
(note that by considering only this diagram we neglect the exchange term, which is equivalent to a simplifying assumption that the nucleons taking part in the interaction are distinguishable). We can explicitly write the conditions for energy and momentum conservation (throughout the entire scattering event and at each vertex) as

\displaystyle\bm{p}_{1}+\bm{p}_{2}=\bm{p}_{3}+\bm{p}_{4}~,

(1)

\displaystyle E_{1}+E_{2}=E_{3}+E_{4}~,

(2)

\displaystyle\bm{p}_{3}=\bm{p}_{1}-\bm{q}~,

(3)

\displaystyle\bm{p}_{4}=\bm{p}_{2}+\bm{q}~,

(4)

\displaystyle E_{3}=E_{1}-q_{0}~,

(5)

\displaystyle E_{4}=E_{2}+q_{0}~.

(6)

Then we easily see that we can write, for example,

\displaystyle q_{0}=E_{4}-E_{2}=\sqrt{\bm{p}_{4}^{2}+M^{2}}-\sqrt{\bm{p}_{2}^{2}+M^{2}}~.

(7)

If the nucleon mass
M
is very large (or, conversely, the nucleons are not too fast), which is known as the limit of static sources, then we can approximate

\displaystyle q_{0}=M\left(\sqrt{1+\frac{\bm{p}_{4}^{2}}{M^{2}}}-\sqrt{1+\frac{\bm{p}_{2}^{2}}{M^{2}}}\right)\approx\frac{\bm{p}_{4}^{2}-\bm{p}_{2}^{2}}{2M}~.

(8)

p_{1}
p_{3}
p_{4}
p_{2}
q

Figure 1:
A diagram of two-nucleon scattering occurring through an exchange of a scalar interaction boson.

Now, let us assume that the scattering is described by the interaction Lagrangian density

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{I}=g\bar{\psi}\psi\phi

(9)

where
\phi
denotes a scalar interaction field and
g
is the coupling constant. The propagator of the scalar interaction field quanta is given by

\displaystyle D(q)=\frac{i}{q^{2}-m_{s}^{2}}~,

(10)

where
q
is the momentum transfer and
m_{s}
denotes the mass of the field quanta, so that the lowest order scattering matrix element in the momentum space is given by

\displaystyle S_{fi}=(-ig)^{2}~(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{4}\big(k_{1}+k_{2}-k_{3}-k_{4}\big)~\frac{1}{\Omega^{2}}~\frac{i}{q^{2}+m_{f}^{2}}~\bar{u}_{a}(k_{3})u_{a}(k_{1})\bar{u}_{b}(k_{4})u_{b}(k_{2})~,

(11)

where
\Omega
is the quantization volume. Using the limit of static sources, Eq. (
8
), we get

\displaystyle D(q)=\frac{i}{q_{0}^{2}-\vec{q}^{2}-m_{s}^{2}}\approx\frac{i}{\left(\frac{\bm{p}_{4}^{2}-\bm{p}_{2}^{2}}{2M}\right)^{2}-\bm{q}^{2}-m_{s}^{2}}\approx-\frac{i}{|\bm{q}|^{2}+m_{s}^{2}}~,

(12)

so that Eq. (
11
) becomes

\displaystyle S_{fi}=(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{4}\big(k_{1}+k_{2}-k_{3}-k_{4}\big)~\frac{1}{\Omega^{2}}~\frac{ig^{2}}{|\bm{q}|^{2}+m_{s}^{2}}~\bar{u}_{a}(k_{3})u_{a}(k_{1})\bar{u}_{b}(k_{4})u_{b}(k_{2})~.

(13)

On the other hand, to the lowest order in nonrelativistic potential scattering we should have

\displaystyle S_{fi}^{(1)}=-i\int d^{4}x~\mathcal{H}_{I}(x)~.

(14)

In the formalism of the second quantization this can be rewritten as

\displaystyle S_{fi}^{(1)}=-i\int d^{4}x\int d^{4}y~\psi^{\dagger}_{a}(x)\psi^{\dagger}_{b}(y)~V_{\textrm{eff}}(y-x)~\psi_{b}(y)\psi_{a}(x)~.

(15)

We can always express
\psi
in terms of its Fourier components

\displaystyle\psi=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\Omega}}\sum_{\lambda}\sum_{\bm{k}}a_{\lambda\bm{k}}\chi_{\lambda}e^{-k\cdot x}~,

(16)

which leads to

\displaystyle S_{fi}^{(1)}=-\frac{i}{\Omega^{2}}(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{4}(k_{1}+k_{2}-k_{3}-k_{4})~\widetilde{V}_{\textrm{eff}}(\bm{q})~,

(17)

where
\bm{q}=\bm{k}_{1}-\bm{k}_{3}=\bm{k}_{4}-\bm{k}_{2}
. Upon comparing Eqs. (
13
) and (
17
) we see that we must have

\displaystyle\widetilde{V}_{\textrm{eff}}(\bm{q})=-\frac{g^{2}}{|\bm{q}|^{2}+m_{s}^{2}}~.

(18)

To recover the potential in the position space we need to Fourier transform
\widetilde{V}_{\textrm{eff}}(q)
,

\displaystyle V_{\textrm{eff}}(\bm{r})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\frac{d^{3}\bm{q}}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\widetilde{V}_{\textrm{eff}}(q)e^{i\bm{q}\cdot\bm{r}}

(19)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}dq~q^{2}\int_{-1}^{+1}d(\cos\theta)~\widetilde{V}_{\textrm{eff}}(q)e^{iqr\cos\theta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{g^{2}}{4\pi^{2}~ir}\int_{0}^{\infty}dq~q~\frac{e^{iqr}-e^{-iqr}}{q^{2}+m_{s}^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{g^{2}}{4\pi^{2}~ir}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dq~q~\frac{e^{iqr}}{q^{2}+m_{s}^{2}}~.

The last integral has to be done by means of complex integration. We can always rewrite

\displaystyle\frac{qe^{iqr}}{q^{2}+m_{s}^{2}}=\frac{e^{iqr}}{2}\left(\frac{1}{q+im_{s}}+\frac{1}{q-im_{s}}\right)~,

(20)

from which we immediately see that closing the contour in the upper half plane encloses the
q=im_{s}
pole. Since now the integral is just
2\pi i
times the residue of the pole with an overall plus sign as the contour is traversed counterclockwise, we have

\displaystyle V_{\textrm{eff}}(\bm{r})=-\frac{g^{2}}{4\pi}~\frac{e^{-m_{s}r}}{r}~,

(21)

which is an attractive Yukawa potential.

Appendix 11.B
The Walecka model

In the Walecka model the interaction Lagrangian density is of the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{I}=ig_{\omega}\hat{\psi}\gamma_{\lambda}\psi\omega^{\lambda}+g_{\sigma}\bar{\psi}\psi\sigma~,

(22)

which leads to the momentum-space potential

\displaystyle V_{W}(q)=g_{\omega}^{2}\frac{\gamma_{\lambda}^{(1)}\cdot\gamma_{\lambda}^{(2)}}{q^{2}-m_{\omega}^{2}-i\epsilon}-g_{\sigma}^{2}\frac{\mathbb{1}^{(1)}\cdot\mathbb{1}^{(2)}}{q^{2}-m_{\sigma}^{2}-i\epsilon}~,

(23)

where
q
is the four-momentum transfer and
\gamma_{\lambda}^{(1,2)}
refer to the first and second particle, respectively. If we assume that the baryons are nonrelativistic (which is equivalent to assuming that they are heavy), we can approximate

\displaystyle\gamma_{\lambda}^{(1)}\cdot\gamma_{\lambda}^{(2)}\hskip 5.0pt\to\hskip 5.0pt\mathbb{1}^{(1)}\cdot\mathbb{1}^{(2)}

(24)

and, just as before,

\displaystyle|q_{0}|\ll|\bm{q}|~,

(25)

in which case the potential becomes instantaneous and spin-independent. Following similar steps as before we can be write
V_{W}(q)
in the coordinate space as

\displaystyle V(r)=\frac{1}{4\pi}\left(g_{\omega}^{2}\frac{e^{-mr}}{r}-g_{\sigma}^{2}\frac{e^{-m_{\sigma}r}}{r}\right)~.

(26)

If
g_{\omega}^{2}>g_{\sigma}^{2}
, this potential is repulsive at short distances. If additionally
m_{\omega}>m_{\sigma}
, the potential will be attractive at large distances. In result, such an interaction will display all main features of the nucleon-nucleon interaction, and indeed we can see that its form is just like that of a Lennard-Jones–type potential.

Chapter 12
Dirac field with vector and scalar interactions

Appendix 12.A
The Dirac equation

In general, the Dirac equation for a fermion interacting with a classical vector field
A^{\mu}
and a classical scalar field
S
is given by

\displaystyle\Big(i\gamma^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}-\gamma^{\mu}A_{\mu}-m+S\Big)\psi(x)=0~;

(1)

the above equation can be brought to the form displayed in Eq. (
13
) by making the fields
A_{\mu}
and
S
appropriately dependent on mean fields. Multiplying Eq. (
1
) by
\gamma^{0}
from the left and writing out the terms leads to

\displaystyle i\partial_{0}\psi(x)=\Big(-i\gamma^{0}\bm{\gamma}\cdot\bm{\nabla}-\gamma^{0}\bm{\gamma}\cdot\bm{A}+A_{0}+\gamma^{0}(m-S)\Big)\psi(x)~.

(2)

We know that the momentum operator is
\bm{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}\equiv-i\bm{\nabla}
, so that furthermore we have

\displaystyle\hskip-36.98857pti\partial_{0}\psi(x)=\Big(\gamma^{0}\bm{\gamma}\cdot\bm{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}-\gamma^{0}\bm{\gamma}\cdot\bm{A}+A_{0}+\gamma^{0}(m-S)\Big)\psi(x)

(3)

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt=\Big(\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&\bm{\sigma}\cdot\bm{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}\\
\bm{\sigma}\cdot\bm{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}&0\end{array}\right]-\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&\bm{\sigma}\cdot\bm{A}\\
\bm{\sigma}\cdot\bm{A}&0\end{array}\right]+A_{0}\mathbb{1}_{4}+\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathbb{1}_{2}&0\\
0&-\mathbb{1}_{2}\end{array}\right](m-S)\Big)\psi(x)~.

where in the second line we have used the explicit form of the gamma matrices in the Dirac representation,

\displaystyle\gamma^{0}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathbb{1}&0\\
0&-\mathbb{1}\end{array}\right]~,\hskip 15.0pt\gamma^{i}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&\sigma_{i}\\
-\sigma_{i}&0\end{array}\right]~,

where
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}
are Pauli matrices. Taking the wavefunction
\psi
to be a stationary solution and expressing it in the bispinor form,

\displaystyle\psi=e^{-iEt}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\varphi\\
\chi\end{array}\right)~,

leads to two coupled equations,

\displaystyle E\varphi=\left[\big(\bm{\sigma}\cdot\bm{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}\big)-\Big(\bm{\sigma}\cdot\bm{A}(x)\Big)\right]\chi+\Big[A_{0}+(m-S)\Big]\varphi~,

(19)

\displaystyle E\chi=\left[\big(\bm{\sigma}\cdot\bm{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}\big)-\Big(\bm{\sigma}\cdot\bm{A}(x)\Big)\right]\varphi+\Big[A_{0}-(m-S)\Big]\chi~,

(20)

where we have made the dependence of the
\bm{A}
field on the position explicit. The second of the above equations can be solved for
\chi
in terms of
\varphi
,

\displaystyle\chi=\frac{\left[\bm{\sigma}\cdot\Big(\bm{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}-\bm{A}(x)\Big)\right]}{E-\Big[A_{0}-(m-S)\Big]}~\varphi~,

(21)

which inserted into the first equation results in

\displaystyle E\varphi=\left(\frac{\left[\bm{\sigma}\cdot\Big(\bm{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}-\bm{A}(x)\Big)\right]^{2}}{E-\Big[A_{0}-(m-S)\Big]}+\Big[A_{0}+(m-S)\Big]\right)\varphi~.

(22)

Denoting
\bm{\Pi}=\bm{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}-\bm{A}(x)
we further arrive at

\displaystyle\bigg(\big[E-A_{0}\big]^{2}-(m-S)^{2}\bigg)\varphi=\big[\bm{\sigma}\cdot\bm{\Pi}\big]^{2}\varphi~.

(23)

Appendix 12.B
Evaluating
\big[\sigma\cdot\Pi\big]^{2}

In order to evaluate the right-hand side of Eq. (
23
) we need to remember that
\bm{\hat{\mathcal{P}}}
does not commute with any function of the coordinate
x
, which in particular includes
\bm{A}(x)
. Specifically, we have

\displaystyle\Big[F(\bm{x}),\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{k}\Big]=i\frac{\partial{F(\bm{x})}}{\partial{x_{k}}}

(24)

(where we note that here and generally in the following part of this appendix, the subscripts do not denote covariant quantities, and in particular in Eq. (
24
) the subscripts
k
both refer to components of contravariant vectors). This means that we need to perform the square in Eq. (
23
) carefully, as
\big[\Pi_{i},\Pi_{j}\big]\neq 0
.

We can write out the square in Eq. (
23
) as

\displaystyle\big[\sigma\cdot\bm{\Pi}\big]^{2}=\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\Pi_{i}\Pi_{j}~.

(25)

Because the Pauli matrices satisfy the anticommutation relation
\{\sigma_{i},\sigma_{j}\}=2\delta_{ij}
, we can write

\displaystyle\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}=2\delta_{ij}-\sigma_{j}\sigma_{i}~,

(26)

which used in Eq. (
25
) along with
\Pi_{i}\Pi_{j}=\big[\Pi_{i},\Pi_{j}\big]+\Pi_{j}\Pi_{i}
yields

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\Pi_{i}\Pi_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\sum_{j}2\delta_{ij}\big[\Pi_{i},\Pi_{j}\big]+2\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\delta_{ij}\Pi_{j}\Pi_{i}

(27)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-~\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\sigma_{j}\sigma_{i}\big[\Pi_{i},\Pi_{j}\big]-\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\sigma_{j}\sigma_{i}\Pi_{j}\Pi_{i}~.

The first term on the right-hand side is identically zero, the second term is simply equal
2\bm{\Pi}^{2}
, and the fourth term, after a change of indeces according to
i\to j
,
j\to i
, is equal in magnitude to the left-hand side of the equation, so that altogether we get

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\Pi_{i}\Pi_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bm{\Pi}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\sigma_{j}\sigma_{i}\big[\Pi_{i},\Pi_{j}\big]~.

(28)

We can always rewrite the last term according to

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\sigma_{j}\sigma_{i}\big[\Pi_{i},\Pi_{j}\big]=-\frac{1}{4}\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\sigma_{j}\sigma_{i}\big[\Pi_{i},\Pi_{j}\big]-\frac{1}{4}\underset{i\to j,~j\to i}{\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\sigma_{j}\sigma_{i}\big[\Pi_{i},\Pi_{j}\big]}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=-\frac{1}{4}\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\sigma_{j}\sigma_{i}\big[\Pi_{i},\Pi_{j}\big]-\frac{1}{4}\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\big[\Pi_{j},\Pi_{i}\big]

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=-\frac{1}{4}\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\sigma_{j}\sigma_{i}\big[\Pi_{i},\Pi_{j}\big]+\frac{1}{4}\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\big[\Pi_{i},\Pi_{j}\big]

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\big[\sigma_{i},\sigma_{j}\big]\big[\Pi_{i},\Pi_{j}\big]~.

(29)

We know that
\big[\sigma_{i},\sigma_{j}\big]=2i\epsilon_{ijk}\sigma_{k}
, and we can explicitly calculate

\displaystyle\hskip-8.53581pt\big[\Pi_{i},\Pi_{j}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big[\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{i}-A_{i}(x),\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{j}-A_{j}\Big]=\big[\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{i},\hat{\mathcal{P}}\big]-\big[\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{i},A_{j}\big]-\big[A_{i},\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{j}\big]+^{2}\big[A_{i},A_{j}\big]

(30)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(i\frac{\partial{A_{j}}}{\partial{x_{i}}}-i\frac{\partial{A_{i}}}{\partial{x_{j}}}\right)~,

(31)

where Eq. (
24
) has been used. We thus arrive at

\displaystyle\left[\sigma\cdot\bm{\Pi}\right]^{2}=\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\Pi_{i}\Pi_{j}=\bm{\Pi}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\epsilon_{ijk}\sigma_{k}\left(\frac{\partial{A_{i}}}{\partial{x_{j}}}-\frac{\partial{A_{j}}}{\partial{x_{i}}}\right)~.

(32)

Finally, we perform the summation explicitly. Writing out all non-zero terms we arrive at

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\epsilon_{ijk}\sigma_{k}\left(\frac{\partial{A_{i}}}{\partial{x_{j}}}-\frac{\partial{A_{j}}}{\partial{x_{i}}}\right)

(33)

\displaystyle\hskip-8.53581pt=2\left[\epsilon_{12k}\sigma_{k}\left(\frac{\partial{A_{1}}}{\partial{x_{2}}}-\frac{\partial{A_{2}}}{\partial{x_{1}}}\right)+\epsilon_{23k}\sigma_{k}\left(\frac{\partial{A_{2}}}{\partial{x_{3}}}-\frac{\partial{A_{3}}}{\partial{x_{2}}}\right)+\epsilon_{13k}\left(\frac{\partial{A_{1}}}{\partial{x_{3}}}-\frac{\partial{A_{3}}}{\partial{x_{1}}}\right)\right]~.

(34)

At this point, we define a “magnetic” field
\bm{B}
such that

\displaystyle\bm{B}\equiv\bm{\nabla}\times\bm{A}=\bm{\hat{x}}_{1}\left(\frac{\partial{A_{3}}}{\partial{x_{2}}}-\frac{\partial{A_{2}}}{\partial{x_{3}}}\right)+\bm{\hat{x}}_{2}\left(\frac{\partial{A_{1}}}{\partial{x_{3}}}-\frac{\partial{A_{3}}}{\partial{x_{1}}}\right)+\bm{\hat{x}}_{3}\left(\frac{\partial{A_{2}}}{\partial{x_{1}}}-\frac{\partial{A_{1}}}{\partial{x_{2}}}\right)~.

(35)

Clearly then,

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\epsilon_{ijk}\sigma_{k}\left(\frac{\partial{A_{i}}}{\partial{x_{j}}}-\frac{\partial{A_{j}}}{\partial{x_{i}}}\right)=2\Big[-\epsilon_{12k}\sigma_{k}B_{3}-\epsilon_{23k}\sigma_{k}B_{1}+\epsilon_{13k}B_{2}\Big]~.

(36)

Inserting the only values of
k
that result in a non-zero Levi-Civita tensor we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\epsilon_{ijk}\sigma_{k}\left(\frac{\partial{A_{i}}}{\partial{x_{j}}}-\frac{\partial{A_{j}}}{\partial{x_{i}}}\right)=-2\bm{\sigma}\cdot\bm{B}~,

(37)

and in this way we ultimately arrive at

\displaystyle\left[\sigma\cdot\bm{\Pi}\right]^{2}=\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\Pi_{i}\Pi_{j}=\bm{\Pi}^{2}-\bm{\sigma}\cdot\bm{B}~.

(38)

Appendix 12.C
The Pauli equation

Using Eq. (
38
), Eq. (
22
) becomes

\displaystyle E\varphi=\left(\frac{\bm{\Pi}^{2}-\bm{\sigma}\cdot\bm{B}}{E-A_{0}+(m-S)}+\Big[A_{0}+(m-S)\Big]\right)\varphi~.

(39)

Let us for a moment neglect the scalar field by putting
S=0
, and let us consider the non-relativistic limit, in which the total energy
E=m+\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}
, where
\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}
is the kinetic energy of the particle such that
\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}\ll m
. Then

\displaystyle(E-m)\varphi=\left(\frac{\bm{\Pi}^{2}-\bm{\sigma}\cdot\bm{B}}{2m+\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}-A_{0}}+A_{0}\right)\varphi~.

(40)

Since both
A_{0}\ll m
and
\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}\ll m
, we can neglect these small terms in the denominator, leading to

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}\varphi=\left(\frac{\bm{\Pi}^{2}-\bm{\sigma}\cdot\bm{B}}{2m}+A_{0}\right)\varphi~.

(41)

By realizing that
\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}\varphi=i\partial_{t}\varphi
we arrive at the Pauli equation, which describes a spin-
\frac{1}{2}
particle in an external electromagnetic field,

\displaystyle i\partial_{t}\varphi=\left(\frac{\big(\bm{p}-\bm{A}\big)^{2}}{2m}-\frac{\bm{\sigma}\cdot\bm{B}}{2m}+A_{0}\right)\varphi~.

(42)

(Note that in our notation the coupling constant is absorbed into the field
A^{\mu}
.)

Appendix 12.D
Energy solutions in uniform nuclear matter

Similarly, using Eq. (
38
) in Eq. (
23
) results in

\displaystyle\big[E-A_{0}\big]^{2}\varphi=\bigg[\Big(\bm{\Pi}^{2}-e\bm{\sigma}\cdot\bm{B}\Big)+(m-S)^{2}\bigg]\varphi~.

(43)

In nuclear matter, the potential field
A^{\mu}(x)
is spatially uniform, which in particular means that
\bm{A}=0
, so that
\bm{B}=0
. With this, Eq. (
43
) becomes

\displaystyle\big[E-A_{0}\big]^{2}\varphi=\Big[\bm{\Pi}^{2}+(m-S)^{2}\Big]\varphi~,

(44)

and we immediately obtain

\displaystyle E=\pm\sqrt{\bm{\Pi}^{2}+(m-S)^{2}}+A_{0}~.

(45)

Note that because the matter in uniform, we in fact have
\bm{\Pi}=\bm{p}
.

Chapter 13
Form of the quasiparticle distribution function

The functional form of the quasiparticle distribution function
f_{\bm{p}}
can be obtained using fundamental thermodynamic relations. Any variation in the energy density is connected to a variation in entropy density
s
and particle density
n
through the fundamental equation of thermodynamics,

\displaystyle\delta\mathcal{E}=T~\delta s+\mu~\delta n~.

(1)

It follows from Eq. (
36
) that
\delta\mathcal{E}\mathcal{\ }\equiv\ \varepsilon_{\bm{p}}~\delta f_{\bm{p}}
. The dependence of
\delta s
on
f_{\bm{p}}
can be easily established by remembering that it is possible to calculate the entropy of a given state by combinatorial considerations only. In view of the one-to-one correspondence between the states of the Fermi liquid and the free Fermi gas, it is natural to assume that the entropy density must have the same form as in the case of the free Fermi gas,

\displaystyle s=-\sum_{\bm{p}}\Big[f_{\bm{p}}\ln f_{\bm{p}}+(1-f_{\bm{p}})\ln(1-f_{\bm{p}})\Big]~.

(2)

Consequently,

\displaystyle\delta s=-\sum_{\bm{p}}\left[\delta f_{\bm{p}}\ln\frac{f_{\bm{p}}}{1-f_{\bm{p}}}\right]~.

(3)

Similarly, because the number of quasiparticles in the interacting system directly corresponds to the number of particles in the corresponding state of the free Fermi gas, and the interaction between the particles is assumed to conserve the particle number, the total number of particles in a state of the interacting system must be the same as in the non-interacting system. In consequence, the quasiparticle density can be expressed using the quasiparticle distribution function in the familiar way,

\displaystyle n=\sum_{\bm{p}}f_{\bm{p}}~,

(4)

from which it follows that
\delta n=\sum_{\bm{p}}\delta f_{\bm{p}}
. With all this, Eq. (
1
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\sum_{\bm{p}}\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}~\delta f_{\bm{p}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-T\sum_{\bm{p}}\ln\frac{f_{\bm{p}}}{1-f_{\bm{p}}}~\delta f_{\bm{p}}+\mu\sum_{\bm{p}}\delta f_{\bm{p}}~,

(5)

which can be further rearranged as

\displaystyle\sum_{\bm{p}}\left[\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}+T\ln\frac{f_{\bm{p}}}{1-f_{\bm{p}}}-\mu\right]~\delta f_{\bm{p}}=0~.

(6)

The above equality will hold for any variation
\delta f_{\bm{p}}
if and only if the term in the square bracket vanishes for any
\bm{p}
, and this fact can be used to solve for the quasiparticle distribution function,

\displaystyle f_{\bm{p}}=\frac{1}{\exp\left(\frac{\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}-\mu}{T}\right)+1}~,

(7)

which turns out to be of the Fermi-Dirac form. This naturally follows one’s basic expectations, as the Landau Fermi-liquid theory is based on an assumption that the states of the Fermi liquid can be obtained as a result of a continuous deformation of the states of the ideal Fermi gas, and it is natural that they would be governed by a distribution function of the same form. We stress, however, that because the quasiparticle energy
\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}
itself depends on the quasiparticle distribution
f_{\bm{p}}
, the above equation is in fact a rather complicated self-consistent equation for
f_{\bm{p}}
, in contrast to the free Fermi gas case.

Chapter 14
Vanishing of the collision integral

Vanishing of the right-hand side of Eq. (
30
), multiplied by
X
and integrated over all momenta (the available phase space), where
X=\{1,\varepsilon_{\bm{p}},p^{j}\}
(corresponding to laws of the conservation of particle number, energy, and momentum, respectively), can be shown as follows. Let us first consider
X=1
, and let us take the discrete limit, in which the integration over all momenta becomes a sum over all particles,

\displaystyle g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\left(\frac{d{f_{\bm{p}}}}{d{t}}\right)_{\textrm{coll}}\hskip 14.22636pt\to\hskip 14.22636pt\sum_{i}\left(\frac{d{f_{i}}}{d{t}}\right)_{\textrm{coll}}~;

(1)

here the index
i
numbers the momenta states. In the discrete limit, the Fermi quasiparticle distribution function is just a list of occupation numbers
\{0,1\}
for states indexed by
i
, and so
\big(\frac{d{f_{i}}}{d{t}}\big)_{\textrm{coll}}
is the change in the occupation numbers due to collisions. Consider an event in which two quasiparticles in states
i=1
and
i=2
collide and as a result change their states to
i=3
and
i=4
. We have a situation in which before the collision, the states
i=\{1,2\}
were occupied and the states
i^{\prime}=\{3,4\}
were unoccupied, while the opposite is true after the collision. Therefore we can see that

\displaystyle\left(\frac{d{f_{i}}}{d{t}}\right)_{\textrm{coll}}\bigg|_{i=\{1,2\}}=-1\hskip 15.0pt{\textrm{and}}\hskip 15.0pt\left(\frac{d{f_{i}}}{d{t}}\right)_{\textrm{coll}}\bigg|_{i=\{3,4\}}=+1~.

(2)

Thus in this event (let us assume that only the states indexed by
i=\{1,2,3,4\}
have been changed, so that contributions from all other states are zero,
\big(\frac{d{f_{i}}}{d{t}}\big)_{\textrm{coll}}\big|_{i>4}=0
)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{4}\left(\frac{d{f_{i}}}{d{t}}\right)_{\textrm{coll}}=-1-1+1+1=0~.

(3)

In the continuous limit, this corresponds to
g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\big(\frac{d{f_{\bm{p}}}}{d{t}}\big)_{\textrm{coll}}=0
.

For the case
X=\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}
, it is again convenient to use the discrete limit. Consider an event in which two quasiparticles with energies
\varepsilon_{1}
and
\varepsilon_{2}
collide and as a result change their energies to
\varepsilon_{3}
and
\varepsilon_{4}
,

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2}\to\varepsilon_{3}+\varepsilon_{4}~.

(4)

Before the collision, the states
i=\{1,2\}
were occupied and the states
i^{\prime}=\{3,4\}
were unoccupied, while the opposite is true after the collision, as in Eq. (
2
). Thus in this event (let us again assume that only the states indexed by
i=\{1,2,3,4\}
have been changed,
\big(\frac{\partial{f_{i}}}{\partial{t}}\big)_{\textrm{coll}}\big|_{i>4}=0
)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{4}\varepsilon_{i}\left(\frac{\partial{f_{i}}}{\partial{t}}\right)_{\textrm{coll}}=-\varepsilon_{1}-\varepsilon_{2}+\varepsilon_{3}+\varepsilon_{4}~.

(5)

By conservation of energy, the right-hand side of Eq. (
5
) must be zero, and in the continuous limit this corresponds to
g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}\big(\frac{\partial{f_{\bm{p}}}}{\partial{t}}\big)_{\textrm{coll}}=0
.

The case for
X=p^{j}
can be shown analogously.

Chapter 15
VSDF model derivations

Appendix 15.A
The quasiparticle energy

To calculate the quasiparticle energy, we calculate a functional differential of the energy density,

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}\equiv\frac{\delta\mathcal{E}}{\delta f_{\bm{p}}}~.

(1)

Taking the energy density to be given by
\mathcal{E}_{v1s1}
, Eq. (
42
), and varying the first term, corresponding to the kinetic energy of the particles in the system, yields

\displaystyle\delta\mathcal{E}_{v1s1,\textrm{kin}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta\int d^{3}\tilde{p}~\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}^{*}~f_{\bm{p}}=\int d^{3}\tilde{p}~\delta\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}^{*}~f_{\bm{p}}+\int d^{3}\tilde{p}~\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}^{*}~\delta f_{\bm{p}}~,

(2)

where we take into account that the kinetic energy
\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}
, Eq. (
43
), is also a functional of the quasiparticle distribution function through its dependence on the baryon current and effective mass. Explicitly,

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt\delta\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}^{*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta\Bigg[\sqrt{\Big(\bm{p}-C_{1}\big(j_{\mu}j^{\mu}\big)^{\alpha_{1}-1}\bm{j}\Big)^{2}+{m^{*}}^{2}}\Bigg]=\frac{\delta\left[\Big(\bm{p}-C_{1}\big(j_{\mu}j^{\mu}\big)^{\alpha_{1}-1}\bm{j}\Big)^{2}+{m^{*}}^{2}\right]}{2\sqrt{\Big(\bm{p}-C_{1}\big(j_{\mu}j^{\mu}\big)^{\alpha_{1}-1}\bm{j}\Big)^{2}+{m^{*}}^{2}}}

(3)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\Big(\bm{p}-C_{1}\big(j_{\mu}j^{\mu}\big)^{\alpha_{1}-1}\bm{j}\Big)\cdot\left[\delta\Big(\bm{p}-C_{1}\big(j_{\mu}j^{\mu}\big)^{\alpha_{1}-1}\bm{j}\Big)\right]+{m^{*}}~\delta m^{*}}{\sqrt{\Big(\bm{p}-C_{1}\big(j_{\mu}j^{\mu}\big)^{\alpha_{1}-1}\bm{j}\Big)^{2}+{m^{*}}^{2}}}~.

We have

\displaystyle\delta\Big(\bm{p}-C_{1}\big(j_{\mu}j^{\mu}\big)^{\alpha_{1}-1}\bm{j}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-C_{1}\delta\Big(\big(j_{\mu}j^{\mu}\big)^{\alpha_{1}-1}\bm{j}\Big)

(4)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-C_{1}\left[2(\alpha_{1}-1)\big(j_{\mu}j^{\mu}\big)^{\alpha_{1}-2}j_{\mu}~\delta j^{\mu}~\bm{j}+\big(j_{\mu}j^{\mu}\big)^{\alpha_{1}-1}~\delta\bm{j}\right]

and

\displaystyle\delta m^{*}=-G_{1}\delta\Big[n_{s}^{\gamma_{1}-1}\Big]=-G_{1}(\gamma_{1}-1)n_{s}^{\gamma_{1}-2}~\delta n_{s}~,

(5)

where in the second equality we used Eq. (
46
), so that altogether

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt\int d^{3}\tilde{p}~\delta\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}^{*}~f_{\bm{p}}

(6)

\displaystyle\hskip-85.35826pt=~-C_{1}\left[2(\alpha_{1}-1)\big(j_{\mu}j^{\mu}\big)^{\alpha_{1}-2}j_{\mu}~\delta j^{\mu}~\bm{j}+\big(j_{\mu}j^{\mu}\big)^{\alpha_{1}-1}~\delta\bm{j}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt\cdot~\int d^{3}\tilde{p}~\frac{\bm{p}-C_{1}\big(j_{\mu}j^{\mu}\big)^{\alpha_{1}-1}\bm{j}}{\sqrt{\Big(\bm{p}-C_{1}\big(j_{\mu}j^{\mu}\big)^{\alpha_{1}-1}\bm{j}\Big)^{2}+{m^{*}}^{2}}}~f_{\bm{p}}

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt-~G_{1}(\gamma_{1}-1)n_{s}^{\gamma_{1}-2}~\delta n_{s}\int d^{3}\tilde{p}~\frac{{m^{*}}}{\sqrt{\Big(\bm{p}-C_{1}\big(j_{\mu}j^{\mu}\big)^{\alpha_{1}-1}\bm{j}\Big)^{2}+{m^{*}}^{2}}}~f_{\bm{p}}~.

By Eqs. (
44
) and (
47
) this becomes

\displaystyle\int d^{3}\tilde{p}~\delta\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}^{*}~f_{\bm{p}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-C_{1}\left[2(\alpha_{1}-1)\big(j_{\mu}j^{\mu}\big)^{\alpha_{1}-2}j_{\mu}~\delta j^{\mu}~\bm{j}+\big(j_{\mu}j^{\mu}\big)^{\alpha_{1}-1}~\delta\bm{j}\right]\cdot\bm{j}

(7)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-~G_{1}(\gamma_{1}-1)n_{s}^{\gamma_{1}-1}~\delta n_{s}~,

and consequently

\displaystyle\delta\mathcal{E}_{v1s1,\textrm{kin}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d^{3}\tilde{p}~\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}^{*}~\delta f_{\bm{p}}-2C_{1}(\alpha_{1}-1)\big(j_{\mu}j^{\mu}\big)^{\alpha_{1}-2}\bm{j}\cdot\bm{j}~j_{\mu}~\delta j^{\mu}

(8)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt-~C_{1}\big(j_{\mu}j^{\mu}\big)^{\alpha_{1}-1}~\bm{j}\cdot\delta\bm{j}-G_{1}(\gamma_{1}-1)n_{s}^{\gamma_{1}-1}~\delta n_{s}~.

Varying the interaction part of the energy density
\mathcal{E}_{\textrm{v1s1}}
, Eq. (
42
), term by term yields

\displaystyle\hskip-22.76219pt\delta\Big[C_{1}\big(j_{\mu}j^{\mu}\big)^{\alpha_{1}-1}\big(j^{0}\big)^{2}\Big]=2C_{1}\left[(\alpha_{1}-1)\big(j_{\mu}j^{\mu}\big)^{\alpha_{1}-2}\big(j^{0}\big)^{2}j_{\mu}~\delta j^{\mu}+\big(j_{\mu}j^{\mu}\big)^{\alpha_{1}-1}j_{0}~\delta j^{0}\right]~,

(9)

\displaystyle\hskip-22.76219pt\delta\left[-C_{1}\left(\frac{2\alpha_{1}-1}{2\alpha_{1}}\right)\big(j_{\mu}j^{\mu}\big)^{\alpha_{1}}\right]=-C_{1}(2\alpha_{1}-1)\big(j_{\mu}j^{\mu}\big)^{\alpha_{1}-1}j_{\mu}~\delta j^{\mu}~,

(10)

\displaystyle\hskip-22.76219pt\delta\left[G_{1}\frac{\gamma_{1}-1}{\gamma_{1}}n_{s}^{\gamma_{1}}\right]=G_{1}(\gamma_{1}-1)n_{s}^{\gamma_{1}-1}~\delta n_{s}~,

(11)

so that the variation of the entire interaction part is

\displaystyle\delta\mathcal{E}_{v1s1,\textrm{int}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{1}(2\alpha_{1}-2)\big(j_{\mu}j^{\mu}\big)^{\alpha_{1}-2}\big(j^{0}\big)^{2}j_{\mu}~\delta j^{\mu}-C_{1}(2\alpha_{1}-1)\big(j_{\mu}j^{\mu}\big)^{\alpha_{1}-1}j_{\mu}~\delta j^{\mu}

(12)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+~2C_{1}\big(j_{\mu}j^{\mu}\big)^{\alpha_{1}-1}j_{0}~\delta j^{0}+G_{1}(\gamma_{1}-1)n_{s}^{\gamma_{1}-1}~\delta n_{s}~.

Putting Eqs. (
8
) and (
12
) together, we obtain the variation of the energy density as given by Eq. (
42
),

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt\delta\mathcal{E}_{\textrm{v1s1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d^{3}\tilde{p}~\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}^{*}~\delta f_{\bm{p}}-C_{1}(2\alpha_{1}-2)\big(j_{\mu}j^{\mu}\big)^{\alpha_{1}-2}\bm{j}\cdot\bm{j}~j_{\mu}~\delta j^{\mu}

(13)

\displaystyle\hskip 8.53581pt-~C_{1}\big(j_{\mu}j^{\mu}\big)^{\alpha_{1}-1}~\bm{j}\cdot\delta\bm{j}-G_{1}(\gamma_{1}-1)n_{s}^{\gamma_{1}-1}~\delta n_{s}

\displaystyle\hskip 22.76219pt+~C_{1}(2\alpha_{1}-2)\big(j_{\mu}j^{\mu}\big)^{\alpha_{1}-2}\big(j^{0}\big)^{2}j_{\mu}~\delta j^{\mu}-C_{1}(2\alpha_{1}-1)\big(j_{\mu}j^{\mu}\big)^{\alpha_{1}-1}j_{\mu}~\delta j^{\mu}

\displaystyle\hskip 36.98857pt+~2C_{1}\big(j_{\mu}j^{\mu}\big)^{\alpha_{1}-1}j_{0}~\delta j^{0}+G_{1}(\gamma_{1}-1)n_{s}^{\gamma_{1}-1}~\delta n_{s}~.

The fourth and eighth term, induced by variation of the scalar density and thus proportional to
G_{1}
, cancel out. Further, the second, fifth, and sixth term can be combined using the fact that
\big(j^{0}\big)^{2}-\bm{j}\cdot\bm{j}=j_{\mu}j^{\mu}
, so we can write

\displaystyle\delta\mathcal{E}_{\textrm{v1s1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d^{3}\tilde{p}~\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}^{*}~\delta f_{\bm{p}}-C_{1}\big(j_{\mu}j^{\mu}\big)^{\alpha_{1}-1}~j_{\mu}~\delta j^{\mu}

(14)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt-~C_{1}\big(j_{\mu}j^{\mu}\big)^{\alpha_{1}-1}~\bm{j}\cdot\delta\bm{j}+2C_{1}\big(j_{\mu}j^{\mu}\big)^{\alpha_{1}-1}j_{0}~\delta j^{0}~.

Noting that
j_{\mu}\delta j^{\mu}=j_{0}\delta j^{0}-\bm{j}\cdot\delta\bm{j}
, we further reduces the above equation to

\displaystyle\delta\mathcal{E}_{\textrm{v1s1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d^{3}\tilde{p}~\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}^{*}~\delta f_{\bm{p}}+C_{1}\big(j_{\mu}j^{\mu}\big)^{\alpha_{1}-1}j_{0}~\delta j^{0}~.

(15)

Finally, using the definition of baryon density
j^{0}
, Eq. (
45
), we arrive at

\displaystyle\delta\mathcal{E}_{\textrm{v1s1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d^{3}\tilde{p}~\bigg[\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}^{*}~+C_{1}\big(j_{\mu}j^{\mu}\big)^{\alpha_{1}-1}j_{0}\bigg]~\delta f_{\bm{p}}~,

(16)

from which we immediately obtain the quasiparticle energy,

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}=\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}^{*}~+C_{1}\big(j_{\mu}j^{\mu}\big)^{\alpha_{1}-1}j_{0}~.

(17)

Appendix 15.B
Construction of the energy-momentum tensor

The energy-momentum tensor,
T^{\mu\nu}
, can be constructed by taking moments of the Boltzmann equation, Eq. (
54
), and identifying the components of
T^{\mu\nu}
which must satisfy the energy and momentum conservation,
\partial_{\nu}T^{\mu\nu}=0
.

First, we multiply both sides of Eq. (
54
) by the quasiparticle energy,
\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}
, and integrate over all momenta,

\displaystyle g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}~\frac{\partial{f_{\bm{p}}}}{\partial{t}}-g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}\frac{\partial{\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}}}{\partial{p_{i}}}~\frac{\partial{f_{\bm{p}}}}{\partial{x^{i}}}+g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}\frac{\partial{\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}}}{\partial{x_{i}}}~\frac{\partial{f_{\bm{p}}}}{\partial{p^{i}}}=0~,

(18)

where get zero on the right-hand side due to the fact that quasiparticle energy is conserved in collisions (see Appendix
14
for more details). By using the product rule for derivatives,

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636ptg\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}\frac{\partial{\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}}}{\partial{p_{i}}}~\frac{\partial{f_{\bm{p}}}}{\partial{x^{i}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{x^{i}}}~g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}\frac{\partial{\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}}}{\partial{p_{i}}}~f_{\bm{p}}

(19)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt-g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\frac{\partial{\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}}}{\partial{x^{i}}}\frac{\partial{\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}}}{\partial{p_{i}}}~f_{\bm{p}}-g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}\frac{\partial^{2}\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}}{\partial x^{i}\partial p_{i}}~f_{\bm{p}}

and

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636ptg\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}\frac{\partial{\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}}}{\partial{x_{i}}}~\frac{\partial{f_{\bm{p}}}}{\partial{p^{i}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p^{i}}}~g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}\frac{\partial{\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}}}{\partial{x_{i}}}~f_{\bm{p}}

(20)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt-~g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\frac{\partial{\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}}}{\partial{p^{i}}}\frac{\partial{\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}}}{\partial{x_{i}}}~f_{\bm{p}}-g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}\frac{\partial^{2}\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}}{\partial p^{i}\partial x_{i}}~f_{\bm{p}}~,

we can rewrite Eq. (
18
) as

\displaystyle g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}~\frac{\partial{f_{\bm{p}}}}{\partial{t}}-\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{x^{i}}}~g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}\frac{\partial{\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}}}{\partial{p_{i}}}~f_{\bm{p}}+\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p^{i}}}~g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}\frac{\partial{\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}}}{\partial{x_{i}}}~f_{\bm{p}}=0~.

(21)

The last term is a definite integral of the integrand, and it is identically zero because the distribution function must vanish for
|\bm{p}|=+\infty
, so that we have

\displaystyle g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}~\frac{\partial{f_{\bm{p}}}}{\partial{t}}-\partial_{i}~g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}\frac{\partial{\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}}}{\partial{p_{i}}}~f_{\bm{p}}=0~.

(22)

Because from the definition
\delta\mathcal{E}\equiv g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}~\delta f_{\bm{p}}
(see Section
3
), where
\mathcal{E}
is the generic energy density of the system, we can further rewrite the first term, thus arriving at

\displaystyle\partial_{0}\mathcal{E}-\partial_{i}~g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}\frac{\partial{\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}}}{\partial{p_{i}}}~f_{\bm{p}}=0~.

(23)

Finally, using
-\partial{\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}}/\partial{p_{i}}\equiv v^{i}
, where
v^{i}
is the velocity of a quasiparticle, the second term in the above equation can be identified as the quasiparticle energy flux, and thus it becomes apparent that Eq. (
23
) must be an energy density conservation equation. By comparing with
\partial_{\nu}T^{0\nu}=0
we see that we must have

\displaystyle T^{00}=\mathcal{E}

(24)

and

\displaystyle T^{0i}=-g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}\frac{\partial{\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}}}{\partial{p_{i}}}~f_{\bm{p}}~.

(25)

To obtain momentum conservation, we multiply both sides of Eq. (
54
) by the
k
-th component of the quasiparticle’s momentum,
p^{k}
, and again integrate over all momenta,

\displaystyle g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~p^{k}~\frac{\partial{f_{\bm{p}}}}{\partial{t}}-g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~p^{k}\frac{\partial{\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}}}{\partial{p_{i}}}~\frac{\partial{f_{\bm{p}}}}{\partial{x^{i}}}+g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~p^{k}\frac{\partial{\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}}}{\partial{x_{i}}}~\frac{\partial{f_{\bm{p}}}}{\partial{p^{i}}}=0~,

(26)

where the right-hand side vanishes because momentum, like energy, is also conserved in collisions (see Appendix
14
for more details). Using the product rule for derivatives,

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636ptg\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~p^{k}\frac{\partial{\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}}}{\partial{p_{i}}}~\frac{\partial{f_{\bm{p}}}}{\partial{x^{i}}}=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{x^{i}}}~g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~p^{k}\frac{\partial{\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}}}{\partial{p_{i}}}~f_{\bm{p}}-g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~p^{k}\frac{\partial^{2}{\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}}}{\partial{x^{i}}\partial{p_{i}}}~f_{\bm{p}}

(27)

and

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636ptg\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~p^{k}\frac{\partial{\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}}}{\partial{x_{i}}}~\frac{\partial{f_{\bm{p}}}}{\partial{p^{i}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p^{i}}}~g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~p^{k}\frac{\partial{\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}}}{\partial{x_{i}}}~f_{\bm{p}}

(28)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt-~g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\frac{\partial{p^{k}}}{\partial{p^{i}}}\frac{\partial{\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}}}{\partial{x_{i}}}~f_{\bm{p}}-g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~p^{k}\frac{\partial^{2}{\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}}}{\partial{p^{i}}\partial{x_{i}}}~f_{\bm{p}}~,

leads to

\displaystyle g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~p^{k}~\frac{\partial{f_{\bm{p}}}}{\partial{t}}-\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{x^{i}}}~g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~p^{k}\frac{\partial{\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}}}{\partial{p_{i}}}~f_{\bm{p}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+~\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p^{i}}}~g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~p^{k}\frac{\partial{\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}}}{\partial{x_{i}}}~f_{\bm{p}}-g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~g^{k}_{~i}\frac{\partial{\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}}}{\partial{x_{i}}}~f_{\bm{p}}=0~,

(29)

where we have used
\frac{\partial{p^{k}}}{\partial{p^{i}}}=\delta^{k}_{i}=g^{kj}g_{ji}=g^{k}_{~i}
, with
g^{k}_{~i}
being a component of the metric tensor. The third term vanishes due to the fact that
f_{\bm{p}}\big(|\bm{p}|=+\infty\big)=0
, while the fourth term can be further rewritten as

\displaystyle g^{k}_{~i}~g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\frac{\partial{\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}}}{\partial{x_{i}}}~f_{\bm{p}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g^{k}_{~i}~\partial^{i}g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}~f_{\bm{p}}-g^{k}_{~i}~\partial^{i}\mathcal{E}~,

(30)

where we again used the derivative product rule and
\delta\mathcal{E}\equiv g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}~\delta f_{\bm{p}}
. Altogether we then have

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{t}}~g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~p^{k}~f_{\bm{p}}-\partial_{i}~g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~p^{k}\frac{\partial{\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}}}{\partial{p_{i}}}~f_{\bm{p}}+\partial_{i}~g^{ik}\left[\mathcal{E}-g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}~f_{\bm{p}}\right]=0~,

(31)

and, using
\partial_{\nu}T^{i\nu}=0
, we can identify

\displaystyle T^{i0}=g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~p^{i}~f_{\bm{p}}

(32)

and

\displaystyle T^{ij}=-g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~p^{i}\frac{\partial{\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}}}{\partial{p_{j}}}~f_{\bm{p}}+g^{ij}\left[\mathcal{E}-g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}^{*}~f_{\bm{p}}\right]~.

(33)

In this way, we constructed all components of the energy-momentum tensor
T^{\mu\nu}
as introduced in Eqs. (
55
-
58
).

Appendix 15.C
Relativistic covariance of the equations of motion

With the definition of the kinetic momentum
\Pi^{\mu}
, Eq. (
76
), the Hamilton’s equations, Eqs. (
73
) and (
74
), can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\frac{dx^{i}}{dt}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\Pi^{i}}{\Pi_{0}}~,

(34)

\displaystyle\frac{dp^{i}}{dt}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{\sum_{j}\Pi_{j}}{\Pi_{0}}\left(\frac{\partial{(A_{k})^{j}}}{\partial{x_{i}}}\right)+\frac{m^{*}}{\Pi_{0}}\frac{\partial{m^{*}}}{\partial{x_{i}}}+\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{\partial{(A_{k})^{0}}}{\partial{x_{i}}}~.

(35)

Using the fact that
H_{(1)}=\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}=p_{0}
, we can see that for the temporal component of
x^{\mu}
we have trivially

\displaystyle\frac{dx^{0}}{dt}=\frac{\partial{H_{(1)}}}{\partial{p_{0}}}=1=\frac{\Pi_{0}}{\Pi_{0}}~,

(36)

which allows us to write Eqs. (
34
) and (
36
) together as

\displaystyle\frac{dx^{\mu}}{dt}=\frac{\Pi^{\mu}}{\Pi_{0}}~.

(37)

For the temporal part of
p^{\mu}
we can likewise write

\displaystyle\frac{dp^{0}}{dt}=\frac{dp^{0}}{dx_{0}}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{\sum_{j}\Pi_{j}}{\Pi_{0}}\frac{\partial{(A_{k})^{j}}}{\partial{x_{0}}}+\frac{m^{*}}{\Pi_{0}}\frac{\partial{m^{*}}}{\partial{x_{0}}}+\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{\partial{(A_{k})^{0}}}{\partial{x_{0}}}~,

(38)

where on the right-hand side we have simply carried out the differentiation with respect to
x_{0}
, and it follows that Eqs. (
35
) and (
38
) can be jointly written as

\displaystyle\frac{dp^{\mu}}{dt}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\left[\frac{\sum_{j}\Pi_{j}}{\Pi_{0}}\frac{\partial{(A_{k})^{j}}}{\partial{x_{\mu}}}+\frac{\partial{(A_{k})^{0}}}{\partial{x_{\mu}}}\right]+\frac{m^{*}}{\Pi_{0}}\frac{\partial{m^{*}}}{\partial{x_{0}}}

(39)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\left[\frac{\sum_{j}\Pi_{j}}{\Pi_{0}}\frac{\partial{(A_{k})^{j}}}{\partial{x_{\mu}}}+\frac{\Pi_{0}}{\Pi_{0}}\frac{\partial{(A_{k})^{0}}}{\partial{x_{\mu}}}\right]+\frac{m^{*}}{\Pi_{0}}\frac{\partial{m^{*}}}{\partial{x_{0}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\left[\sum_{\nu}\frac{\Pi_{\nu}}{\Pi_{0}}\frac{\partial{(A_{k})^{\nu}}}{\partial{x_{\mu}}}\right]+\frac{m^{*}}{\Pi_{0}}\frac{\partial{m^{*}}}{\partial{x_{0}}}~.

Let us note that from the definition of the kinetic momentum
\Pi
we have

\displaystyle\frac{d\Pi^{\mu}}{dt}=\frac{dp^{\mu}}{dt}-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{d(A_{k})^{\mu}}{dt}~.

(40)

Using Eq. (
39
), the above equation becomes

\displaystyle\frac{d\Pi^{\mu}}{dt}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\left[\sum_{\nu}\frac{\Pi_{\nu}}{\Pi_{0}}\frac{\partial{(A_{k})^{\nu}}}{\partial{x_{\mu}}}\right]+\frac{m^{*}}{\Pi_{0}}\frac{\partial{m^{*}}}{\partial{x_{0}}}-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{d(A_{k})^{\mu}}{dt}~.

(41)

We can always write

\displaystyle\frac{d(A_{k})^{\mu}}{dt}=\sum_{\nu}\frac{\partial{(A_{k})^{\mu}}}{\partial{x^{\nu}}}\frac{dx^{\nu}}{dt}=\sum_{\nu}\frac{\partial{(A_{k})^{\mu}}}{\partial{x^{\nu}}}\frac{\Pi^{\nu}}{\Pi_{0}}~,

(42)

so that Eq. (
41
) can be transformed into

\displaystyle\frac{d\Pi^{\mu}}{dt}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{\nu}\left[\frac{\Pi_{\nu}}{\Pi_{0}}\frac{\partial{(A_{k})^{\nu}}}{\partial{x_{\mu}}}-\frac{\Pi^{\nu}}{\Pi_{0}}\frac{\partial{(A_{k})^{\mu}}}{\partial{x^{\nu}}}\right]+\frac{m^{*}}{\Pi_{0}}\frac{\partial{m^{*}}}{\partial{x_{0}}}

(43)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{\nu}\frac{\Pi_{\nu}}{\Pi_{0}}\Big[\partial^{\mu}(A_{k})^{\nu}-\partial^{\nu}(A_{k})^{\mu}\Big]+\frac{m^{*}}{\Pi_{0}}\frac{\partial{m^{*}}}{\partial{x_{0}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\nu}\frac{\Pi_{\nu}}{\Pi_{0}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}(F_{k})^{\mu\nu}+\frac{m^{*}}{\Pi_{0}}\frac{\partial{m^{*}}}{\partial{x_{0}}}~,

(44)

where
(F_{k})^{\mu\nu}
is defined similarly as the field strength in relativistic electrodynamics.

Both Eq. (
37
) and Eq. (
44
) are written in a relativistically covariant form.

Appendix 15.D
Lorentz force

The relativistically covariant equation of motion for the quasiparticle momentum, Eq. (
80
), naturally includes the Lorentz force. Explicitly, let us see that for
\mu=i
we get

\displaystyle\frac{d\Pi^{i}}{dt}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{\nu}\frac{\Pi_{\nu}}{\Pi_{0}}\Big[\partial^{i}(A_{k})^{\nu}-\partial^{\nu}(A_{k})^{i}\Big]+\frac{m^{*}}{\Pi_{0}}\frac{\partial{m^{*}}}{\partial{x_{0}}}

(45)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\left\{\Big[\partial^{i}(A_{k})^{0}-\partial^{0}(A_{k})^{i}\Big]+\sum_{j}\frac{\Pi_{j}}{\Pi_{0}}\Big[\partial^{i}(A_{k})^{j}-\partial^{j}(A_{k})^{i}\Big]\right\}+\frac{m^{*}}{\Pi_{0}}\frac{\partial{m^{*}}}{\partial{x_{0}}}~.

(46)

By summing over
i
we obtain (here one needs to remember that
(\bm{\nabla})_{i}=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{x^{i}}}=-\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{x_{i}}}
, see Appendix
7
)

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt\frac{d\bm{\Pi}}{dt}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\left\{\Big[-\bm{\nabla}A_{k}^{0}-\partial^{0}\bm{A}_{k}\Big]+\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\frac{\Pi_{j}}{\Pi_{0}}\Big[\partial^{i}(A_{k})^{j}-\partial^{j}(A_{k})^{i}\Big]\right\}+\frac{m^{*}}{\Pi_{0}}\frac{\partial{m^{*}}}{\partial{x_{0}}}~.

(47)

To interpret the second term in the above equation, let us see that

\displaystyle\bm{\Pi}\times\big(\bm{\nabla}\times\bm{A}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\begin{array}[]{ccc}\bm{\hat{x}}&\bm{\hat{y}}&\bm{\hat{z}}\\
\Pi_{x}&\Pi_{y}&\Pi_{z}\\
\big(\bm{\nabla}\times\bm{A}\big)_{x}&\big(\bm{\nabla}\times\bm{A}\big)_{y}&\big(\bm{\nabla}\times\bm{A}\big)_{z}\end{array}\right|

(52)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bm{\hat{x}}\bigg[\Pi_{y}\big(\bm{\nabla}\times\bm{A}\big)_{z}-\Pi_{z}\big(\bm{\nabla}\times\bm{A}\big)_{y}\bigg]

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+~\bm{\hat{y}}\bigg[\Pi_{z}\big(\bm{\nabla}\times\bm{A}\big)_{x}-\Pi_{x}\big(\bm{\nabla}\times\bm{A}\big)_{z}\bigg]

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+~\bm{\hat{z}}\bigg[\Pi_{x}\big(\bm{\nabla}\times\bm{A}\big)_{y}-\Pi_{y}\big(\bm{\nabla}\times\bm{A}\big)_{x}\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{a}\left[\sum_{b}\sum_{c}\epsilon_{abc}~\bm{\hat{x}}^{a}\Pi^{b}\big(\bm{\nabla}\times\bm{A}\big)^{c}\right]~,

where
\epsilon_{abc}
is the Levi-Civita symbol and
\bm{\hat{x}}^{a}
are the unit vectors. In the same way, we can further rewrite

\displaystyle\big(\bm{\nabla}\times\bm{A}\big)^{c}=\sum_{d}\sum_{f}\epsilon_{cdf}~\partial_{d}A^{f}~,

(53)

so that altogether

\displaystyle\bm{\Pi}\times\big(\bm{\nabla}\times\bm{A}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{a}\left[\sum_{b}\sum_{c}\sum_{d}\sum_{f}\epsilon_{abc}~\epsilon_{cdf}~\bm{\hat{x}}^{a}\Pi^{b}~\partial_{d}A^{f}\right]~.

(54)

It can be shown that

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\epsilon_{ijk}\epsilon^{imn}=\delta_{j}^{~m}\delta_{k}^{~n}-\delta_{j}^{~n}\delta_{k}^{~m}~,

(55)

which, bearing in mind that
\epsilon_{ijk}=\epsilon_{jki}
, can be used to obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{c}\epsilon_{abc}\epsilon_{cdf}=\delta_{a}^{~d}\delta_{b}^{~f}-\delta_{a}^{~f}\delta_{b}^{~d}~.

(56)

Then Eq. (
54
) becomes
