\displaystyle\hskip-19.91692pt\bm{\Pi}\times\big(\bm{\nabla}\times\bm{A}\big)=\sum_{a}\left[\sum_{b}\sum_{d}\sum_{f}\Big(\delta_{a}^{~d}\delta_{b}^{~f}\Big)~\bm{\hat{x}}^{a}\Pi^{b}~\partial_{d}A^{f}-\sum_{b}\sum_{d}\sum_{f}\Big(\delta_{a}^{~f}\delta_{b}^{~d}\Big)~\bm{\hat{x}}^{a}\Pi^{b}~\partial_{d}A^{f}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt=\sum_{a}\left[\sum_{b}\bm{\hat{x}}^{a}\Pi^{b}~\partial_{a}A^{b}-\sum_{b}\bm{\hat{x}}^{a}\Pi^{b}~\partial_{b}A^{a}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt=-\sum_{a}\sum_{b}\Pi^{b}~\left[\partial^{a}A^{b}~\bm{\hat{x}}^{a}-\partial^{b}A^{a}~\bm{\hat{x}}^{a}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt=\sum_{a}\sum_{b}\Pi_{b}~\left[\partial^{a}A^{b}~\bm{\hat{x}}^{a}-\partial^{b}A^{a}~\bm{\hat{x}}^{a}\right]~,

(57)

where in the two last lines we have used the fact that
\partial_{i}=-\partial^{i}
and
\Pi_{i}=-\Pi_{i}
. Comparing Eq. (
47
) against the above formula reveals that

\displaystyle\frac{d\bm{\Pi}}{dt}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\left\{\Big[-\bm{\nabla}A_{k}^{0}-\partial^{0}\bm{A}_{k}\Big]+\frac{\bm{\Pi}}{\Pi_{0}}\times\big(\bm{\nabla}\times\bm{A}_{k}\big)\right\}+\frac{m^{*}}{\Pi_{0}}\frac{\partial{m^{*}}}{\partial{x_{0}}}~.

(58)

Noting that by the equation of motion for the quasiparticle position, Eq. (
79
), we have
\frac{\bm{\Pi}}{\Pi_{0}}=\frac{d\bm{x}}{dt}
, we see that the middle term in the above equation is evidently the Lorentz force,
\bm{v}\times\bm{B}
, where
\bm{B}\equiv\bm{\nabla}\times\bm{A}_{k}
.

Appendix 15.E
Minimization of the auxiliary fields

Thermodynamic consistency demands that the thermodynamic potential is minimized by the auxiliary fields,
n_{s}
and
n
. In the grand canonical ensemble, this is equivalent to minimizing the pressure,

\displaystyle\frac{d{P}}{d{n_{s}}}\bigg|_{T,\mu}=0\hskip 15.0pt{\textrm{and}}\hskip 15.0pt\frac{d{P}}{d{n}}\bigg|_{T,\mu}=0~.

(59)

With the density dependence of the kinetic term explicit, the pressure, Eq. (
83
), is given by

\displaystyle P_{(K,M)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~T~\ln\bigg[1+e^{-\beta\big(\sqrt{p^{2}+{m^{*}}^{2}}+\sum_{k}C_{k}n^{b_{k}-1}-\mu\big)}\bigg]

(60)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+~\sum_{k=1}^{K}C_{k}\left(\frac{b_{k}-1}{b_{k}}\right)n^{b_{k}}-\sum_{m=1}^{M}G_{m}\left(\frac{d_{m}-1}{d_{m}}\right)n_{s}^{d_{m}}~.

Then we can calculate

\displaystyle\frac{d{P}}{d{n_{s}}}\bigg|_{T,\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~~\left(\frac{dm^{*}}{dn_{s}}\frac{m^{*}}{\epsilon^{*}}+\frac{dn}{dn_{s}}\sum_{k}C_{k}(b_{k}-1)n^{b_{k}-2}\right)~f_{\bm{p}}

(62)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+~\frac{dn}{dn_{s}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}C_{k}(b_{k}-1)n^{b_{k}-1}-\sum_{m=1}^{M}G_{m}(d_{m}-1)n_{s}^{d_{m}-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-n_{s}\bigg(\frac{dm^{*}}{dn_{s}}+\sum_{m=1}^{M}G_{m}(d_{m}-1)n_{s}^{d_{m}-2}\bigg)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+~\frac{dn}{dn_{s}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}C_{k}(b_{k}-1)n^{b_{k}-2}\bigg(n-g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~f_{\bm{p}}\bigg)=0~;

here, the the second term vanishes from the definition of the baryon current, Eq. (
78
), while demanding that the first term disappears as well yields, after integration, the gap equation,

\displaystyle m^{*}=m_{N}-\sum_{m=1}^{M}G_{m}n_{s}^{d_{m}-1}~,

(63)

which is identical to the definition of the effective mass, Eq. (
72
).

Similarly, we calculate

\displaystyle\frac{d{P}}{d{n}}\bigg|_{T,\mu_{B}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{dm^{*}}{dn}\left[n_{s}+\frac{d{n_{s}}}{d{m^{*}}}\sum_{m=1}^{M}G_{m}(d_{m}-1)n_{s}^{d_{m}-1}\right]

(64)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+~\sum_{k=1}^{K}C_{k}(b_{k}-1)n^{b_{k}-2}\bigg(n-g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~f_{\bm{p}}\bigg)=0~,

where the vanishing of the first term can be easily established by taking the derivative of the gap equation, Eq. (
72
) (or equivalently Eq. (
63
)), with respect to the effective mass
m^{*}
, while the second term again disappears by Eq. (
78
).

Thus we show that the pressure is minimized by the auxiliary fields,
n_{s}
and
n
, provided that these auxiliary fields are defined self-consistently by Eqs. (
72
) and (
78
), respectively.

Chapter 16
Form of the energy density functional

We want to write down a generic energy density in which the interaction couples to the mean-field vector and scalar currents (here we will consider only one vector and one scalar term, which corresponds to
K=1
and
M=1
in the notation used in Section
2
and onward, however, the derivation can be easily generalized). We know that in such cases the energy of the quasiparticle takes the form

\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}=\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}+A_{0}~,

(1)

where
\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}
is kinetic energy which also depends on the interaction,

\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}=\sqrt{\big(\bm{p}-\bm{A}\big)^{2}+{m^{*}}^{2}}~,

(2)

and the interaction term is in general given by

\displaystyle A^{\mu}=\alpha(n)j^{\mu}~,

(3)

where
\alpha(n)
an arbitrary function of the rest frame baryon density
n
and
j^{\mu}
is the baryon 4-current,

j^{\mu}=\int d^{3}\tilde{p}~\frac{p^{\mu}-A^{\mu}}{\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}}~f_{\bm{p}}~,

(4)

while the effective mass is given by

\displaystyle m^{*}=m_{0}-B,

(5)

where
B=B(n_{s})
is some function of the scalar density
n_{s}
. In general, the energy density will then look like

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}~f_{\bm{p}}-g^{00}\Gamma A_{\lambda}j^{\lambda}+\Lambda Bn_{s}

(6)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}~f_{\bm{p}}+A_{0}j^{0}-g^{00}\Gamma A_{\lambda}j^{\lambda}+\Lambda Bn_{s}~,

where
\Gamma
and
\Lambda
are constants in front of the counterterms
A_{\lambda}j^{\lambda}
and
Bn_{s}
, respectively. The counterterms are necessary to avoid overcounting potential energy contributions from the kinetic term, which sums quasiparticle energies at all possible momenta; intuitively, the counterterms can be understood to “adjust” the energy density to the correct value (for an elementary example, see the discussion following Eq. (
36
) in Chapter
1
).

The quasiparticle energy is from the definition given by

\varepsilon_{\bm{p}}\equiv\frac{\delta\mathcal{E}}{\delta f_{\bm{p}}}~.

(7)

Taking the functional differential of Eq. (
6
),

\displaystyle\delta\mathcal{E}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\frac{\bm{p}-\bm{A}}{\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}}\big(-\delta\bm{A}\big)~f_{\bm{p}}+g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\frac{m^{*}}{\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}}\delta m^{*}~f_{\bm{p}}+\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}~\delta f_{\bm{p}}

(9)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+~\big(\delta A_{0}\big)j^{0}+A_{0}\big(\delta j^{0}\big)-\Gamma\big(\delta A_{\lambda}\big)j^{\lambda}-\Gamma A_{\lambda}\big(\delta j^{\lambda}\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+~\Lambda\big(\delta B\big)n_{s}+\Lambda B\big(\delta n_{s}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(-\delta\bm{A}\big)\cdot\bm{j}+n_{s}\big(\delta m^{*}\big)+g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}~\delta f_{\bm{p}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+~\big(\delta A_{0}\big)j^{0}+A_{0}~g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\delta f_{\bm{p}}-\Gamma\big(\delta A_{\lambda}\big)j^{\lambda}-\Gamma A_{\lambda}\big(\delta j^{\lambda}\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+~\Lambda\big(\delta B\big)n_{s}+\Lambda B\big(\delta n_{s}\big)~,

we see that the quasiparticle energy will only take the form as in Eq. (
1
) if

\begin{cases}\big(-\delta\bm{A}\big)\cdot\bm{j}+\big(\delta A_{0}\big)j^{0}-\Gamma\big(\delta A_{\lambda}\big)j^{\lambda}-\Gamma A_{\lambda}\big(\delta j^{\lambda}\big)=0\\
n_{s}\big(\delta m^{*}\big)+\Lambda\big(\delta B\big)n_{s}+\Lambda B\big(\delta n_{s}\big)=0\end{cases}

(10)

The first of the equations can be further rewritten as

\displaystyle\big[1-\Gamma\big]\big(\delta A_{\mu}\big)j^{\mu}-\Gamma A_{\lambda}\big(\delta j^{\lambda}\big)=0~.

(11)

We then calculate

\displaystyle\delta A_{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big(\delta\alpha(n)\Big)j_{\mu}+\alpha(n)\big(\delta j_{\mu}\big)

(12)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigg(\frac{d\alpha(n)}{dn}\delta n\bigg)j_{\mu}+\alpha(n)\big(\delta j_{\mu}\big)

(13)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigg(\frac{d\alpha(n)}{dn}\frac{1}{n}j^{\mu}\big(\delta j_{\mu}\big)\bigg)j_{\mu}+\alpha(n)\big(\delta j_{\mu}\big)~,

(14)

where we have used the fact that
n=\big(j_{\mu}j^{\mu}\big)^{1/2}
, so that Eq. (
11
) becomes

\displaystyle\big[1-\Gamma\big]\Bigg(\rho\frac{d\alpha(n)}{dn}j^{\mu}\big(\delta j_{\mu}\big)+\alpha(n)\big(\delta j_{\mu}\big)j^{\mu}\Bigg)-\Gamma A^{\mu}\big(\delta j_{\mu}\big)=0~,

(15)

which further reduces to

\displaystyle\big[1-\Gamma\big]\Bigg(\rho\frac{d\alpha(n)}{dn}+\alpha(n)\Bigg)-\Gamma\alpha(n)=0~.

(16)

This is a differential equation for
\alpha(n)
,

\displaystyle\frac{d\alpha(n)}{dn}=\frac{2\Gamma-1}{1-\Gamma}\frac{\alpha(n)}{n}~,

(17)

and because
\Gamma
is a constant, it follows that we must have

\displaystyle\alpha(n)=C_{k}n^{\beta}~,

(18)

where
C_{k}
is some constant and
\Gamma
is related to
\beta
through

\displaystyle\Gamma=\frac{\beta+1}{\beta+2}~.

(19)

Identifying
\beta=b_{k}-2
leads to the same vector-current–dependent term as in Eq. (
89
) in the case
K=1
.

Using the fact that the effective mass is given by Eq. (
5
), from which we have
\delta m^{*}=-\delta B
where
\delta B(n_{s})=(dB(n_{s})/dn_{s})\delta n_{s}
, the second of the conditions in Eq. (
10
) becomes

-n_{s}\frac{dB(n_{s})}{dn_{s}}\big(\delta n_{s}\big)+\Lambda\frac{dB(n_{s})}{dn_{s}}\big(\delta n_{s}\big)n_{s}+\Lambda B(n_{s})\big(\delta n_{s}\big)=0~,

(20)

which can be immediately rewritten as a differential equation for
B(n_{s})
,

\frac{dB(n_{s})}{dn_{s}}=\frac{\Lambda}{1-\Lambda}\frac{B(n_{s})}{n_{s}}~.

(21)

Because
\Lambda
is also a constant, here it also follows that

\displaystyle B(n_{s})=G_{m}n_{s}^{\gamma}~,

(22)

where
G_{m}
is some constant and
\Lambda
is related to
\gamma
through

\displaystyle\Lambda=\frac{\gamma}{\gamma+1}~.

(23)

Identifying
\gamma=d_{m}-1
leads to the same scalar-current–dependent term as in Eq. (
89
) in the case
M=1
.

Chapter 17
Parametrization equations

Here, we derive explicit forms of the parametrization equations, Eqs. (
6
-
13
), in the VSDF model with an arbitrary number of vector and scalar interaction terms,
K
and
M
. For simplicity, we consider only matter composed of protons and neutrons with degenerate masses, and their antiparticles.

The parameters of the EOS are established based on the thermodynamic properties of the system, that is for uniform nuclear matter in the rest frame. In particular, the energy density in the VSDF model, Eq. (
89
), becomes

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{(K,M)}\big|_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{rest}\\
\text{frame}\end{subarray}}=g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\epsilon^{*}_{\textrm{kin}}~\big(f_{\bm{p}}+\bar{f}_{\bm{p}}\big)+\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{C_{k}}{b_{k}}n_{B}^{b_{k}}+\sum_{m=1}^{M}G_{m}\frac{d_{m}-1}{d_{m}}n_{s}^{d_{m}}~.

(1)

In the following we will drop the subscript “rest frame” for clarity. In the limit
T\to 0
, the Fermi-Dirac distribution function for particles approaches the behavior of a Heaviside-theta function, while the distribution for antiparticles becomes zero, so that at
T=0
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{(K,M)}^{(T=0)}=\frac{g}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{p_{F}}dp~p^{2}\sqrt{p^{2}+{m^{*}}^{2}}+\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{C_{k}}{b_{k}}n_{B}^{b_{k}}+\sum_{m=1}^{M}G_{m}\frac{d_{m}-1}{d_{m}}n_{s}^{d_{m}}~.

(2)

The integral in the above expression can be explicitly calculated,

\displaystyle\frac{g}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{p_{F}}dp~p^{2}\sqrt{p^{2}+{m^{*}}^{2}}=\frac{g}{16\pi^{2}}\left[2{E_{F}^{*}}^{3}p_{F}-{m^{*}}^{2}E_{F}^{*}p_{F}-{m^{*}}^{4}\ln\left(\frac{E_{F}^{*}+p_{F}}{m^{*}}\right)\right]~;

(3)

we note, however, that often the integral form is more convenient for calculations such as shown below.

Similarly, the pressure in the VSDF model, Eq. (
98
), is easily generalized to the case with antiparticles, and adopting the notation utilizing the effective chemical potential, Eq. (
84
), can be written as

\displaystyle P_{(K,M)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~T~\ln\Big[1+e^{-\beta\big(\sqrt{p^{2}+{m^{*}}^{2}}-\mu^{*}\big)}\Big]

(4)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt~+g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~T~\ln\Big[1+e^{-\beta\big(\sqrt{p^{2}+{m^{*}}^{2}}+\mu^{*}\big)}\Big]

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt~+\sum_{k=1}^{K}C_{k}\left(\frac{b_{k}-1}{b_{k}}\right)n_{B}^{b_{k}}-\sum_{m=1}^{M}G_{m}\left(\frac{d_{m}-1}{d_{m}}\right)n_{s}^{d_{m}}~.

Here as well the expression simplifies in the
T\to 0
limit,

\displaystyle P_{(K,M)}^{(T=0)}=\frac{g}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{p_{F}}dp~\frac{p^{4}}{3E^{*}}+\sum_{k=1}^{K}C_{k}\left(\frac{b_{k}-1}{b_{k}}\right)n_{B}^{b_{k}}-\sum_{m=1}^{M}G_{m}\frac{d_{m}-1}{d_{m}}n_{s}^{d_{m}}~,

(5)

and the integral for the kinetic energy term can be calculated explicitly,

\displaystyle\frac{g}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{p_{F}}dp~\frac{p^{4}}{3E^{*}}=\frac{g}{16\pi^{2}}\left[\frac{2}{3}{E_{F}^{*}}p_{F}^{3}-{m^{*}}^{2}E_{F}^{*}p_{F}+{m^{*}}^{4}\ln\left(\frac{E_{F}^{*}+p_{F}}{m^{*}}\right)\right]~.

(6)

Appendix 17.A
The minimum of the energy per particle

The position of the minimum of the binding energy per particle, which is equivalent to the minimum of the energy per particle (where we note that
E/N_{B}=\mathcal{E}/n_{B}
), is determined by the equation

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dn_{B}}\left(\frac{\mathcal{E}^{(T=0)}_{(K,M)}}{n_{B}}\right)=0~,

(7)

the solution to which gives the saturation density
n_{0}
. At zero temperature, the energy density is a function of number density
n_{B}
only. However, due to the presence of many terms with complicated
n_{B}
-dependence, see Eq. (
2
), we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{(T=0)}_{(K,M)}=\mathcal{E}^{(T=0)}_{(K,M)}\Big(p_{F}(n_{B}),m^{*}(n_{s}),n_{B},n_{s}(n_{B})\Big)

(8)

and it is convenient to rewrite the derivative using the chain rule,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dn_{B}}=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{n_{B}}}\bigg|_{p_{F},n_{s}}+\frac{dp_{F}}{dn_{B}}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p_{F}}}\bigg|_{n_{B},n_{s}}+\frac{dn_{s}}{dn_{B}}\left(\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{n_{s}}}\Big|_{m^{*}}+\frac{dm^{*}}{dn_{s}}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{m^{*}}}\Big|_{n_{s}}\right)\bigg|_{n_{B},p_{F}}~.

(9)

First, we can see that

\displaystyle\hskip-42.67912pt\left(\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{n_{s}}}\Big|_{m^{*}}+\frac{dm^{*}}{dn_{s}}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{m^{*}}}\Big|_{n_{s}}\right)\bigg|_{n_{B},p_{F}}\mathcal{E}^{(T=0)}_{(K,M)}

\displaystyle\hskip-8.53581pt=\sum_{m=1}^{M}G_{m}(d_{m}-1)n_{s}^{d_{m}-1}-\sum_{m=1}^{M}G_{m}(d_{m}-1)n_{s}^{d_{m}-2}~\frac{g}{2\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{p_{F}}dp~p^{2}~\frac{m^{*}}{\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}^{*}}=0~.

(10)

Here we note that while the vanishing of the derivative of the energy density with respect to the auxiliary field
n_{s}
is reminiscent of the vanishing of the same derivative of the pressure, where the latter is a consequence of the minimization of the thermodynamic potential with respect to the auxiliary field (see Appendix
15.E
), in the case of the energy density this derivative vanishes only at
T=0
due to the trivial form of the distribution function, and should not be thought of as the minimization condition. Next, we calculate

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{n_{B}}}\bigg|_{p_{F},n_{s}}+\frac{dp_{F}}{dn_{B}}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p_{F}}}\bigg|_{n_{B},n_{s}}\right)\mathcal{E}^{(T=0)}_{(K,M)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}C_{k}n_{B}^{b_{k}-1}+\frac{dp_{F}}{dn_{B}}~\frac{g}{2\pi^{2}}p_{F}^{2}\sqrt{p_{F}^{2}+{m^{*}}^{2}}

(11)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{p_{F}^{2}+{m^{*}}^{2}}+\sum_{k=1}^{K}C_{k}n_{B}^{b_{k}-1}~,

where we have used
p_{F}=\left(\frac{6\pi^{2}n_{B}}{g}\right)^{1/3}
(see Eq. (
27
) and below). Thus we have

\displaystyle\frac{d\mathcal{E}^{(T=0)}_{(K,M)}}{dn_{B}}=\epsilon_{F}+\sum_{k=1}^{K}C_{k}n_{B}^{b_{k}-1}~,

(12)

where
\epsilon_{F}
is the kinetic energy at the Fermi surface,
\epsilon_{F}=\sqrt{p_{F}^{2}+{m^{*}}^{2}}
.

Let us note that the above result can be obtained in a much easier way: at
T=0
, the first law of thermodynamics
d\mathcal{E}=Tds+\mu_{B}dn_{B}
reduces to
d\mathcal{E}=\mu_{B}dn_{B}
, so that

\displaystyle\frac{d\mathcal{E}^{(T=0)}_{(K,M)}}{dn_{B}}=\mu_{B}=\varepsilon_{F}~,

(13)

where by
\varepsilon_{F}
we denote the quasiparticle energy at the Fermi surface.

Altogether, in the VSDF model Eq. (
7
) can be explicitly written as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n_{B}}\left(\varepsilon_{F}-\frac{\mathcal{E}^{(T=0)}_{(K,M)}}{n_{B}}\right)=0~.

(14)

This agrees with the Hugenholtz-van-Hove theorem
[
226
]
, which states that
\varepsilon_{F}=(E_{0}/N)+(P/n)
, where
E_{0}
is the system’s ground state energy, and from which it follows that for systems at equilibrium (where pressure
P=0
) the Fermi energy and the energy per particle in the system,
E/N=\mathcal{E}/n_{B}
, are equal. The fact that
P
vanishes at equilibrium an be easily seen for systems at zero temperature, as at
T=0
the pressure is given by

\displaystyle P=n_{B}^{2}\frac{d{\left(\frac{\mathcal{E}}{n_{B}}\right)}}{d{n_{B}}}~,

(15)

which is automatically zero if Eq. (
7
) holds, that is if the system is in equilibrium.

Appendix 17.B
Spinodal region boundary at
T=0

The boundaries of the spinodal region at
T=0
are given by the extremal points of the pressure, given by

\displaystyle\frac{dP_{(K,M)}^{(T=0)}}{dn_{B}}=0~.

(16)

Similarly as in the case of energy density considered in the previous section, pressure has a complicated dependence on baryon density
n_{B}
, see Eq. (
5
), and so it is convenient to use the chain rule,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dn_{B}}\bigg|_{T}=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{n_{B}}}\bigg|_{T,p_{F},n_{s}}+\frac{dp_{F}}{dn_{B}}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p_{F}}}\bigg|_{T,n_{B},n_{s}}+\frac{dn_{s}}{dn_{B}}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{n_{s}}}\bigg|_{T,n_{B},p_{F}}~.

(17)

We note that while the last term in the equations above disappears in minimization of the thermodynamic potential when
T
and
\mu_{B}
are held fixed, in this case it is finite. We calculate

\displaystyle\hskip-51.21495pt\frac{\partial{P_{(K,M)}^{(T=0)}}}{\partial{n_{B}}}\bigg|_{p_{F},n_{s}}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}C_{k}(b_{k}-1)n_{B}^{b_{k}-1}~,

(18)

\displaystyle\hskip-51.21495pt\frac{dp_{F}}{dn_{B}}\frac{\partial{P_{(K,M)}^{(T=0)}}}{\partial{p_{F}}}\bigg|_{n_{B},n_{s}}=\frac{p_{F}^{2}}{3E_{F}^{*}}~,

(19)

\displaystyle\hskip-51.21495pt\frac{dn_{s}}{dn_{B}}\frac{\partial{P_{(K,M)}^{(T=0)}}}{\partial{n_{s}}}\bigg|_{n_{B},p_{F}}=\frac{dn_{s}}{dn_{B}}\bigg[\frac{g}{16\pi^{2}}\frac{dm^{*}}{dn_{s}}\bigg(\frac{8p_{F}^{3}m^{*}}{3E_{F}^{*}}\bigg)-n_{s}\frac{dm^{*}}{dn_{s}}-\sum_{m=1}^{M}G_{m}(d_{m}-1)n_{s}^{d_{m}-1}\bigg]~,

(20)

where we note that the last equation requires a considerable amount of algebra, including using the expression for the scalar density at
T=0
,

\displaystyle n_{s}=\frac{g}{4\pi^{2}}\left[m^{*}E_{F}^{*}p_{F}-{m^{*}}^{3}\ln\left(\frac{E_{F}^{*}+p_{F}}{m^{*}}\right)\right]~.

(21)

By the gap equation, Eq. (
94
), we have

\displaystyle-n_{s}\frac{dm^{*}}{dn_{s}}=\sum_{m=1}^{M}G_{m}(d_{m}-1)n_{s}^{d_{m}-1}~,

(22)

so that Eq. (
20
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\frac{dn_{s}}{dn_{B}}\frac{\partial{P_{(K,M)}^{(T=0)}}}{\partial{n_{s}}}\bigg|_{n_{B},p_{F}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{g}{16\pi^{2}}\bigg(\frac{8p_{F}^{3}m^{*}}{3E_{F}^{*}}\Bigg)\frac{dm^{*}}{dn_{B}}=\frac{p_{F}m^{*}}{3E_{F}^{*}}\frac{dm^{*}}{dp_{F}}~.

(23)

The gap equation can be further used to calculate

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{m^{*}}}{\partial{p_{F}}}=-\sum_{m=1}^{M}G_{m}(d_{m}-1)n_{s}^{d_{m}-2}\frac{\partial{n_{s}}}{\partial{p_{F}}}=-\mathcal{G}\frac{\partial{n_{s}}}{\partial{p_{F}}}~,

(24)

where we have introduced a short-hand notation for the sum over interaction terms,

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}=\sum_{m=1}^{M}G_{m}(d_{m}-1)n_{s}^{d_{m}-2}~.

(25)

Using Eq. (
21
), we further have

\displaystyle\hskip-42.67912pt\frac{dn_{s}}{dp_{F}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{g}{4\pi^{2}}\Bigg\{\frac{2m^{*}p_{F}^{2}}{E_{F}^{*}}

(26)

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt+~\left[E_{F}^{*}p_{F}+\frac{{m^{*}}^{2}p_{F}}{E_{F}^{*}}-3{m^{*}}^{2}\ln\left(\frac{E_{F}^{*}+p_{F}}{m^{*}}\right)-\frac{{m^{*}}^{4}}{E_{F}^{*}(E_{F}^{*}+p_{F})}+{m^{*}}^{2}\right]\frac{dm^{*}}{dp_{F}}\Bigg\}~.

With

\displaystyle{m^{*}}^{4}=\Big({m^{*}}^{2}\Big)^{2}=\Big({E_{F}^{*}}^{2}-p_{F}^{2}\Big)^{2}=\Big(({E_{F}^{*}}-p_{F})({E_{F}^{*}}+p_{F})\Big)^{2}

(27)

the term in the square bracket can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\Big[\dots\Big]=E_{F}^{*}p_{F}+\frac{2{m^{*}}^{2}p_{F}}{E_{F}^{*}}-3{m^{*}}^{2}\ln\left(\frac{E_{F}^{*}+p_{F}}{m^{*}}\right)~,

(28)

and inserting Eq. (
26
) back into Eq. (
24
) finally yields

\displaystyle\frac{dm^{*}}{dp_{F}}=-\frac{\frac{2m^{*}p_{F}^{2}}{E_{F}^{*}}}{{4\pi^{2}}/{g\mathcal{G}}+\left[E_{F}^{*}p_{F}+\frac{2{m^{*}}^{2}p_{F}}{E_{F}^{*}}-3{m^{*}}^{2}\ln\left(\frac{E_{F}^{*}+p_{F}}{m^{*}}\right)\right]}~,

(29)

where
\mathcal{G}
is given by Eq. (
25
).

Altogether we then have

\displaystyle\frac{dP_{(K,M)}^{(T=0)}}{dn_{B}}=\frac{p_{F}^{2}}{3E_{F}^{*}}+\sum_{k=1}^{K}C_{k}(b_{k}-1)n_{B}^{b_{k}-1}+\frac{p_{F}m^{*}}{3E_{F}^{*}}\frac{dm^{*}}{dp_{F}}~,

(30)

where
\frac{dm^{*}}{dp_{F}}
is given by Eq. (
29
).

Appendix 17.C
Incompressibility

Classically, compressibility is defined as

\displaystyle\beta\equiv-\frac{1}{V}\left(\frac{\partial{P}}{\partial{V}}\right)^{-1}=\frac{1}{V}\left(\frac{n_{B}^{2}}{N_{B}}\frac{\partial{P}}{\partial{n_{B}}}\right)^{-1}~,

(31)

so that the incompressibility is

\displaystyle\beta^{-1}=V\frac{n_{B}^{2}}{N_{B}}\frac{\partial{P}}{\partial{n_{B}}}=n_{B}\frac{\partial{P}}{\partial{n_{B}}}~.

(32)

In nuclear physics, however, it is customary to use the following expression for incompressibility,

\displaystyle\beta^{-1}\to K=9\frac{\partial{P}}{\partial{n_{B}}}~.

(33)

Formally, the incompressibility of nuclear matter at zero temperature and saturation density,
n_{B}=n_{0}
, is defined as

\displaystyle K_{0}\equiv\left(p_{F}^{2}\frac{d^{2}}{dp_{F}^{2}}\left(\frac{\mathcal{E}^{(T=0)}_{(K,M)}}{n_{B}}\right)\right)_{n_{B}=n_{0}}~.

(34)

By expressing the derivative with respect to the Fermi momentum
p_{F}
in terms of the derivative with respect to the baryon density,
{d}/{dp_{F}}={3n_{B}}/{p_{F}}{d}/{dn_{B}}
, and using the fact that at
n_{B}=n_{0}
we have
d{\mathcal{E}^{(T=0)}_{(K,M)}}/{n_{B}}/dn_{B}=0
, we can rewrite the above equation to obtain

\displaystyle K_{0}=\left(9n_{B}^{2}\left[\frac{d^{2}}{dn_{B}^{2}}\left(\frac{\mathcal{E}^{(T=0)}_{(K,M)}}{n_{B}}\right)\right]\right)_{n_{B}=n_{0}}~.

(35)

Furthermore, again using the fact that
n_{0}
is the saturation density, this can be rewritten as

\displaystyle K_{0}=\left(9n_{B}\frac{d^{2}\mathcal{E}^{(T=0)}_{(K,M)}}{dn_{B}^{2}}\right)_{n_{B}=n_{0}}~.

(36)

Noting that the pressure at
T=0
is given by Eq. (
15
), we can at the same time calculate

\displaystyle\frac{dP}{dn_{B}}=\bigg|_{n_{B}=n_{0}}\left(n_{B}^{2}\frac{d^{2}}{dn_{B}^{2}}\left(\frac{\mathcal{E}}{n_{B}}\right)\right)_{n_{B}=n_{0}}~,

(37)

from which, by comparison with Eq. (
35
), it follows immediately that

\displaystyle K_{0}=9\left(\frac{dP}{dn_{B}}\right)_{n_{B}=n_{0}}~,

(38)

in agreement with Eq. (
33
).

To calculate the incompressibility, we will use Eq. (
36
). We know the first derivative of energy density from (
12
). Using the chain rule we can write

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dn_{B}}=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{n_{B}}}\bigg|_{p_{F},m^{*}}+\frac{dp_{F}}{dn_{B}}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{p_{F}}}\bigg|_{n_{B},m^{*}}+\frac{dp_{F}}{dn_{B}}\frac{dm^{*}}{dp_{F}}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{m^{*}}}\bigg|_{n_{B},p_{F}}~,

(39)

from which we get

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dn_{B}}\left(\frac{d\mathcal{E}^{(T=0)}_{(K,M)}}{dn_{B}}\right)=\frac{p_{F}}{3n_{B}}\left[\frac{p_{F}}{E_{F}^{*}}+\frac{\partial{m^{*}}}{\partial{p_{F}}}\frac{m^{*}}{E_{F}^{*}}\right]+\sum_{k=1}^{K}C_{k}(b_{k}-1)n_{B}^{b_{k}-2}~,

(40)

leading to

\displaystyle K_{0}\bigg|_{n_{B}=n_{0}}=\left[9n_{B}\left(\frac{p_{F}^{2}}{3n_{B}E_{F}^{*}}+\sum_{k=1}^{K}C_{k}(b_{k}-1)n_{B}^{b_{k}-2}+\frac{p_{F}m^{*}}{3n_{B}E_{F}^{*}}\frac{dm^{*}}{dp_{F}}\right)\right]_{n_{B}=n_{0}}~,

(41)

where
\frac{dm^{*}}{dp_{F}}
is given by (
29
).

We note that using Eq. (
38
) leads to the same result, as is clear from Eq. (
30
).

Appendix 17.D
The critical point

To apply the conditions for identifying the location of the critical point at
(n_{c},T_{c})
,

\displaystyle\left(\frac{dP}{dn_{B}}\right)_{T}(n_{c},T_{c})=0~,

(42)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{d^{2}P}{dn_{B}^{2}}\right)_{T}(n_{c},T_{c})=0~.

(43)

one needs to know the first and second derivative of the pressure with respect to baryon number density.

Here we note that the resulting formulas are relatively complicated. In view of this, it may be more convenient to utilize numerical derivatives in solving Eqs. (
42
) and (
43
). Nevertheless, obtaining analytical formulas is possible, and in particular they work very well in the case of the VDF model, where scalar type interactions are neglected (
M=0
). To illustrate the calculation, below we compute the first derivative of the pressure in the VSDF model. The formula for the VDF model is easily obtained by taking
M=0
. The second and third derivatives of the pressure in the VDF model are provided in Appendix
21
.

1
First derivative of the pressure

We want to calculate
\left({dP_{(K,M)}}/{dn_{B}}\right)\big|_{T}
. It’s clear from Eq. (
4
) that the pressure is a function of

\displaystyle P_{(K,M)}=P_{(K,M)}\Big(T,m^{*}\big(n_{s}(T,n_{B})\big),\mu^{*}(T,n_{B}),n_{B},n_{s}(T,n_{B})\Big).

(44)

We will keep
T
constant, so that the derivative with respect to
n_{B}
can be written as

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dn_{B}}=\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{n_{B}}}\bigg|_{T,\mu^{*},n_{s}}+\frac{d\mu^{*}}{dn_{B}}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\mu^{*}}}\bigg|_{T,n_{B},n_{s}}+\frac{dn_{s}}{dn_{B}}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{n_{s}}}\bigg|_{T,n_{B},\mu^{*}}~.

(45)

Note here that the effective mass is an explicit function of
n_{s}
,
m^{*}=m^{*}(n_{s})
, and that we take
\mu^{*}
, as opposed to
\mu_{B}
, as our chosen variable; an important thing to bear in mind is that one needs to be consistent about this decision.

We have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{P}}{\partial{n_{B}}}\bigg|_{T,\mu^{*},n_{s}}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}C_{k}(b_{k}-1)n_{B}^{b_{k}-1}

(46)

and

\displaystyle\frac{d\mu^{*}}{dn_{B}}\frac{\partial{P}}{\partial{\mu^{*}}}\bigg|_{T,n_{B},n_{s}}=\left(\frac{dn_{B}}{d\mu^{*}}\right)^{-1}n_{B}~,

(47)

where the latter equation follows directly from Eq. (
4
). The last derivative term stemming from Eq. (
45
) disappears because the scalar field minimizes the thermodynamic potential (see Appendix
15.E
, which can be easily generalized to the case with antiparticles, considered here), so that altogether

\displaystyle\frac{dP}{dn_{B}}\bigg|_{T}=n_{B}\left(\frac{dn_{B}}{d\mu^{*}}\right)^{-1}+\sum_{k=1}^{K}C_{k}(b_{k}-1)n_{B}^{b_{k}-1}~.

(48)

We introduce the following vector-density– and scalar-density–like integrals over powers of the distribution function,

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{a}=g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~f_{\bm{p}}^{a}~,

(49)

\displaystyle\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{a}=g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\bar{f}_{\bm{p}}^{a}~,

(50)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{a}=g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\frac{m^{*}}{E^{*}}~f_{\bm{p}}^{a}~,

(51)

\displaystyle\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.27777pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{S}$}}_{a}=g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\frac{m^{*}}{E^{*}}~\bar{f}_{\bm{p}}^{a}~.

(52)

Using these, we calculate the following derivative,

\displaystyle\frac{d\big(\mathcal{B}_{k}\pm\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{k}\big)}{d\mu^{*}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta k\Bigg[\big(\mathcal{B}_{k}-\mathcal{B}_{k+1}\big)\mp\big(\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{k}-\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{k+1}\big)

(53)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-~\frac{d{m^{*}}}{d{\mu^{*}}}\bigg(\big(\mathcal{S}_{k}-\mathcal{S}_{k+1}\big)\pm\big(\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.27777pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{S}$}}_{k}-\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.27777pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{S}$}}_{k+1}\big)\bigg)\Bigg]~.

In particular, we have
n_{B}=\mathcal{B}_{1}-\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{1}
, so that

\displaystyle\frac{dn_{B}}{d\mu^{*}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta\Bigg[\Big(\big(\mathcal{B}_{1}-\mathcal{B}_{2}\big)+\big(\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{1}-\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{2}\big)\Big)-\frac{dm^{*}}{d\mu^{*}}\bigg(\big(\mathcal{S}_{1}-\mathcal{S}_{2}\big)-\big(\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.27777pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{S}$}}_{1}-\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.27777pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{S}$}}_{2}\big)\bigg)\Bigg]~.

(54)

Next, we can always rewrite

\displaystyle\frac{dm^{*}}{d\mu^{*}}=\frac{dn_{s}}{d\mu^{*}}\frac{dm^{*}}{dn_{s}}=-\frac{dn_{s}}{d\mu^{*}}\sum_{m=1}^{M}G_{m}(d_{m}-1)n_{s}^{d_{m}-2}=-\mathcal{G}\frac{dn_{s}}{d\mu^{*}}~,

(55)

where
\mathcal{G}
is given by Eq. (
25
). In general, we can calculate

\displaystyle\frac{d\big(\mathcal{S}_{k}\pm\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.27777pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{S}$}}_{k}\big)}{d\mu^{*}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{dm^{*}}{d\mu^{*}}\Bigg[g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\frac{p^{2}}{{\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}^{*}}^{3}}~\big(f_{\bm{p}}^{k}\pm\bar{f}_{\bm{p}}^{k}\big)

(58)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-~\beta k~g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\frac{{m^{*}}^{2}}{{\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}^{*}}^{2}}~\bigg(\big(f_{\bm{p}}^{k}\mp\bar{f}_{\bm{p}}^{k}\big)-\big(f_{\bm{p}}^{k+1}\mp\bar{f}_{\bm{p}}^{k+1}\big)\bigg)\Bigg]

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+~\beta k\Bigg[\big(\mathcal{S}_{k}-\mathcal{S}_{k+1}\big)\pm\big(\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.27777pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{S}$}}_{k}-\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.27777pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{S}$}}_{k+1}\big)\Bigg]~.

In particular, the scalar density
n_{s}=\mathcal{S}_{1}+\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.27777pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{S}$}}_{1}
, so that

\displaystyle\frac{dn_{s}}{d\mu^{*}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{dm^{*}}{d\mu^{*}}\Bigg[g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\frac{p^{2}}{{\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}^{*}}^{3}}~\big(f_{\bm{p}}+\bar{f}_{\bm{p}}\big)

(60)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-~\beta~g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\frac{{m^{*}}^{2}}{{\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}^{*}}^{2}}~\bigg(\big(f_{\bm{p}}-\bar{f}_{\bm{p}}\big)-\big(f_{\bm{p}}^{2}-\bar{f}_{\bm{p}}^{2}\big)\bigg)\Bigg]

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+~\beta k\Bigg[\big(\mathcal{S}_{1}-\mathcal{S}_{2}\big)+\big(\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.27777pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{S}$}}_{1}-\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.27777pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{S}$}}_{2}\big)\Bigg]~.

(61)

Inserting Eq. (
61
) into Eq. (
55
) leads to

\displaystyle\frac{dm^{*}}{d\mu^{*}}=\frac{-\mathcal{G}\beta k\Bigg[\big(\mathcal{S}_{1}-\mathcal{S}_{2}\big)+\big(\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.27777pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{S}$}}_{1}-\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.27777pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{S}$}}_{2}\big)\Bigg]}{1+\mathcal{G}g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\Bigg[\frac{p^{2}}{{\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}^{*}}^{3}}~\big(f_{\bm{p}}+\bar{f}_{\bm{p}}\big)-\beta\frac{{m^{*}}^{2}}{{\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}^{*}}^{2}}~\bigg(\big(f_{\bm{p}}-\bar{f}_{\bm{p}}\big)-\big(f_{\bm{p}}^{2}-\bar{f}_{\bm{p}}^{2}\big)\bigg)\Bigg]}~.

(62)

Putting all of these terms together, the first derivative of the pressure with respect to
n_{B}
is

\displaystyle\frac{dP}{dn_{B}}\bigg|_{T}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{Tn_{B}}{\Big(\big(\mathcal{B}_{1}-\mathcal{B}_{2}\big)+\big(\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{1}-\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{2}\big)\Big)-\frac{dm^{*}}{d\mu^{*}}\bigg(\big(\mathcal{S}_{1}-\mathcal{S}_{2}\big)-\big(\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.27777pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{S}$}}_{1}-\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 0.27777pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{S}$}}_{2}\big)\bigg)}

(63)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+~\sum_{k=1}^{K}C_{k}(b_{k}-1)n_{B}^{b_{k}-1}~,

where
{dm^{*}}/{d\mu^{*}}
is given by Eq. (
62
).

\big(T_{c}^{(Q)},n_{c}^{(Q)},\eta_{L},\eta_{R}\big)
, listed in Table
1
. It is important to note that the values of the coefficients of the interaction terms,
\{C_{1},C_{2},C_{3},C_{4}\}
, depend on a chosen system of units. Here, we adopt a convention used in many Skyrme-like parametrizations, in which the single-particle potential is written in the form

\displaystyle U=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\tilde{C}_{i}\left(\frac{n_{B}}{n_{0}}\right)^{b_{i}-1}~,

(1)

where
n_{0}
is the saturation density, so that
\tilde{C}_{i}
must have a dimension of energy. Naturally,
\tilde{C}_{i}
and
C_{i}
are related by

\displaystyle C_{i}=\frac{\tilde{C}_{i}}{n_{0}^{b_{i}-1}}~.

(2)

In Table
1
, we list coefficients
\{\tilde{C}_{1},\tilde{C}_{2},\tilde{C}_{3},\tilde{C}_{4}\}
in units of MeV. Note that in particular, the sum of all coefficients yields the (rest frame) value of the single-particle potential at
n_{B}=n_{0}
,
\sum_{i=1}^{N}\tilde{C}_{i}=-52.484\ \textrm{MeV}
.

Chapter 19
Symmetric spinodal regions

The spinodal region is the range of baryon number densities between two local extrema of pressure, a maximum at
\eta_{L}
and a minimum at
\eta_{R}
, with
\eta_{L}<\eta_{R}
. A curve exhibiting two extrema will most naturally have an inflection point approximately in between them. We can see this by considering the following polynomial:

\displaystyle f(x)=ax^{3}+bx^{2}+cx+d~,

(1)

which is a “minimal” polynomial needed to produce two local extrema. The condition for an extremum at some point
x_{0}
is

\displaystyle\frac{df}{dx}\bigg|_{x=x_{0}}=3ax^{2}+2bx+c\bigg|_{x=x_{0}}=0~.

(2)

We can solve this equation to yield the positions of the extrema
x_{L}
and
x_{R}
,

\displaystyle x_{L}=\frac{-b-\sqrt{b^{2}-3ac}}{3a}~,

(3)

\displaystyle x_{R}=\frac{-b+\sqrt{b^{2}-3ac}}{3a}~.

(4)

The position of the inflection point is established through the condition

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}f}{dx^{2}}\bigg|_{x=x_{\textrm{infl}}}=6ax+2b\bigg|_{x=x_{\textrm{infl}}}=0~,

(5)

from which we get

\displaystyle x_{\textrm{infl}}=-\frac{b}{3a}~.

(6)

It is immediately apparent that

\displaystyle x_{\textrm{infl}}=\frac{x_{L}+x_{R}}{2}~,

(7)

placing the inflection point exactly in the middle between the two extrema. This result is only exact for a third-order polynomial, and will be changed if the polynomial includes additional terms with which one is able to manipulate the behavior of the curve between the extrema.

We will now argue that in a model with vector-type interactions only, the inflection point of the pressure curve at zero temperature,

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}P(T=0)}{dn_{B}^{2}}\bigg|_{n_{B}=n_{\textrm{infl}}}=0~,

(8)

will coincide with the location of the critical point on the
n_{B}
axis. Let us first write the pressure as a sum of an ideal gas term and an interaction term,

\displaystyle P=P_{\textrm{ideal}}+P_{\textrm{int}}~.

(9)

In particular, at
T=0
the ideal part of the pressure is given by the ideal Fermi gas,
P_{\textrm{ideal}}(T=0)=P^{\textrm{FG}}_{0}
. Because the Fermi gas at zero temperature depends on the baryon density as
P^{\textrm{FG}}_{0}\propto n_{B}^{4/3}
, for large densities we can safely assume that

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}P^{\textrm{FG}}_{0}}{dn_{B}^{2}}=\frac{4}{9}n_{B}^{-2/3}\approx 0~.

(10)

It then follows that at the inflection point we must have

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}P_{\textrm{int}}}{dn_{B}^{2}}\bigg|_{n_{B}=n_{\textrm{infl}}}\approx 0~.

(11)

At the same time, the condition for the position of the critical point at some location
(T_{c},n_{c})
leads to

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}P_{\textrm{int}}}{dn_{B}^{2}}\bigg|_{n_{B}=n_{c}}=-\frac{d^{2}P_{\textrm{ideal}}}{dn_{B}^{2}}\bigg|_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{B}=n_{c}\\
T=T_{c}\end{subarray}}~.

(12)

For large enough temperatures, the ideal Fermi gas is well approximated by the ideal Boltzmann gas, and we can write the ideal part of the pressure as

\displaystyle P_{\textrm{ideal}}\approx Tn_{B}~.

(13)

As a result, Eq. (
12
) becomes

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}P_{\textrm{int}}}{dn_{B}^{2}}\bigg|_{n_{B}=n_{c}}=0~,

(14)

which immediately confirms that in this case, the location of the critical density
n_{c}
coincides with the location of the inflection point
n_{\textrm{infl}}
of the pressure at zero temperature. Moreover, going beyond the approximation used in Eq. (
10
), we see that at zero temperature the pressure at
n_{B}=n_{c}
will have a very small and positive curvature, which means that the critical density is somewhat larger than the inflection point density,
n_{c}\gtrsim n_{\textrm{infl}}
.

The VDF model largely reproduces the behavior described above. First, due to the fact that the pressure fits in the VDF model are “minima” fits reproducing (among other constraints) two local extrema, a maximum at
\eta_{L}
and a minimum at
\eta_{R}
, the inflection point of the pressure lies roughly in the middle between
\eta_{L}
and
\eta_{R}
. Second, due to the thermal part of the pressure being just like that of an ideal gas, the location of the critical point
n_{c}
and the location of the inflection point of the pressure at zero temperature
n_{\textrm{infl}}
are related by
n_{c}=n_{\textrm{infl}}+\delta n
, where
\delta n
is a small positive correction. This explains why in the VDF model the critical baryon number density
n_{c}
lies roughly in the middle of the spinodal region
(\eta_{L},\eta_{R})
.

Chapter 20
The speed of sound

The isentropic speed of sound is defined as the following total derivative

\displaystyle c_{\sigma}^{2}\equiv\left(\frac{d{P}}{d{\mathcal{E}}}\right)_{S/N}~,

(1)

where
\sigma\equiv S/N
,
S
is the entropy, and
N
is the number of particles, so that
\sigma
is the entropy per particle. The isothermal speed of sound is defined as

\displaystyle c_{T}^{2}\equiv\left(\frac{d{P}}{d{\mathcal{E}}}\right)_{T}~,

(2)

In practice, carrying out either of these derivatives depends strongly on the primary variables in terms of which the variations in pressure, energy density, and entropy per particle can be obtained.

In general, the total differential of the pressure can be given in terms of its two primary variables
A
and
B
,

\displaystyle dP(A,B)=\left(\frac{d{P}}{d{A}}\right)_{B}dA+\left(\frac{d{P}}{d{B}}\right)_{A}dB~.

(3)

In order to calculate the speed of sound as defined in Eq. (
1
) or (
2
), we instead need to have

\displaystyle dP\big(\mathcal{E},Y\big)=\left(\frac{d{P}}{d{\mathcal{E}}}\right)_{Y}d\mathcal{E}+\left(\frac{d{P}}{d{Y}}\right)_{\mathcal{E}}dY~,

(4)

where
Y=\{(S/N),T\}
. The above equation has the obvious advantage that we can explicitly set
dY
to zero.

The following is based on the assumption that since
A
and
B
are the primary variables in which the pressure is given (either as an analytic formula or as a table in
A
and
B
), we know what the derivatives
\left(d{P}/d{A}\right)_{B}
and
\left(d{P}/d{B}\right)_{A}
are. Therefore, we only need to find expressions for
dA
and
dB
in terms of
d\mathcal{E}
and
dY
, as this will allow us to compute
\left(\frac{d{P}}{d{\mathcal{E}}}\right)_{Y}
and
\left(\frac{d{P}}{d{Y}}\right)_{\mathcal{E}}
. To this end we express the total differentials of
\mathcal{E}
and
C
in terms of
A
and
B
,

\displaystyle d\mathcal{E}=\left(\frac{d{\mathcal{E}}}{d{A}}\right)_{B}dA+\left(\frac{d{\mathcal{E}}}{d{B}}\right)_{A}dB~,

(5)

\displaystyle dY=\left(\frac{d{Y}}{d{A}}\right)_{B}dA+\left(\frac{d{Y}}{d{B}}\right)_{A}dB~.

(6)

We can solve the above equations for the differentials
dA
and
dB
in terms of
d\mathcal{E}
and
dY
,

\displaystyle dA=\left[d\mathcal{E}-\frac{\left(\frac{d{\mathcal{E}}}{d{B}}\right)_{A}}{\left(\frac{d{Y}}{d{B}}\right)_{A}}dY\right]\left[\left(\frac{d{\mathcal{E}}}{d{A}}\right)_{B}-\frac{\left(\frac{d{Y}}{d{A}}\right)_{B}}{\left(\frac{d{Y}}{d{B}}\right)_{A}}\left(\frac{d{\mathcal{E}}}{d{B}}\right)_{A}\right]^{-1}~,

(7)

\displaystyle dB=\left[\frac{\left(\frac{d{\mathcal{E}}}{d{A}}\right)_{B}}{\left(\frac{d{Y}}{d{B}}\right)_{A}}dY-\frac{\left(\frac{d{Y}}{d{A}}\right)_{B}}{\left(\frac{d{Y}}{d{B}}\right)_{A}}d\mathcal{E}\right]\left[\left(\frac{d{\mathcal{E}}}{d{A}}\right)_{B}-\frac{\left(\frac{d{Y}}{d{A}}\right)_{B}}{\left(\frac{d{Y}}{d{B}}\right)_{A}}\left(\frac{d{\mathcal{E}}}{d{B}}\right)_{A}\right]^{-1}~,

(8)

Because the derivatives in Eqs. (
1
) and (
2
) are taken at constant
Y
, we can set
dY=0
, which allows us to write

\displaystyle dA\Big|_{dY=0}=\frac{\left(\frac{d{Y}}{d{B}}\right)_{A}}{\left[\left(\frac{d{\mathcal{E}}}{d{A}}\right)_{B}\left(\frac{d{Y}}{d{B}}\right)_{A}-\left(\frac{d{Y}}{d{A}}\right)_{B}\left(\frac{d{\mathcal{E}}}{d{B}}\right)_{A}\right]}d\mathcal{E}~,

(9)

\displaystyle dB\Big|_{dY=0}=-\frac{\left(\frac{d{Y}}{d{A}}\right)_{B}}{\left[\left(\frac{d{\mathcal{E}}}{d{A}}\right)_{B}\left(\frac{d{Y}}{d{B}}\right)_{A}-\left(\frac{d{Y}}{d{A}}\right)_{B}\left(\frac{d{\mathcal{E}}}{d{B}}\right)_{A}\right]}d\mathcal{E}~.

(10)

Inserting the above expressions into Eq. (
3
) immediately allows us to take the derivative of the pressure with respect to the energy density with
Y
kept constant, that is

\displaystyle c_{Y}^{2}=\frac{\left(\frac{d{P}}{d{A}}\right)_{B}\left(\frac{d{Y}}{d{B}}\right)_{A}-\left(\frac{d{P}}{d{B}}\right)_{A}\left(\frac{d{Y}}{d{A}}\right)_{B}}{\left(\frac{d{\mathcal{E}}}{d{A}}\right)_{B}\left(\frac{d{Y}}{d{B}}\right)_{A}-\left(\frac{d{\mathcal{E}}}{d{B}}\right)_{A}\left(\frac{d{Y}}{d{A}}\right)_{B}}~.

(11)

By identifying
Y=\{(S/N),T\}
, explicit expressions for Eqs. (
1
) and (
2
) can be obtained, where it is useful to remember that we have

\displaystyle dP=T~ds+n~d\mu~,

(12)

\displaystyle d\mathcal{E}=T~ds+\mu~dn~,

(13)

\displaystyle d(S/N)=d(s/n)=\frac{1}{n}~ds-\frac{s}{n^{2}}~dn~.

(14)

Appendix 20.A
The isentropic speed of sound
c_{\sigma}^{2}
given pressure as a function of
T
and
\mu

In this case we set
A=T
,
B=\mu
, and
Y=(S/N)
, and we can immediately calculate

\displaystyle\left(\frac{d{P}}{d{T}}\right)_{\mu}=s\hskip 15.0pt{\textrm{and}}\hskip 15.0pt\left(\frac{d{P}}{d{\mu}}\right)_{T}=n~,

(15)

as well as

\displaystyle\left(\frac{d{\mathcal{E}}}{d{T}}\right)_{\mu}=T\left(\frac{d{s}}{d{T}}\right)_{\mu}+\mu\left(\frac{d{n}}{d{T}}\right)_{\mu}~,

(16)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{d{\mathcal{E}}}{d{\mu}}\right)_{T}=T\left(\frac{d{s}}{d{\mu}}\right)_{T}+\mu\left(\frac{d{n}}{d{\mu}}\right)_{T}~,

(17)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{d{Y}}{d{T}}\right)_{\mu}=\left(\frac{d{(S/N)}}{d{T}}\right)_{\mu}=\frac{1}{n}\left(\frac{d{s}}{d{T}}\right)_{\mu}-\frac{s}{n^{2}}\left(\frac{d{n}}{d{T}}\right)_{\mu}~,

(18)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{d{Y}}{d{\mu}}\right)_{T}=\left(\frac{d{(S/N)}}{d{\mu}}\right)_{T}=\frac{1}{n}\left(\frac{d{s}}{d{\mu}}\right)_{T}-\frac{s}{n^{2}}\left(\frac{d{n}}{d{\mu}}\right)_{T}~.

(19)

Inserting all od these into Eq. (
11
) yields

\displaystyle c_{\sigma}^{2}=\frac{sn\left(\frac{d{s}}{d{\mu}}\right)_{T}-s^{2}\left(\frac{d{n}}{d{\mu}}\right)_{T}-n^{2}\left(\frac{d{s}}{d{T}}\right)_{\mu}+sn\left(\frac{d{n}}{d{T}}\right)_{\mu}}{\left(sT+\mu n\right)\left[\left(\frac{d{s}}{d{\mu}}\right)_{T}\left(\frac{d{n}}{d{T}}\right)_{\mu}-\left(\frac{d{s}}{d{T}}\right)_{\mu}\left(\frac{d{n}}{d{\mu}}\right)_{T}\right]}~.

(20)

We note that from Gibbs relation we have

\displaystyle sT+\mu n=\mathcal{E}+P~,

(21)

while from Maxwell’s relations

\displaystyle\left(\frac{d{s}}{d{\mu}}\right)_{T}=\left(\frac{d{n}}{d{\mu}}\right)_{T}~.

(22)

Appendix 20.B
The isentropic speed of sound
c_{\sigma}^{2}
given pressure as a function of
T
and
n

In this case we set
A=T
,
B=n
, and
Y=(S/N)
. We assume that both

\displaystyle\left(\frac{d{P}}{d{T}}\right)_{n}\hskip 15.0pt{\textrm{and}}\hskip 15.0pt\left(\frac{d{P}}{d{n}}\right)_{T}

(23)

are known; for example they may be obtained by means of taking numerical derivatives based on a table of the values of
P
at different points
(T,n)
. We also have

\displaystyle\left(\frac{d{\mathcal{E}}}{d{T}}\right)_{n}=T\left(\frac{d{s}}{d{T}}\right)_{n}~,

(24)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{d{\mathcal{E}}}{d{n}}\right)_{T}=T\left(\frac{d{s}}{d{n}}\right)_{T}+\mu~,

(25)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{d{Y}}{d{T}}\right)_{n}=\left(\frac{d{(S/N)}}{d{T}}\right)_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\left(\frac{d{s}}{d{T}}\right)_{n}~,

(26)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{d{Y}}{d{n}}\right)_{T}=\left(\frac{d{(S/N)}}{d{n}}\right)_{T}=\frac{1}{n}\left(\frac{d{s}}{d{n}}\right)_{T}-\frac{s}{n^{2}}~.

(27)

Inserting all od these into Eq. (
11
) yields

\displaystyle c_{\sigma}^{2}=\frac{s\left(\frac{d{P}}{d{T}}\right)_{n}+n\left(\frac{d{P}}{d{n}}\right)_{T}\left(\frac{d{s}}{d{T}}\right)_{n}-n\left(\frac{d{P}}{d{T}}\right)_{n}\left(\frac{d{s}}{d{n}}\right)_{T}}{\big(sT+\mu n\big)\left(\frac{d{s}}{d{T}}\right)_{n}}~.

(28)

Appendix 20.C
The isothermal speed of sound
c_{T}^{2}
given pressure as a function of
T
and
\mu

In this case we set
A=T
,
B=\mu
, and
Y=T
. We reuse Eqs. (
15
-
17
), while for the other two derivatives we get trivial results

\displaystyle\left(\frac{d{Y}}{d{T}}\right)_{\mu}=\left(\frac{d{T}}{d{T}}\right)_{\mu}=1~,

(29)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{d{Y}}{d{\mu}}\right)_{T}=\left(\frac{d{T}}{d{\mu}}\right)_{T}=0~.

(30)

Thus

\displaystyle c_{T}^{2}=\frac{n}{T\left(\frac{d{s}}{d{\mu}}\right)_{T}+\mu\left(\frac{d{n}}{d{\mu}}\right)_{T}}~.

(31)

Appendix 20.D
The isothermal speed of sound
c_{T}^{2}
given pressure as a function of
T
and
n

In this case we set
A=T
,
B=n
, and
Y=T
. We reuse Eqs. (
24
-
25
) , while for the other two derivatives we again get trivial results

\displaystyle\left(\frac{d{Y}}{d{T}}\right)_{n}=\left(\frac{d{T}}{d{T}}\right)_{n}=1~,

(32)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{d{Y}}{d{n}}\right)_{T}=\left(\frac{d{T}}{d{n}}\right)_{T}=0~.

(33)

Therefore

\displaystyle c_{T}^{2}=\frac{\left(\frac{d{P}}{d{n}}\right)_{T}}{T\left(\frac{d{s}}{d{n}}\right)_{T}+\mu}~.

(34)

Appendix 20.E
Showing the equivalency of formulas

The equivalence of the two formulas for the isentropic speed of sound squared, Eqs. (
20
) and (
28
), as well as of the two formulas for the isothermal speed of sound squared, Eqs. (
31
) and (
34
), can be easily shown. It is convenient to start from Eq. (
28
). Using the same strategy as that leading to Eqs. (
7
) and (
8
), but adapted a change of variables according to
(T,n)\to(T,\mu)
, we arrive at

\displaystyle\left(\frac{d{P}}{d{n}}\right)_{T}=\frac{n}{\left(\frac{d{n}}{d{\mu}}\right)_{T}}~,

(35)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{d{P}}{d{T}}\right)_{n}=s-n\frac{\left(\frac{d{n}}{d{T}}\right)_{\mu}}{\left(\frac{\partial{n}}{\partial{\mu}}\right)_{T}}~,

(36)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{d{s}}{d{n}}\right)_{T}=\frac{\left(\frac{d{s}}{d{\mu}}\right)_{T}}{\left(\frac{\partial{n}}{\partial{\mu}}\right)_{T}}~,

(37)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{d{s}}{d{T}}\right)_{n}=\left(\frac{d{s}}{d{T}}\right)_{\mu}-\left(\frac{d{s}}{d{\mu}}\right)_{T}\frac{\left(\frac{d{n}}{d{T}}\right)_{\mu}}{\left(\frac{d{n}}{d{\mu}}\right)_{T}}~.

(38)

The above formulas, inserted into Eq. (
28
), after some algebra yield Eq. (
20
). The same allows to transform Eq. (
34
) into Eq. (
31
).

Appendix 20.F
Behavior in the
\mu\to 0
and
T\to 0
limits

1
The limit of
\mu\to 0

Let us consider the
\mu\to 0
limit of the isentropic speed of sound, which also implies
n\to 0
. Using Eq. (
28
) as well as Eqs. (
36
) and (
38
) together with the fact that
\lim_{\mu\to 0}~\left(d{n}/d{T}\right)_{\mu}=0
, we immediately get

\displaystyle\lim_{\mu\to 0}c_{\sigma}^{2}=\frac{s}{T\left(\frac{d{s}}{d{T}}\right)_{\mu}}~.

(39)

This can be rewritten by realizing that the specific heat at constant volume is defined as

\displaystyle C_{V}=\left(\frac{dE}{dT}\right)_{V,N}=T\left(\frac{d{S}}{d{T}}\right)_{n}~,

(40)

where
E
is the total energy of the system. Because at the same time

\displaystyle S=V\left(\frac{d{P}}{d{T}}\right)_{\mu}~,

(41)

we can rewrite Eq. (
40
) as

\displaystyle C_{V}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle TV\frac{d{}}{d{T}}\bigg|_{n}\left(\frac{d{P}}{d{T}}\right)_{\mu}=TV\frac{d{}}{d{T}}\bigg|_{\mu}\left(\frac{d{P}}{d{T}}\right)_{n}=TV\frac{d{}}{d{T}}\bigg|_{\mu}\left[s+n\left(\frac{d{\mu}}{d{T}}\right)_{n}\right]~.

(42)

In the limit
n\to 0
the second term in the square bracket drops out, so that we get

\displaystyle c_{v}=\frac{C_{V}}{V}=T\left(\frac{d{s}}{d{T}}\right)_{\mu}~.

(43)

We will obtain

\displaystyle\lim_{\mu\to 0}c_{\sigma}^{2}=\frac{s}{c_{V}}

(44)

as long as
\left(d{}/d{T}\right)_{\mu}=\left(d{}/d{T}\right)_{n}
. This indeed is the case in the limit
\mu\to 0
, which can be seen as follows.

Let us consider quite generally the derivative

\displaystyle\left(\frac{dA(T,\mu_{B})}{dC(T,\mu_{B})}\right)_{n_{B}}~,

(45)

where
A
and
C
are any functions of
T
and
\mu_{B}
. We can always write it out in the following way,

\displaystyle\left(\frac{dA(T,\mu_{B})}{dC(T,\mu_{B})}\right)_{n_{B}}=\left(\frac{\left(\frac{dA}{dT}\right)_{\mu_{B}}dT+\left(\frac{dA}{d\mu_{B}}\right)_{T}d\mu_{B}}{\left(\frac{dC}{dT}\right)_{\mu_{B}}dT+\left(\frac{dC}{d\mu_{B}}\right)_{T}d\mu_{B}}\right)_{n_{B}}~.

(46)

We can also write

\displaystyle dn_{B}=\left(\frac{dn_{B}}{dT}\right)_{\mu_{B}}dT+\left(\frac{dn_{B}}{d\mu_{B}}\right)_{T}d\mu_{B}~.

(47)

If we demand that
dn_{B}=0
, then we can solve for
dT
,

\displaystyle dT=-\frac{\left(\frac{dn_{B}}{d\mu_{B}}\right)_{T}}{\left(\frac{dn_{B}}{dT}\right)_{\mu_{B}}}d\mu_{B}~.

(48)

Inserting the above into Eq. (
46
) results in

\displaystyle\left(\frac{dA(T,\mu_{B})}{dC(T,\mu_{B})}\right)_{n_{B}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\left(\frac{dA}{d\mu_{B}}\right)_{T}\left(\frac{dn_{B}}{dT}\right)_{\mu_{B}}-\left(\frac{dA}{dT}\right)_{\mu_{B}}\left(\frac{dn_{B}}{d\mu_{B}}\right)_{T}}{\left(\frac{dC}{d\mu_{B}}\right)_{T}\left(\frac{dn_{B}}{dT}\right)_{\mu_{B}}-\left(\frac{dC}{dT}\right)_{\mu_{B}}\left(\frac{dn_{B}}{d\mu_{B}}\right)_{T}}~.

(49)

We can now take the limit
\mu_{B}\to 0
. Remembering that for a gas with antiparticles
\left({dn_{B}}/{dT}\right)_{\mu=0}=0
, we arrive at

\displaystyle\lim_{\mu_{B}\to 0}\left(\frac{dA(T,\mu_{B})}{dC(T,\mu_{B})}\right)_{n_{B}}=\lim_{\mu_{B}\to 0}\frac{\left(\frac{dA}{dT}\right)_{\mu_{B}}}{\left(\frac{dC}{dT}\right)_{\mu_{B}}}=\lim_{\mu_{B}\to 0}\left(\frac{dA}{dC}\right)_{\mu_{B}}=\left(\frac{dA}{dC}\right)_{\mu_{B}=0}~.

(50)

Thus the
\mu\to 0
limit of the isentropic speed of sound
c_{\sigma}^{2}
is indeed given by Eq. (
44
).

2
The limit of
T\to 0

In the limit
T\to 0
we also have
s\to 0
, and using Eq. (
20
) we immediately get

\displaystyle\lim_{T\to 0}c_{\sigma}^{2}=\frac{n}{\mu\left(\frac{d{n}}{d{\mu}}\right)_{T}}~.

(51)

Similarly, using Eq. (
34
) it is immediately clear that

\displaystyle\lim_{T\to 0}c_{T}^{2}=\frac{\left(\frac{d{P}}{d{n}}\right)_{T}}{\mu}=\lim_{T\to 0}c_{\sigma}^{2}~.

(52)

That is, the isothermal and isetropic speed of sound are identical at
T=0
.

Additionally, let us note that in the limit
T\to 0
, for a non-interacting gas we have
\mu_{B}=\mu_{F}=\epsilon_{F}=\sqrt{p_{F}^{2}+m^{2}}
, as well as
n={gp_{F}^{3}}/{6\pi^{2}}
, so that

\displaystyle\frac{d{n}}{d{\mu_{B}}}=\frac{dp_{F}}{d\epsilon_{F}}\frac{d{n}}{d{p_{F}}}=\frac{g\epsilon_{F}p_{F}}{2\pi^{2}}

(53)

and

\displaystyle{c_{\sigma}^{2}}\Big|_{T=0}=\frac{1}{3}\frac{p_{F}^{2}}{\epsilon_{F}^{2}}~.

(54)

In the nonrelativistic limit we have
\epsilon_{F}\approx m
and thus

\displaystyle{c_{\sigma}^{2}}\Big|_{\begin{subarray}{c}T=0\\
\textrm{non-rel}\end{subarray}}=\frac{1}{3}\frac{p_{F}^{2}}{m^{2}}~.

(55)

Conversely, in the ultrarelativistic limit the speed of sound of a non-interacting Fermi gas becomes

\displaystyle{c_{\sigma}^{2}}\Big|_{\begin{subarray}{c}T=0\\
\textrm{ultra-rel}\end{subarray}}=\frac{1}{3}~,

(56)

which is consistent with the conformal limit.

Chapter 21
Pressure derivatives in the VDF model

The pressure in the VDF model, Eq. (
4
), can be divided into a Fermi-gas–like term and the interaction term,

\displaystyle P_{(K)}=P_{\textrm{FG}}+P_{\textrm{int}}~.

(1)

In the presence of antiparticles, the first term in the above equation is given by

\displaystyle\hskip-8.53581ptP_{\textrm{FG}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P_{\textrm{FG}}^{\textrm{(particles)}}+P_{\textrm{FG}}^{\textrm{(antiparticles)}}

(2)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~T~\ln\Big[1+e^{-\beta\big(\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}-\mu^{*}\big)}\Big]+g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~T~\ln\Big[1+e^{-\beta\big(\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}+\mu^{*}\big)}\Big]~,

while the second term is simply

\displaystyle P_{\textrm{int}}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}C_{k}\left(\frac{b_{k}-1}{b_{k}}\right)n_{B}^{b_{k}}~,

(3)

where
n_{B}
is the net baryon number density. With the distribution functions for baryons and antibaryons given by

\displaystyle f_{\bm{p}}=\frac{1}{e^{\beta\big(\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}-\mu^{*}\big)}+1}\hskip 15.0pt{\textrm{and}}\hskip 15.0pt\bar{f}_{\bm{p}}=\frac{1}{e^{\beta\big(\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}+\mu^{*}\big)}+1}~,

(4)

respectively, we can define the following density-like integrals over powers of the distribution functions,

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{a}=g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~f_{\bm{p}}^{a}\hskip 15.0pt{\textrm{and}}\hskip 15.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{a}=g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\bar{f}_{\bm{p}}^{a}~.

(5)

In particular, the net baryon density, entering explicitly in the second term in Eq. (
1
), is given by

\displaystyle n_{B}\equiv\mathcal{B}_{1}-\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{1}~.

(6)

In calculating
d{{}^{j}P_{(K)}}/d{n_{B}^{j}}
, the derivatives of the interaction part of the pressure
P_{\textrm{int}}
are straightforward, leading to

\displaystyle\frac{dP_{(K)}}{dn_{B}}=\frac{dP_{\textrm{FG}}}{dn_{B}}+\sum_{k=1}^{K}C_{k}\big(b_{k}-1\big)n_{B}^{b_{k}-1}~,

(7)

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}P_{(K)}}{dn_{B}^{2}}=\frac{d^{2}P_{\textrm{FG}}}{dn_{B}^{2}}+\sum_{k=1}^{K}C_{k}\big(b_{k}-1\big)^{2}n_{B}^{b_{k}-2}~,

(8)

\displaystyle\frac{d^{3}P_{(K)}}{dn_{B}^{3}}=\frac{d^{3}P_{\textrm{FG}}}{dn_{B}^{3}}+\sum_{k=1}^{K}C_{k}\big(b_{k}-1\big)^{2}\big(b_{k}-2\big)n_{B}^{b_{k}-3}~.

(9)

Below, we derive expressions for the derivatives of the Fermi-gas–like part of the pressure
d{{}^{j}P_{\textrm{FG}}}/d{n_{B}^{j}}
.

We take
P_{\textrm{FG}}
to be a function of temperature
T
and effective chemical potential
\mu^{*}
,
P_{\textrm{FG}}=P_{\textrm{FG}}(T,\mu^{*})
(we note that this choice is arbitrary and one can likewise use
P_{\textrm{FG}}=P_{\textrm{FG}}(T,\mu_{B})
or
P_{\textrm{FG}}=P_{\textrm{FG}}(T,n_{B})
, although we do find that dealing with
P_{\textrm{FG}}=P_{\textrm{FG}}(T,\mu^{*})
is the most straightforward). In this case we can write

\displaystyle\frac{dP_{\textrm{FG}}}{dn_{B}}=\left(\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{n_{B}}}+\frac{d\mu^{*}}{dn_{B}}\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\mu^{*}}}\right)P_{FG}~.

(10)

There is no explicit dependence on
n_{B}
in
P_{FG}=P_{\textrm{FG}}(T,\mu^{*})
, so that the first term is zero. First, we calculate

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{P_{FG}}}{\partial{\mu^{*}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~T~\frac{\beta e^{-\beta\big(\epsilon_{\textrm{kinetic}}-\mu^{*}\big)}}{1+e^{-\beta\big(\epsilon_{\textrm{kinetic}}-\mu^{*}\big)}}-g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~T~\frac{\beta e^{-\beta\big(\epsilon_{\textrm{kinetic}}+\mu^{*}\big)}}{1+e^{-\beta\big(\epsilon_{\textrm{kinetic}}+\mu^{*}\big)}}

(11)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~f_{\bm{p}}-g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\bar{f}_{\bm{p}}=\mathcal{B}_{1}-\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{1}=n_{B}~,

which is what we should expect. Then we want to calculate

\displaystyle\frac{d\mu^{*}}{dn_{B}}=\left(\frac{dn_{B}}{d\mu^{*}}\right)^{-1}=\left(\frac{d}{d\mu^{*}}\big(\mathcal{B}_{1}-\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{1}\big)\right)^{-1}~.

(12)

Let us see that in general

\displaystyle\hskip-42.67912pt\frac{d}{d\mu^{*}}\Big(\mathcal{B}_{k}\pm\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{k}\Big)=\frac{d}{d\mu^{*}}\left[g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\frac{1}{\Big[e^{\beta\big(\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}-\mu^{*}\big)}+1\Big]^{k}}\pm g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\frac{1}{\Big[e^{\beta\big(\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}+\mu^{*}\big)}+1\Big]^{k}}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 22.76219pt=\beta k~\left[g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\frac{e^{\beta\big(\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}-\mu^{*}\big)}}{\Big[e^{\beta\big(\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}-\mu^{*}\big)}+1\Big]^{k+1}}\mp g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\frac{e^{\beta\big(\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}+\mu^{*}\big)}}{\Big[e^{\beta\big(\epsilon_{\textrm{kin}}+\mu^{*}\big)}+1\Big]^{k+1}}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 22.76219pt=\beta k~\left[g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\Big(f_{\bm{p}}^{k}-f_{\bm{p}}^{k+1}\Big)\mp g\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}~\Big(\bar{f}_{\bm{p}}^{k}-\bar{f}_{\bm{p}}^{k+1}\Big)\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 22.76219pt=\beta k~\Big[\big(\mathcal{B}_{k}-\mathcal{B}_{k+1}\big)\mp\big(\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{k}-\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{k+1}\big)\Big]~,

(13)

where in going from the second to the third line we used the fact that the numerators can be rewritten according to
x=x+1-1
. In particular, we can use Eq. (
13
) to evaluate Eq. (
12
),

\displaystyle\frac{d{\mu^{*}}}{d{n_{B}}}=\frac{T}{\Big[\big(\mathcal{B}_{1}+\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{1}\big)-\big(\mathcal{B}_{2}+\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{2}\big)\Big]}~,

(14)

and thus we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{d{P_{FG}}}{d{n_{B}}}=\frac{Tn_{B}}{\Big[\big(\mathcal{B}_{1}+\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{1}\big)-\big(\mathcal{B}_{2}+\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{2}\big)\Big]}~.

(15)

Using Eq. (
15
), we further calculate

\displaystyle\hskip-8.53581pt\frac{d^{2}P_{\textrm{FG}}}{dn_{B}^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{T}{\big(\mathcal{B}_{1}+\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{1}\big)-\big(\mathcal{B}_{2}+\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{2}\big)}\left(1-n_{B}\frac{\frac{d}{dn_{B}}\Big[\big(\mathcal{B}_{1}+\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{1}\big)-\big(\mathcal{B}_{2}+\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{2}\big)\Big]}{\Big[\big(\mathcal{B}_{1}+\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{1}\big)-\big(\mathcal{B}_{2}+\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{2}\big)\Big]}\right)~.

(16)

Eq. (
13
) allows us to compute that in general

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\frac{d\big(\mathcal{B}_{k}\pm\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{k}\big)}{dn_{B}}=\left(\frac{d\big(\mathcal{B}_{1}-\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{1}\big)}{d\mu^{*}}\right)^{-1}\frac{d\big(\mathcal{B}_{k}\pm\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{k}\big)}{d\mu^{*}}=k~\frac{\Big[\big(\mathcal{B}_{k}-\mathcal{B}_{k+1}\big)\mp\big(\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{k}-\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{k+1}\big)\Big]}{\Big[\big(\mathcal{B}_{1}+\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{1}\big)-\big(\mathcal{B}_{2}+\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{2}\big)\Big]}

(17)

so that we have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dn_{B}}\Big[\big(\mathcal{B}_{1}+\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{1}\big)-\big(\mathcal{B}_{2}+\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{2}\big)\Big]=\frac{\big(\mathcal{B}_{1}-\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{1}\big)-3\big(\mathcal{B}_{2}-\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{2}\big)+2\big(\mathcal{B}_{3}-\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{3}\big)}{\Big[\big(\mathcal{B}_{1}+\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{1}\big)-\big(\mathcal{B}_{2}+\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{2}\big)\Big]}~,

(18)

with which Eq. (
16
) becomes

\displaystyle\hskip-11.38109pt\frac{d^{2}P_{\textrm{FG}}}{dn_{B}^{2}}=\frac{T}{\big(\mathcal{B}_{1}+\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{1}\big)-\big(\mathcal{B}_{2}+\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{2}\big)}\Bigg(1-n_{B}~\frac{\big(\mathcal{B}_{1}-\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{1}\big)-3\big(\mathcal{B}_{2}-\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{2}\big)+2\big(\mathcal{B}_{3}-\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{3}\big)}{\Big[\big(\mathcal{B}_{1}+\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{1}\big)-\big(\mathcal{B}_{2}+\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{2}\big)\Big]^{2}}\Bigg)~.

(19)

Using the same approach, we can likewise calculate

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt\frac{d^{3}P_{\textrm{FG}}}{dn_{B}^{3}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2T~\frac{\big(\mathcal{B}_{1}-\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{1}\big)-3\big(\mathcal{B}_{2}-\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{2}\big)+2\big(\mathcal{B}_{3}-\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{3}\big)}{\Big[\big(\mathcal{B}_{1}+\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{1}\big)-\big(\mathcal{B}_{2}+\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{2}\big)\Big]^{3}}

(20)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+~3Tn_{B}~\frac{\Big[\big(\mathcal{B}_{1}-\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{1}\big)-3\big(\mathcal{B}_{2}-\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{2}\big)+2\big(\mathcal{B}_{3}-\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{3}\big)\Big]^{2}}{\Big[\big(\mathcal{B}_{1}+\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{1}\big)-\big(\mathcal{B}_{2}+\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{2}\big)\Big]^{5}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-~Tn_{B}~\frac{\big(\mathcal{B}_{1}+\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{1}\big)-7\big(\mathcal{B}_{2}+\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{2}\big)+12\big(\mathcal{B}_{3}+\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{3}\big)-6\big(\mathcal{B}_{4}+\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{4}\big)}{\Big[\big(\mathcal{B}_{1}+\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{1}\big)-\big(\mathcal{B}_{2}+\hbox to0.0pt{\hskip 1.04167pt\hbox{$\overline{\hbox{}}$}\hss}{\hbox{$\mathcal{B}$}}_{2}\big)\Big]^{4}}~.

Chapter 22
Cumulants in terms of derivatives of the chemical potential

In calculation of the cumulants, it may be more convenient to consider the derivatives of the chemical potential instead of the derivatives of the pressure. In particular, while the latter are easier to interpret based on the expected behavior of the EOS, the former lead to simpler expressions. Here we provide formulas for the first six cumulants,

\displaystyle\kappa_{1}=Vn_{B}~,

(1)

\displaystyle\kappa_{2}=VT\Bigg[\frac{1}{\Big(\frac{d\mu_{B}}{dn_{B}}\Big)_{T}}\Bigg]~,

(2)

\displaystyle\kappa_{3}=VT^{2}\Bigg[-\frac{\left(\frac{d{{}^{2}\mu_{B}}}{d{n_{B}^{2}}}\right)_{T}}{\Big(\frac{d{\mu_{B}}}{d{n_{B}}}\Big)^{3}}\Bigg]~,

(3)

\displaystyle\kappa_{4}=VT^{3}\Bigg[3\frac{\left(\frac{d{{}^{2}\mu_{B}}}{d{n_{B}^{2}}}\right)_{T}^{2}}{\Big(\frac{d{\mu_{B}}}{d{n_{B}}}\Big)_{T}^{5}}-\frac{1}{\Big(\frac{d{\mu_{B}}}{d{n_{B}}}\Big)_{T}^{4}}\left(\frac{d{{}^{3}\mu_{B}}}{d{n_{B}^{3}}}\right)_{T}\Bigg]~,

(4)

\displaystyle\kappa_{5}=VT^{4}\Bigg[-15\frac{\left(\frac{d{{}^{2}\mu_{B}}}{d{n_{B}^{2}}}\right)_{T}^{3}}{\Big(\frac{d{\mu_{B}}}{d{n_{B}}}\Big)_{T}^{7}}+10\frac{\left(\frac{d{{}^{2}\mu_{B}}}{d{n_{B}^{2}}}\right)_{T}\left(\frac{d{{}^{3}\mu_{B}}}{d{n_{B}^{3}}}\right)_{T}}{\Big(\frac{d{\mu_{B}}}{d{n_{B}}}\Big)_{T}^{6}}-\frac{\left(\frac{d{{}^{4}\mu_{B}}}{d{n_{B}^{4}}}\right)_{T}}{\Big(\frac{d{\mu_{B}}}{d{n_{B}}}\Big)_{T}^{5}}\Bigg]~,

(5)

\displaystyle\kappa_{6}=VT^{5}\Bigg[105\frac{\left(\frac{d{{}^{2}\mu_{B}}}{d{n_{B}^{2}}}\right)_{T}^{4}}{\Big(\frac{d{\mu_{B}}}{d{n_{B}}}\Big)_{T}^{9}}-105\frac{\left(\frac{d{{}^{2}\mu_{B}}}{d{n_{B}^{2}}}\right)_{T}^{2}\left(\frac{d{{}^{3}\mu_{B}}}{d{n_{B}^{3}}}\right)_{T}}{\Big(\frac{d{\mu_{B}}}{d{n_{B}}}\Big)_{T}^{8}}+10\frac{\left(\frac{d{{}^{3}\mu_{B}}}{d{n_{B}^{3}}}\right)_{T}^{2}}{\Big(\frac{d{\mu_{B}}}{d{n_{B}}}\Big)_{T}^{7}}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+~15\frac{\left(\frac{d{{}^{2}\mu_{B}}}{d{n_{B}^{2}}}\right)_{T}\left(\frac{d{{}^{4}\mu_{B}}}{d{n_{B}^{4}}}\right)_{T}}{\Big(\frac{d{\mu_{B}}}{d{n_{B}}}\Big)_{T}^{7}}-\frac{\left(\frac{d{{}^{5}\mu_{B}}}{d{n_{B}^{5}}}\right)_{T}}{\Big(\frac{\partial{\mu_{B}}}{\partial{n_{B}}}\Big)^{6}}\Bigg]~.

(6)

In the VDF model the derivatives of the chemical potential are given by

\displaystyle\frac{d{{}^{j}\mu_{B}}}{d{n_{B}^{j}}}=\frac{d{{}^{j}\mu^{*}}}{d{n_{B}^{j}}}+\sum_{i}C_{i}\prod_{k=1}^{j}\big(b_{i}-j\big)n_{B}^{b_{i}-1-j}~,

(7)

and the consecutive derivatives of the effective chemical potential
\mu^{*}
can be calculated using Eqs. (
14
) and (
17
) from Appendix
21
.

Chapter 23
Basics of the kinetic theory

In this appendix, Sections
23.A
and
23.B
are based on Ref.
[
227
]
, Section
23.C
is based on Refs.
[
228
,
229
]
, Section
23.D
is based on Ref.
[
229
]
, and Section
23.E
is based on Refs.
[
229
,
230
]
.

Appendix 23.A
Phase space

We consider a system of a large number
N
of particles, described by positions
\bm{q}_{i}
and momenta
\bm{p}_{i}
,
i=1,...,N
, which are in a given configuration
(\bm{q}_{1},\dots,\bm{q}_{N},\bm{p}_{1},\dots,\bm{p}_{N})
; for simplicity of notation we denote
\bm{q}=(\bm{q}_{1},\dots,\bm{q}_{N})
and
\bm{p}=(\bm{p}_{1},\dots,\bm{p}_{N})
, so that a particular configuration of the system can be written as
(\bm{q},\bm{p})
. The considered system is characterized by a given thermodynamic property
X
; in particular,
X
may be a collection of properties, e.g., for a system characterized by given values of temperature and density we have
X=(T,n)
. We say that the system is in a macrostate characterized by
X
, and the corresponding configuration
(\bm{q},\bm{p})
is called a microstate of the system. Even though the macrostate of the system is a product of a specific microstate, there is a large number of distinct microstates that also lead to the same macrostate characterized by
X
. Moreover, while the macrostate of the system may remain unchanged, the system in consideration is dynamic and over time it will explore the available microstates (here “available” may for example mean “microstates with the same energy”, etc.). This can be conveniently visualized using the concept of the phase space
\Gamma
, which is a
6N
-dimensional space of all possible positions and momenta of particles in the system. Within
\Gamma
, there is a subset space
\Gamma_{X}
corresponding to the macrostate
X
. The thermodynamic postulate of equal
a priori
probabilities states that any microstate
(\bm{q},\bm{p})
consistent with a given macrostate
X
has an equal probability. If
X
is an equilibrium state, the system will over time move along a trajectory in phase space contained within
\Gamma_{X}
, and given a sufficiently long time, it will explore all possible microstates associated with
X
.

Appendix 23.B
Statistical ensemble

For any thermodynamic quantity
X
, taking
S
samples (measurements) of
X
allows one to compute the average of
X
,

\displaystyle\langle X\rangle=\frac{1}{S}\sum_{s=1}^{S}X_{s}~.

(1)

If the measurements are consecutive in time, as is often the case for macroscopic thermodynamic properties, the above equation may be also written as

\displaystyle\langle X\rangle=\frac{1}{S}\sum_{s=1}^{S}X(t_{s})~,

(2)

where
t_{s}
are moments in time when the measurement is made; in particular, in the limit of infinitesimal time intervals between the measurements we have

\displaystyle\langle X\rangle=\frac{1}{T}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}dt~X(t)~.

(3)

At different instances of the measurement, the system is found in different microstates in the phase space, leading to particular values of
X(t_{s})
at the moments of measurement
t_{1},t_{2},...,t_{S}
. Note that this measurement of the states of the system over time is equivalent to considering
S
snapshots of the system with particular values of
\big(\bm{q}(t_{s}),\bm{p}(t_{s})\big)
, corresponding to a particular value of
X(t_{s})=X\big(\bm{q}(t_{s}),\bm{p}(t_{s})\big)
. This in turn is equivalent to considering
S
copies of the system characterized by particular configurations in phase space
\big(\bm{q}_{s},\bm{p}_{s}\big)
and the corresponding values of
X\big(\bm{q}_{s},\bm{p}_{s}\big)
.

In fact, rather than calculating averages of observables over time, in general it is more convenient to consider a hypothetical collection of copies of the system corresponding to different phase space configurations, called a statistical ensemble. The members of the ensemble (that is, hypothetical copies of the system) reflect the possible phase space configurations that the system may explore. One can then define the probability density
f(\bm{q},\bm{p})
for a member of the ensemble to occupy the volume element
d\bm{q}~d\bm{p}
in the phase space, normalized such that the number of ensemble systems found within
d\bm{q}~d\bm{p}
is given by

\displaystyle f(\bm{q},\bm{p})~d\bm{q}~d\bm{p}~.

(4)

(We note that the particular normalization used isn’t very important as it ultimately drops out of any expressions of interest, and therefore one can choose a normalization which is most convenient for a given discussion; an alternative normalization to the one used here could prioritize that
f(\bm{q},\bm{p})
is a properly defined probability, i.e. that
\int d\bm{q}~d\bm{p}~f(\bm{q},\bm{p})=1
holds.) The ensemble average of an observable
X(\bm{q},\bm{p})
is then naturally given by

\displaystyle\langle X\rangle=\frac{\int d\bm{q}~d\bm{p}~X(\bm{q},\bm{p})~f(\bm{q},\bm{p})}{\int d\bm{q}~d\bm{p}~f(\bm{q},\bm{p})}~.

(5)

Appendix 23.C
The Liouville theorem

Let us now consider a small region of phase space around the phase space point
(\bm{q},\bm{p})
, whose boundaries extend from
\bm{q}
to
\bm{q}+\Delta\bm{q}
and from
\bm{p}
to
\bm{p}+\Delta\bm{p}
and whose volume is given by
\Delta V=\Delta\bm{q}\Delta\bm{p}
. This region contains a number of ensemble members
N=N(\Delta V)
, which is given by Eq. (
4
). Consequently, by definition, the probability density associated with this subset of the ensemble members can be written as

\displaystyle f(\Delta V)=\frac{N}{\Delta V}~.

(6)

Each of the
N
ensemble members considered here is characterized by a particular set of coordinates and momenta
(\bm{q},\bm{p})
, and is in fact a snapshot, taken at some time
t
, of a dynamical system whose positions and momenta are evolving in time. The question that can arise here is: if we were to consider the same
N
ensemble members at some later time
t+dt
, what would be the associated probability?

From the setup of the problem the number of considered ensemble members
N
is constant, therefore any change in the associated probability can only come from the change in the phase space volume
\Delta V
occupied by these ensemble members, see Eq. (
6
). We then consider

\displaystyle\frac{d\Delta V}{dt}=\frac{d\big(\Delta\bm{q}\Delta\bm{p}\big)}{dt}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{d\Delta\bm{q}}{dt}\Delta\bm{p}+\Delta\bm{q}\frac{d\Delta\bm{p}}{dt}

(7)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{d\Big[\big(\bm{q}+\Delta\bm{q}\big)-\bm{q}\Big]}{dt}\Delta\bm{p}+\Delta\bm{q}\frac{d\Big[\big(\bm{p}+\Delta\bm{p}\big)-\bm{p}\Big]}{dt}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigg[\frac{d\big(\bm{q}+\Delta\bm{q}\big)}{dt}-\frac{d\bm{q}}{dt}\bigg]\Delta\bm{p}+\Delta\bm{q}\bigg[\frac{d\big(\bm{p}+\Delta\bm{p}\big)}{dt}-\frac{d\bm{p}}{dt}\bigg]~.

Because
\Delta q
is small, we can approximate

\displaystyle\frac{d\big(\bm{q}+\Delta\bm{q}\big)}{dt}\approx\frac{d\bm{q}}{dt}+\Delta\bm{q}\frac{d}{d\bm{q}}\frac{d\bm{q}}{dt}

(8)

and similarly for the second term in Eq. (
7
) involving
\bm{p}
(here we note that we’re using a somewhat abstract notation, as the derivatives with respect to the variables
\bm{q}=(\bm{q}_{1},\dots,\bm{q}_{N})
and
\bm{p}=(\bm{p}_{1},\dots,\bm{p}_{N})
are really sums over derivatives with respect to individual particle positions
\bm{q}_{i}
and momenta
\bm{p}_{i}
; introduction of such more explicit notation is trivial and we omit it here for clarity). These approximations will become exact in the limit
\Delta\bm{q}\to 0,\Delta\bm{p}\to 0
, leading to

\displaystyle\frac{d\Delta V}{dt}=\Delta\bm{q}\Delta\bm{p}\bigg(\frac{d}{d\bm{q}}\frac{d\bm{q}}{dt}+\frac{d}{d\bm{p}}\frac{d\bm{p}}{dt}\bigg)~.

(9)

The equations of motion,
{d\bm{q}}/{dt}
and
{d\bm{p}}/{dt}
, are given by the Hamilton equations, yielding

\displaystyle\frac{d\Delta V}{dt}=\Delta\bm{q}\Delta\bm{p}\bigg(\frac{d}{d\bm{q}}\frac{dH}{d\bm{p}}-\frac{d}{d\bm{p}}\frac{dH}{d\bm{q}}\bigg)=0~,

(10)

where in the second equality we used the fact that the Hamiltonian is a well-behaved function satisfying
\left(d{{}^{2}H}/d{\bm{q}d\bm{p}}\right)=\left(d{{}^{2}H}/d{\bm{p}d\bm{q}}\right)
. Thus we have shown that the volume of the phase space associated with the chosen subset of the statistical ensemble does not change in time, and from this it immediately follows that the associated probability density doesn’t change as well,

\displaystyle\frac{df(\Delta V)}{dt}=0~.

(11)

This, in fact, is the Liouville theorem, usually written more explicitly as

\displaystyle\frac{df(t,\bm{q},\bm{p})}{dt}=\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{t}}+\frac{d{\bm{q}}}{d{t}}\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{\bm{q}}}+\frac{d{\bm{p}}}{d{t}}\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{\bm{p}}}=0~,

(12)

or, using the Poisson bracket, as

\displaystyle\frac{df(t,\bm{q},\bm{p})}{dt}=\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{t}}+\big\{f,H\big\}=0~,

(13)

and it simply states that the probability density associated with a given volume element of the phase space does not change in time.

That the Liouville theorem is satisfied is extremely important to some of the most basic thermodynamic assumptions. Let us take for example the assumption of equal
a priori
probabilities, which states that all microstates
(\bm{q},\bm{p})
corresponding to a chosen macrostate
X
have an equal probability density. The Liouville theorem ensures that if this statement is initially true, then it will also remain true with time evolution. More generally, any state characterized by a given initial probability density will over time evolve through states characterized by the same probability density.

Let us see what the Liouville theorem implies for the time evolution of observable averages,

\displaystyle\frac{d{\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle}}{d{t}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}~\frac{d{}}{d{t}}\int d\bm{q}d\bm{p}~\mathcal{O}(\bm{q},\bm{p})~f(t,\bm{q},\bm{p})

(14)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}~\int d\bm{q}d\bm{p}~\mathcal{O}(\bm{q},\bm{p})~\frac{\partial{f(t,\bm{q},\bm{p})}}{\partial{t}}~,

where
\mathcal{N}
is a normalization factor. Eq. (
12
) together with Hamilton’s equations allows one to write this as

\displaystyle\frac{d{\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle}}{d{t}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\mathcal{N}~\int d\bm{q}d\bm{p}~\mathcal{O}(\bm{q},\bm{p})\left(\frac{\partial{H}}{\partial{\bm{p}}}\frac{\partial{f(t,\bm{q},\bm{p})}}{\partial{\bm{q}}}-\frac{\partial{H}}{\partial{\bm{q}}}\frac{\partial{f(t,\bm{q},\bm{p})}}{\partial{\bm{p}}}\right)~,

(15)

which can be further rewritten using integration by parts,

\displaystyle\frac{d{\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle}}{d{t}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}~\int d\bm{q}d\bm{p}~\left(\frac{\partial{\mathcal{O}(\bm{q},\bm{p})}}{\partial{\bm{q}}}\frac{\partial{H}}{\partial{\bm{p}}}-\frac{\partial{\mathcal{O}(\bm{q},\bm{p})}}{\partial{\bm{p}}}\frac{\partial{H}}{\partial{\bm{q}}}\right)~f(t,\bm{q},\bm{p})~,

(16)

where some of the terms disappear by the same arguments as used in Eq. (
10
). Using the definition of the Poisson bracket we arrive at

\displaystyle\frac{d{\langle\mathcal{O}\rangle}}{d{t}}=\mathcal{N}~\int d\bm{q}d\bm{p}~\big\{\mathcal{O}(\bm{q},\bm{p}),H\big\}~f(t,\bm{q},\bm{p})=\langle\big\{\mathcal{O}(\bm{q},\bm{p}),H\big\}\rangle~.

(17)

Interestingly, the Liouville theorem implies that it is impossible for any closed system, initialized away from equilibrium, to come to an equilibrium. This follows from the discussion above: If initially the system occupies a region of phase space represented by a given microstate
(\bm{q},\bm{p})
which is characterized by a particular probability density
f(t,\bm{x},\bm{p})
and corresponds to some non-equilibrium macrostate
X
, then even after arbitrarily long evolution in the phase space the system will be still described by the same probability density
f
and therefore it will correspond to the same macrostate
X
; that is, the system will only explore the subspace
\Gamma_{X}
of the full phase space.

Naturally, everyday experience tells us that in general systems do equilibrate. First, this is because one can never prepare a truly isolated system. Second, the Liouville theorem rests on the assumption that the considered region of the phase space is infinitesimally small, see Eq. (
8
). Any small but finite region of the phase space can be considered to consist of many even smaller regions. While these regions will initially follow very similar evolutions (and so the associated occupied volumes of the phase space will be initially adjacent), over time their phase space trajectories will spread out. This is true for any region of the phase space, so that after some time any small but finite region of phase space will contain states that originate from many different points in the phase space. Each of these microstates maintains the same probability density as at the initial time, but the probability density associated with a region spanned by any finite values of
\Delta x
and
\Delta p
