will be an average of the many probability densities from different contributing microstates. Ultimately, the system comes to an equilibrium when the probability densities become sufficiently mixed, and the probability density associated with any finite phase space volume element becomes a uniform average. Most importantly, the Liouville theorem only considers the effects due to the drift of particles in the phase space (note that the left-hand side of Eq. (
12
) is in fact a hydrodynamic derivative, and the Liouville theorem states that the phase space density behaves like an incompressible fluid), and in particular it does not take into account the effects of particle collisions and particle transformations such as decays or resonance formation. An isolated system in which particle collisions and particle-number–changing processes take place will, over time, come to an equilibrium.

Appendix 23.D
The BBGKY hierarchy

The phase space distribution function
f(t,\bm{q},\bm{p})
contains detailed information about the system. Note that using an unabbreviated notation, the phase space distribution function for
N
particles can be explicitly written as

\displaystyle f(t,\bm{q},\bm{p})=f_{N}\big(t,\bm{q}_{1},\bm{p}_{1},\bm{q}_{2},\bm{p}_{2},\dots,\bm{q}_{N},\bm{p}_{N}\big)~.

(18)

This means that
f_{N}
gives the probability that at time
t
particle 1 has position
\bm{q}_{1}
and momentum
\bm{p}_{1}
, particle 2 has position
\bm{q}_{2}
and momentum
\bm{p}_{2}
, and so on. For most applications, however, we are not interested in such detailed knowledge. For example, calculating the pressure of the gas only requires knowing whether any particle can be found at a given position
\bm{q}^{\prime}
with a momentum of
\bm{p}^{\prime}
. This information is contained in a one-particle distribution function
f_{1}(t,\bm{q}^{\prime},\bm{p}^{\prime})
, which can be obtained from the
N
-particle distribution function through

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274ptf_{1}(t,\bm{q}^{\prime},\bm{p}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d\bm{q}_{1}d\bm{p}_{1}\int d\bm{q}_{2}d\bm{p}_{2}~\dots\int d\bm{q}_{N}d\bm{p}_{N}~f_{N}\big(t,\bm{q}_{1},\bm{p}_{1},\bm{q}_{2},\bm{p}_{2},\dots,\bm{q}_{N},\bm{p}_{N}\big)

(19)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\times~\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta^{3}\big(\bm{q}^{\prime}-\bm{q}_{i}\big)\delta^{3}\big(\bm{p}^{\prime}-\bm{p}_{i}\big)\right)~.

By assuming that the distribution function
f_{N}
is symmetric with respect to permuting the particles, the above equation becomes

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274ptf_{1}(t,\bm{q}^{\prime},\bm{p}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle N~\int\prod_{i=2}^{N}d\bm{q}_{i}d\bm{p}_{i}~f_{N}\big(t,\bm{q}_{1}=\bm{q}^{\prime},\bm{p}_{1}=\bm{p}^{\prime},\bm{q}_{2},\bm{p}_{2},\dots,\bm{q}_{N},\bm{p}_{N}\big)~.

(20)

Similarly, a two-particle distribution is given by

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274ptf_{1}(t,\bm{q}^{\prime},\bm{p}^{\prime},\bm{q}^{\prime\prime},\bm{p}^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle N(N-1)~\int\prod_{i=3}^{N}d\bm{q}_{i}d\bm{p}_{i}

(21)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\times~f_{N}\big(t,\bm{q}_{1}=\bm{q}^{\prime},\bm{p}_{1}=\bm{p}^{\prime},\bm{q}_{2}=\bm{q}^{\prime\prime},\bm{p}_{2}=\bm{p}^{\prime\prime},\dots,\bm{q}_{N},\bm{p}_{N}\big)~,

and in general the
s
-particle distribution is defined by

\displaystyle f_{s}(t,\bm{q}_{1},\bm{p}_{1},\dots,\bm{q}_{s},\bm{p}_{s})=\frac{N!}{(N-s)!}\int\prod_{i=s+1}^{N}d\bm{q}_{i}d\bm{p}_{i}~f_{N}(t,\bm{q},\bm{p})~.

(22)

The evolution equation for the
s
-particle distribution
f_{s}
can be derived within an approach named after the works of Nikolay Bogolyubov, Max Born, Herbert Green, John Kirkwood, and Jacques Yvon, and often called the Bogoliubov-Born-Green-Kirkwood-Yvon (BBGKY) hierarchy for reasons that will become apparent. Consider a system of
N
particles whose Hamiltonian contains terms related to the kinetic energy
T
, the external potential
U
, and the two-body interaction
V
,

\displaystyle H(\bm{q},\bm{p})=\sum_{i=1}^{N}T(\bm{p}_{i})+\sum_{i=1}^{N}U(\bm{q}_{i})+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}V(\bm{q}_{i}-\bm{q}_{j})~.

(23)

(Note that in principle one should further include terms corresponding to three-body interactions, four-body interactions, and so on; in practice, these terms can be often neglected, which is an excellent approximation, e.g., in the case of dilute gases.) The strategy to compute the
s
-particle distribution function is to divide the Hamiltonian
H(\bm{q},\bm{p})
, Eq. (
23
), into a part related to the
s
particles in question, a part related to the remaining
N-s
particles, and an interaction term between the two groups of particles,

\displaystyle H=H_{s}+H_{N-s}+H^{\prime}~,

(24)

with

\displaystyle H_{s}=\sum_{i=1}^{s}T(\bm{p}_{i})+\sum_{i=1}^{s}U(\bm{q}_{i})+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{s}\sum_{j=1}^{s}V(\bm{q}_{i}-\bm{q}_{j})~,

(25)

\displaystyle H_{N-s}=\sum_{i=s+1}^{N}T(\bm{p}_{i})+\sum_{i=s+1}^{N}U(\bm{q}_{i})+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=s+1}^{N}\sum_{j=s+1}^{N}V(\bm{q}_{i}-\bm{q}_{j})~,

(26)

\displaystyle H^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{s}\sum_{j=s+1}^{N}V(\bm{q}_{i}-\bm{q}_{j})~.

(27)

Using Eqs. (
13
) and (
22
) we can then write the time evolution of the
s
-body distribution function as

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{f_{s}}}{\partial{t}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{N!}{(N-s)!}\int\prod_{i=s+1}^{N}d\bm{q}_{i}d\bm{p}_{i}~\frac{\partial{f_{N}}}{\partial{t}}

(28)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{N!}{(N-s)!}\int\prod_{i=s+1}^{N}d\bm{q}_{i}d\bm{p}_{i}~\bigg[\big\{f_{N},H_{s}\big\}+\big\{f_{N},H_{N-s}\big\}+\big\{f_{N},H^{\prime}\big\}\bigg]~.

Note that the first of the Poisson brackets is explicitly given by

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt\big\{f_{N},H_{s}\big\}=\sum_{a=1}^{N}\bigg(\frac{\partial{f_{N}}}{\partial{\bm{q}_{a}}}\cdot\frac{\partial{H_{s}}}{\partial{\bm{p}_{a}}}-\frac{\partial{f_{N}}}{\partial{\bm{p}_{a}}}\cdot\frac{\partial{H_{s}}}{\partial{\bm{q}_{a}}}\bigg)=\sum_{a=1}^{s}\bigg(\frac{\partial{f_{N}}}{\partial{\bm{q}_{a}}}\cdot\frac{\partial{H_{s}}}{\partial{\bm{p}_{a}}}-\frac{\partial{f_{N}}}{\partial{\bm{p}_{a}}}\cdot\frac{\partial{H_{s}}}{\partial{\bm{q}_{a}}}\bigg)~,

(29)

so that the integrations and differentiations present in the first term in Eq. (
28
) are performed over different sets of variables, allowing us to exchange their order and arrive at

\displaystyle-\bigg\{\frac{N!}{(N-s)!}\int\prod_{i=s+1}^{N}d\bm{q}_{i}d\bm{p}_{i}f_{N},H_{s}\bigg\}=-\big\{f_{s},H_{s}\big\}~.

(30)

The second term in Eq. (
28
) vanishes, which can be seen by writing the integrand explicitly using Eq. (
26
),

\displaystyle\hskip-22.76219pt\int\prod_{i=s+1}^{N}d\bm{q}_{i}d\bm{p}_{i}~\sum_{a=s+1}^{N}\bigg[\frac{\partial{f_{N}}}{\partial{\bm{q}_{a}}}\cdot\frac{\partial{T(\bm{p}_{a})}}{\partial{\bm{p}_{a}}}-\frac{\partial{f_{N}}}{\partial{\bm{p}_{a}}}\cdot\bigg(\frac{\partial{U(\bm{q}_{a})}}{\partial{\bm{q}_{a}}}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=s+1}^{N}\frac{\partial{V(\bm{q}_{s}-\bm{q}_{j})}}{\partial{\bm{q}_{a}}}\bigg)\bigg]~,

(31)

and performing integration by parts with respect to
\bm{q}_{a}
in the first term and with respect to
\bm{p}_{a}
in the second term in the square bracket. Finally, because
H^{\prime}=H^{\prime}(\bm{q})
, the third term in Eq. (
28
) is explicitly

\displaystyle-\frac{N!}{(N-s)!}\int\prod_{i=s+1}^{N}d\bm{q}_{i}d\bm{p}_{i}~\bigg[\bigg(\sum_{a=1}^{s}\frac{\partial{f_{N}}}{\partial{\bm{p}_{a}}}+\sum_{a=s+1}^{N}\frac{\partial{f_{N}}}{\partial{\bm{p}_{a}}}\bigg)\cdot\sum_{j=s+1}^{N}\frac{\partial{V(\bm{q}_{a}-\bm{q}_{j})}}{\partial{\bm{q}_{a}}}\bigg]~,

(32)

where the sum over all particles has been divided into two parts. The second term disappears through integration by parts, and because the derivatives over
\bm{q}_{a}
are the same for each term in the sum over
j
, Eq. (
32
) is equal to

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt-(N-s)\frac{N!}{(N-s)!}\int\prod_{i=s+1}^{N}d\bm{q}_{i}d\bm{p}_{i}~\sum_{a=1}^{s}\frac{\partial{f_{N}}}{\partial{\bm{p}_{a}}}\cdot\frac{\partial{V(\bm{q}_{a}-\bm{q}_{s+1})}}{\partial{\bm{q}_{a}}}

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt=-\frac{N!}{\big(N-(s+1)\big)!}\sum_{a=1}^{s}\int d\bm{q}_{s+1}d\bm{p}_{s+1}~\frac{\partial{V(\bm{q}_{a}-\bm{q}_{s+1})}}{\partial{\bm{q}_{a}}}\cdot\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\bm{p}_{a}}}\int\prod_{i=s+2}^{N}d\bm{q}_{i}d\bm{p}_{i}~f_{N}

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt=-\sum_{a=1}^{s}\int d\bm{q}_{s+1}d\bm{p}_{s+1}~\frac{\partial{V(\bm{q}_{a}-\bm{q}_{s+1})}}{\partial{\bm{q}_{a}}}\cdot\frac{\partial{f_{s+1}}}{\partial{\bm{p}_{a}}}~,

(33)

where in the last equality we have used Eq. (
22
). Altogether, Eq. (
28
) becomes

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{f_{s}}}{\partial{t}}+\big\{f_{s},H_{s}\big\}=\sum_{a=1}^{s}\int d\bm{q}_{s+1}d\bm{p}_{s+1}~\frac{\partial{V(\bm{q}_{a}-\bm{q}_{s+1})}}{\partial{\bm{q}_{a}}}\cdot\frac{\partial{f_{s+1}}}{\partial{\bm{p}_{a}}}~.

(34)

It is easy to see that if there are no interactions between the particles described by the
s
-particle distribution function, then we arrive at the Liouville theorem, Eq. (
13
), for
f_{s}
. If, on the other hand, the interactions with the other
N-s
particles are non-zero, then the change of
f_{s}
in time is equal to the right-hand side of Eq. (
34
), often called the collision term or collision integral because of the fact that it sums over possible interactions (collisions) of particles from the group of
s
particles with particles from the group of
N-s
particles, weighted by the probability
f_{s+1}
of finding a particle from the
N-s
group. This results in a sequence, or hierarchy, of equations, where
df_{1}/dt
depends on
f_{2}
,
df_{2}/dt
depends on
f_{3}
, and so on, which is the origin of the name “BBGKY hierarchy of equations”.

As it is, the BBGKY hierarchy, Eq. (
34
), retains all the information originally contained within the
N
-body distribution function
f_{N}
, and solving for the consecutive
s
-body distribution functions
f_{s}
using Eq. (
34
) is as complicated as solving for the original
N
-body distribution function
f_{N}
. However, in contrast to the Liouville equation specifying
f_{N}
, the BBGKY hierarchy can be systematically truncated, leading to solvable problems.

Appendix 23.E
The Boltzmann equation

A detailed discussion of the derivation of the Boltzmann equation from the BBGKY hierarchy in the dilute regime can be found in Ref.
[
229
]
; here we briefly present the main steps. We focus on the first two equations in the BBGKY hierarchy, obtained by setting
s=1
and
s=2
in Eq. (
34
). Using Eq. (
25
) and assuming that the potential is symmetric,
V(\bm{q}_{1}-\bm{q}_{2})=V(\bm{q}_{2}-\bm{q}_{1})
(from which it follows that
\partial{V(\bm{q}_{1}-\bm{q}_{2})}/\partial{\bm{q}_{1}}=-\partial{V(\bm{q}_{2}-\bm{q}_{1})}/\partial{\bm{q}_{2}}
), we arrive at

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{f_{1}}}{\partial{t}}+\frac{\partial{T(\bm{p}_{1})}}{\partial{\bm{p}_{1}}}\cdot\frac{\partial{f_{1}}}{\partial{\bm{q}_{1}}}-\frac{\partial{U(\bm{q}_{1})}}{\partial{\bm{q}_{1}}}\cdot\frac{\partial{f_{1}}}{\partial{\bm{p}_{1}}}=\int d^{3}\bm{q}_{2}d^{3}\bm{p}_{2}~\frac{\partial{V(\bm{q}_{1}-\bm{q}_{2})}}{\partial{\bm{q}_{1}}}\cdot\frac{\partial{f_{2}}}{\partial{\bm{p}_{1}}}

(35)

and

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\frac{\partial{f_{2}}}{\partial{t}}+\left[\frac{\partial{T(\bm{p}_{1})}}{\partial{\bm{p}_{1}}}\cdot\frac{\partial{f_{2}}}{\partial{\bm{q}_{1}}}+\frac{\partial{T(\bm{p}_{2})}}{\partial{\bm{p}_{2}}}\cdot\frac{\partial{f_{2}}}{\partial{\bm{q}_{2}}}\right]-\left[\frac{\partial{U(\bm{q}_{1})}}{\partial{\bm{q}_{1}}}\cdot\frac{\partial{f_{2}}}{\partial{\bm{p}_{1}}}+\frac{\partial{U(\bm{q}_{2})}}{\partial{\bm{q}_{2}}}\cdot\frac{\partial{f_{2}}}{\partial{\bm{p}_{2}}}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt-~\frac{\partial{V(\bm{q}_{1}-\bm{q}_{2})}}{\partial{\bm{q}_{1}}}\cdot\left[\frac{\partial{f_{2}}}{\partial{\bm{p}_{1}}}-\frac{\partial{f_{2}}}{\partial{\bm{p}_{2}}}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=\int d^{3}\bm{q}_{3}d^{3}\bm{p}_{3}~\left[\frac{\partial{V(\bm{q}_{1}-\bm{q}_{3})}}{\partial{\bm{q}_{1}}}\cdot\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\bm{p}_{1}}}+\frac{\partial{V(\bm{q}_{2}-\bm{q}_{3})}}{\partial{\bm{q}_{2}}}\cdot\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\bm{p}_{2}}}\right]f_{3}~.

(36)

All terms acting on
f_{s}
in above equations have dimensions of inverse time, and their magnitude can be estimated by dimensional analysis. We can define the timescale
\tau_{U}
associated with terms of the form

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{U(\bm{q})}}{\partial{\bm{q}}}\cdot\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\bm{p}}}\propto\frac{1}{\tau_{U}}\propto\frac{v}{L}~,

(37)

where
v
is the typical particle velocity and
L
is the length scale over which the external potential
U
varies. Likewise, we can define the timescale
\tau_{V}
associated with terms involving the particle-particle interactions (collisions),

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{V}}{\partial{\bm{q}}}\cdot\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\bm{p}}}\propto\frac{1}{\tau_{V}}\propto\frac{v}{d},

(38)

where
d
is the effective range of the scattering potential. Finally, we note that while a scattering term only occurs on the right-hand side of Eq. (
35
), in case of Eq. (
36
) there are scattering terms both on the left- and right-hand side of the equation. The relative magnitude of these terms will be proportional to the ratio
f_{3}/f_{2}
. Over small volumes
\Delta V\approx d^{3}
corresponding to the effective range of the potential
d
,
f_{2}
is proportional to the number of particle pairs in
\Delta V
, while
f_{3}
is proportional to the number of particle triplets, therefore
f_{3}/f_{2}\propto n
, where
n
is the number density of particles. Consequently, the time scale
\tau_{I}
associated with the collision integral on the right-hand side of Eq. (
35
) is given by

\displaystyle\int d^{3}\bm{q}_{3}d^{3}\bm{p}_{3}~\frac{\partial{V}}{\partial{\bm{q}}}\cdot\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{\bm{p}}}~\frac{f_{3}}{f_{2}}\propto\frac{1}{\tau_{I}}\propto\frac{nd^{3}}{\tau_{V}}\propto nvd^{2}~.

(39)

From the above equation one can see that
\tau_{I}
is in fact the mean free time, that is a typical period between subsequent collisions of the same particle; in agreement with the basic intuition, the mean free time is inversely proportional to particle density, the particle’s velocity, and the effective “cross section area” of the particle given by the square of interaction range
d
.

For dilute systems, the average number of particles in the volume associated with the scattering range is very small,
nd^{3}\ll 1
. Therefore the scattering term on the right-hand side of Eq. (
35
) will be negligible as compared to the scattering term on the left-hand side of the same equation, and it is reasonable to truncate the BBGKY hierarchy at
s=2
by setting the right-hand side of Eq. (
35
) to zero. Furthermore, typically the time scale associated with scattering
\tau_{V}
is much shorter than the time scale associated with the variation of the external potential
\tau_{U}
, which allows one to neglect terms proportional to
1/\tau_{U}
in Eq. (
35
). Finally, additionally assuming that
\partial{f}/\partial{t}\ll 1
yields

\displaystyle\left[\frac{\partial{T(\bm{p}_{1})}}{\partial{\bm{p}_{1}}}\cdot\frac{\partial{f_{2}}}{\partial{\bm{q}_{1}}}+\frac{\partial{T(\bm{p}_{2})}}{\partial{\bm{p}_{2}}}\cdot\frac{\partial{f_{2}}}{\partial{\bm{q}_{2}}}\right]-\frac{\partial{V(\bm{q}_{1}-\bm{q}_{2})}}{\partial{\bm{q}_{1}}}\cdot\left[\frac{\partial{f_{2}}}{\partial{\bm{p}_{1}}}-\frac{\partial{f_{2}}}{\partial{\bm{p}_{2}}}\right]=0~.

(40)

The derivatives with respect to
\partial{}/\partial{\bm{q}_{1}}
and
\partial{}/\partial{\bm{q}_{2}}
can be expressed in terms of the center-of-mass and relative coordinate,
\bm{Q}=(\bm{q}_{1}+\bm{q}_{2})/2
and
\bm{q}=(\bm{q}_{2}-\bm{q}_{1})/2
, as we have
d\bm{q}_{1}=d\bm{Q}+d\bm{q}
and
d\bm{q}_{2}=d\bm{Q}-d\bm{q}
. The two-body distribution function
f_{2}
is expected to vary slowly along the
\bm{Q}
direction and appreciably over the
\bm{q}
direction (this can be understood based on the fact that the average position of two particles
\bm{Q}
is governed by the long-range part of the interaction, while their relative position
\bm{q}
is governed by the short range part of the interaction), so that
\partial{}/\partial{\bm{q}_{1}}\approx-\partial{}/\partial{\bm{q}_{2}}\approx\partial{}/\partial{\bm{q}}
, and we can rewrite Eq. (
40
) as

\displaystyle\left[\frac{\partial{T(\bm{p}_{1})}}{\partial{\bm{p}_{1}}}-\frac{\partial{T(\bm{p}_{2})}}{\partial{\bm{p}_{2}}}\right]\cdot\frac{\partial{f_{2}}}{\partial{\bm{q}}}=\frac{\partial{V(\bm{q}_{1}-\bm{q}_{2})}}{\partial{\bm{q}_{1}}}\cdot\left[\frac{\partial{f_{2}}}{\partial{\bm{p}_{1}}}-\frac{\partial{f_{2}}}{\partial{\bm{p}_{2}}}\right]~.

(41)

We can use the above relation on the right-hand side of Eq. (
35
), where we can perform the substitution
\partial{f_{2}}/\partial{\bm{p}_{1}}\to\partial{f_{2}}/\partial{\bm{p}_{1}}-\partial{f_{2}}/\partial{\bm{p}_{2}}
because
\partial{f_{2}}/\partial{\bm{p}_{1}}
is a complete derivative which integrates to zero. Additionally, we use the fact that for a relativistic system
\partial{T(\bm{p})}/\partial{\bm{p}}=\bm{p}/E=\bm{v}
, and in this way we arrive at

\displaystyle\hskip-8.53581pt\frac{\partial{f_{1}}}{\partial{t}}+\frac{\bm{p}_{1}}{E_{1}}\cdot\frac{\partial{f_{1}}}{\partial{\bm{q}_{1}}}-\frac{\partial{U(\bm{q}_{1})}}{\partial{\bm{q}_{1}}}\cdot\frac{\partial{f_{1}}}{\partial{\bm{p}_{1}}}=\int d^{3}\bm{q}d^{3}\bm{p}_{2}~\big(\bm{v}_{1}-\bm{v}_{2}\big)\cdot\frac{\partial{f_{2}(t,\bm{q}_{1},\bm{p}_{1},\bm{q},\bm{p}_{2})}}{\partial{\bm{q}}}~.

(42)

On the right-hand side of the above equation we have a derivative of
f_{2}
with respect to
\bm{q}
along the direction of th relative motion
\bm{p}=\bm{p}_{2}-\bm{p}_{1}
, and it is convenient to express
\bm{q}
in a new basis in which one axis, denoted with
a
, is parallel to
\bm{p}=\bm{p}_{2}-\bm{p}_{1}
, while the remaining two coordinates form a plane perpendicular to
\bm{p}
,

\displaystyle\bm{q}\to\bm{a}+\bm{b}\hskip 15.0pt{\textrm{and}}\hskip 15.0ptd^{3}\bm{q}\to d\bm{a}~d^{2}\bm{b}~;

(43)

we note that
\bm{b}
is an impact parameter vector, which in particular is zero for a head-on collision where
\bm{q}_{1}-\bm{q}_{2}
is parallel to
\bm{p}_{1}-\bm{p}_{2}
. We can then attempt to integrate Eq. (
42
) over the
a
coordinate. As this integral is performed, we go from negative relative distances, starting from some lower boundary value
a_{-}
, through a zero relative distance which is where the collision takes place, and then through positive relative distances up to some upper boundary value
a_{+}
(on a side note,
|a_{+}-a_{-}|
can be taken to be small because
f_{2}
changes appreciably only over distances comparable with the collision range
d
). In this way, we “observe” the system from some time before the collision to some time after the collision. Importantly, the momenta of the individual particles are changed throughout the collision even though the total momentum is conserved,

\displaystyle\bm{p}_{1}+\bm{p}_{2}=\bm{p}_{1}^{\prime}+\bm{p}_{2}^{\prime}~.

(44)

Therefore the integral of Eq. (
42
) over the
a
coordinate can be written as

\displaystyle\frac{d{f_{1}}}{d{t}}=\int d^{2}\bm{b}d^{3}\bm{p}_{2}~\big|\bm{v}_{1}-\bm{v}_{2}\big|\Big[f_{2}(t,\bm{q}_{1},\bm{p}_{1}^{\prime},\bm{b},a_{+},\bm{p}_{2}^{\prime})-f_{2}(t,\bm{q}_{1},\bm{p}_{1},\bm{b},a_{-},\bm{p}_{2})\Big]~,

(45)

where
|\bm{v}_{1}-\bm{v}_{2}|
is the relative speed. As the next step, we change the integration variable from the impact parameter vector to the solid angle
\Omega
,

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt\frac{d{f_{1}}}{d{t}}=\int d\Omega d^{3}\bm{p}_{2}~\left|\frac{d\sigma}{d\Omega}\right|\big|\bm{v}_{1}-\bm{v}_{2}\big|\Big[f_{2}(t,\bm{q}_{1},\bm{p}_{1}^{\prime},\bm{b},a_{+},\bm{p}_{2}^{\prime})-f_{2}(t,\bm{q}_{1},\bm{p}_{1},\bm{b},a_{-},\bm{p}_{2})\Big]~,

(46)

where the Jacobian of the transformation
\left|{d\sigma}/{d\Omega}\right|
has a dimension of area and is known as the differential cross section. Finally, we use the assumption of molecular chaos, according to which the two-body distribution function is given by a product of one-body distribution functions,

\displaystyle f_{2}(t,\bm{q}_{1},\bm{p}_{1},\bm{b},a_{\pm},\bm{p}_{2})=f_{1}(t,\bm{q}_{1},\bm{p}_{1})f_{1}(t,\bm{q}_{1},\bm{p}_{2})~,

(47)

and which means that, except for the moment in which the collision take place, the particles are assumed to be uncorrelated. This allows us to arrive at the Boltzmann equation,

\displaystyle\hskip-42.67912pt\frac{\partial{f_{1}}}{\partial{t}}+\frac{\bm{p}_{1}}{E_{1}}\cdot\frac{\partial{f_{1}}}{\partial{\bm{q}_{1}}}-\frac{\partial{U(\bm{q}_{1})}}{\partial{\bm{q}_{1}}}\cdot\frac{\partial{f_{1}}}{\partial{\bm{p}_{1}}}

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt=\int d\Omega d^{3}\bm{p}_{2}~\left|\frac{d\sigma}{d\Omega}\right|\big|\bm{v}_{1}-\bm{v}_{2}\big|\Big[f_{1}(t,\bm{q}_{1},\bm{p}_{1}^{\prime})f_{1}(t,\bm{q}_{1},\bm{p}_{2}^{\prime})-f_{1}(t,\bm{q}_{1},\bm{p}_{1})f_{1}(t,\bm{q}_{1},\bm{p}_{2})\Big]~,

(48)

which is an integrodifferential equation for the evolution of the one-body distribution
f_{1}
.

Although various parts of the above derivation sketch have characteristics of a
deux ex machina
, the form of Eq. (
48
) can be also argued on phenomenological grounds. The terms on the left-hand side are connected to a motion of a single particle in the external potential
U
. The collision term on the right-hand side describes the change in the probability to find a particle of momentum
\bm{p}_{1}
at
\bm{q}_{1}
that can happen due to a collision with another particle of momentum
\bm{p}_{2}
, in result of which both particles acquire new momenta
\bm{p}_{1}^{\prime}
and
\bm{p}_{2}^{\prime}
. This probability is proportional to the cross section for the scattering
\left|{d\sigma}/{d\Omega}\right|\big|
, the flux of incident particles
\big|\bm{v}_{1}-\bm{v}_{2}\big|
, the probability of finding the particles at a position
\bm{q}
with momenta
\bm{p}_{1}
and
\bm{p}_{2}
, and the probability of the final states with momenta
\bm{p}_{1}^{\prime}
and
\bm{p}_{2}^{\prime}
.

For describing relativistic heavy-ion collisions, it is useful to write Eq. (
48
) in a manifestly covariant way,

\displaystyle p^{\mu}\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{x^{\mu}}}+m\frac{\partial{\big(K^{\mu}f\big)}}{\partial{p^{\mu}}}=\int\frac{d^{3}\bm{p}_{2}}{E_{2}}\frac{d^{3}\bm{p}_{1}^{\prime}}{E_{1}^{\prime}}\frac{d^{3}\bm{p}_{2}^{\prime}}{E_{2}^{\prime}}~W\big(\bm{p}_{1}^{\prime},\bm{p}_{2}^{\prime}\leftarrow\bm{p},\bm{p}_{2}\big)\Big(f_{1}^{\prime}f_{2}^{\prime}-ff_{2}\Big)

(49)

where
K^{\mu}
is a four-force vector and
W\big(\bm{p}_{1}^{\prime},\bm{p}_{2}^{\prime}\leftarrow\bm{p},\bm{p}_{2}\big)
is a Lorentz invariant cross section for scattering of particles with momenta
\bm{p}
,
\bm{p}_{2}
into particles of momenta
\bm{p}_{1}^{\prime}
,
\bm{p}_{2}^{\prime}
. Here, it is useful to adopt an interpretation of the expression
\Big(f_{1}^{\prime}f_{2}^{\prime}-ff_{2}\Big)
in which the first term corresponds to particles scattering into the considered volume element (gain term), while the second term corresponds to particles scattering out of the considered volume element (loss term). Importantly, Boltzmann equation can also include quantum effects, which was first studied by Edwin Uehling and George Uhlenbeck
[
231
]
, leading to

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274ptp^{\mu}\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{x^{\mu}}}+m\frac{\partial{\big(K^{\mu}f\big)}}{\partial{p^{\mu}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\frac{d^{3}\bm{p}_{2}}{E_{2}}\frac{d^{3}\bm{p}_{1}^{\prime}}{E_{1}^{\prime}}\frac{d^{3}\bm{p}_{2}^{\prime}}{E_{2}^{\prime}}~

(50)

\displaystyle\hskip-99.58464pt\times~W\big(\bm{p}_{1}^{\prime},\bm{p}_{2}^{\prime}\leftarrow\bm{p},\bm{p}_{2}\big)\Bigg(f_{1}^{\prime}f_{2}^{\prime}\big(1-af\big)\big(1-af_{2}\big)-ff_{2}\big(1-af_{1}^{\prime}\big)\big(1-af_{2}^{\prime}\big)\Bigg)~,

where
a=1
for fermions and
a=-1
for bosons. The additional terms in the big round bracket can be easily understood: for example, in the case of fermions, a particle in a state 2’ can only scatter into a state 2 if that state is unoccupied, the probability of which is given by
1-f_{2}
. The above equation is known as the Boltzmann-Uehling-Uhlenbeck (BUU) equation.

So far we have only dealt with the Boltzmann equation for one particle species. To describe heavy-ion collisions one needs to include hundreds of hadron species which can collide with each other, decay, and form resonances. This means that the system is described by hundreds (as many as particle species) of coupled Boltzmann equations. This extremely complex system can be solved by means of Monte-Carlo simulations, where the particles are propagated using the equations of motion entering the left-hand side of Eq. (
50
) and the collision integral on the right-hand side is realized by explicitly simulating collisions, decays, and resonance formation. For more details see Chapter
3
.

Chapter 24
Pair distribution function and the second-order cumulant

The procedure to compute the radial distribution function
g_{i}(r)
, given by Eq. (
1
), can be generalized to the case of a continuous system described by a particle density distribution
n(\bm{r}^{\prime})
,

\displaystyle g_{i}(r,\Delta r)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d\bm{r}^{\prime}\Bigg(n(\bm{r}^{\prime})-1~\delta(\bm{r}_{i}-\bm{r}^{\prime})\Bigg)

(1)

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\times~\theta\Big(r+\Delta r-|\bm{r}_{i}-\bm{r}^{\prime}|\Big)\theta\Big(|\bm{r}_{i}-\bm{r}^{\prime}|-(r-\Delta r)\Big)~,

where care must be taken to subtract the self-contribution from the reference particle. Similarly, the pair distribution function
\widetilde{g}(r)
, Eq. (
2
), can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt\widetilde{g}(r,\Delta r)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{N}}{2}\int d\bm{r}^{\prime}\int d\bm{r}^{\prime\prime}~n(\bm{r}^{\prime})\Big(n(\bm{r}^{\prime\prime})-\delta(\bm{r}^{\prime}-\bm{r}^{\prime\prime})\Big)

(2)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times~\theta\Big(r+\Delta r-|\bm{r}^{\prime}-\bm{r}^{\prime\prime}|\Big)\theta\Big(|\bm{r}^{\prime}-\bm{r}^{\prime\prime}|-(r-\Delta r)\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{N}}{2}\int d\bm{r}^{\prime}\int d\bm{r}^{\prime\prime}~n(\bm{r}^{\prime})n(\bm{r}^{\prime\prime})~\theta\Big(r+\Delta r-|\bm{r}^{\prime}-\bm{r}^{\prime\prime}|\Big)\theta\Big(|\bm{r}^{\prime}-\bm{r}^{\prime\prime}|-(r-\Delta r)\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt-~\frac{\mathcal{N}}{2}\int d\bm{r}^{\prime}~n(\bm{r}^{\prime})~\theta\Big(\Delta r-r\Big)~.

We note that the second term is only non-zero when
r<\Delta r
, which is correct given that the self-contribution only needs to be subtracted if we consider the pair distribution function within a distance
\Delta r
around the reference particles.

It is possible to establish a connection between the pair distribution function and the second-order cumulant
\kappa_{2}
. For this, we consider the pair distribution function
\widetilde{g}(r)
at distances close to the reference particle, that is we put
r=0
, by means of which Eq. (
2
) becomes

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\widetilde{g}(0,\Delta r)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{N}}{2}\bigg[\int d\bm{r}^{\prime}\int d\bm{r}^{\prime\prime}~n(\bm{r}^{\prime})n(\bm{r}^{\prime\prime})~\theta\Big(\Delta r-|\bm{r}^{\prime}-\bm{r}^{\prime\prime}|\Big)-\int d\bm{r}^{\prime}~n(\bm{r}^{\prime})\bigg]~.

(3)

Let us assume that
\Delta r
is small and that within the distance
\Delta r
from
\bm{r}^{\prime}
the density is smooth enough for
n(\bm{r}^{\prime\prime})\approx n(\bm{r^{\prime}})
to hold, in which case

\displaystyle\hskip-19.91692pt\widetilde{g}(0,\Delta r)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{N}}{2}\bigg[\int d\bm{r}^{\prime}~\big[n(\bm{r}^{\prime})\big]^{2}~\int d\bm{r}^{\prime\prime}~\theta\Big(\Delta r-|\bm{r}^{\prime}-\bm{r}^{\prime\prime}|\Big)-\int d\bm{r}^{\prime}~n(\bm{r}^{\prime})\bigg]

(4)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{N}}{2}\left[V_{\Delta}\int d\bm{r}^{\prime}~\big[n(\bm{r}^{\prime})\big]^{2}-\int d\bm{r}^{\prime}~n(\bm{r}^{\prime})\right]~,

where
V_{\Delta}=(4/3)\pi r_{\Delta}^{3}
. Furthermore, let us divide the volume of the system
V
into cubes of volume
V_{\Delta}
,
N_{\textrm{cubes}}=V/V_{\Delta}
, and assume that we can safely discretize the remaining integrals in Eq. (
4
) according to
\int d\bm{r}^{\prime}~f(\bm{r^{\prime}})\to\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\textrm{cubes}}}V_{\Delta}f(\bm{r}_{i})
, where
\bm{r}_{i}
points to the center of each cube. With this and taking the number of particles in the
i
-th cube to be
N_{i}(\bm{r}_{i})\equiv V_{\Delta}n(\bm{r}_{i})
, Eq. (
4
) becomes

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt\widetilde{g}(0,\Delta r)

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}~\frac{1}{2}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\textrm{cubes}}}\big[N(\bm{r}_{i})\big]^{2}-\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\textrm{cubes}}}N(\bm{r}_{i})\right]~.

(5)

Since the normalization can be freely chosen given that
\widetilde{\rho}(0,\Delta r)
should be compared to a reference distribution for an ideal gas
\widetilde{\rho}_{0}(0,\Delta r)
, in particular we can take
\mathcal{N}=2/N_{\textrm{cubes}}
, so that finally

\displaystyle\hskip-19.91692pt\widetilde{g}(0,\Delta r)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N_{\textrm{cubes}}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\textrm{cubes}}}\big[N(\bm{r}_{i})\big]^{2}-\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\textrm{cubes}}}N(\bm{r}_{i})\right]~,

(6)

where
N_{\textrm{cubes}}
is determined by
\Delta r
.

It is clear from Eq. (
6
) that the radial distribution function of all distinct particle pairs at distances close to the reference particles is

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}(0,\Delta r)=M_{2}-M_{1}=F_{2}=\langle N(N-1)\rangle~,

(7)

where
M_{i}
and
F_{i}
are moments and factorial moments of the distribution, respectively. Moreover, assuming that the pair distribution function for uncorrelated pairs
\widetilde{g}_{0}(0,\Delta r)
is described by the Poisson distribution, for which
\langle N\rangle=\lambda
and
\langle N^{2}\rangle=\lambda^{2}+\lambda
(where
\lambda
is the mean), we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}_{0}(0,\Delta r)=\langle N\rangle^{2}~.

(8)

Let us consider the deviation of the behavior of the pair distribution function
\widetilde{g}(0,\Delta r)
from the ideal case of
\widetilde{g}_{0}(0,\Delta r)
, which can be conveniently done by considering the measure

\displaystyle R=\frac{\widetilde{g}~(0,\Delta r)}{\widetilde{g}_{0}(0,\Delta r)}-1~.

(9)

Using Eqs. (
7
) and (
8
) we can immediately rewrite this as

\displaystyle R=\frac{\langle N^{2}\rangle-\langle N\rangle-\langle N\rangle^{2}}{\langle N\rangle^{2}}=\frac{\kappa_{2}-\kappa_{1}}{\kappa_{1}^{2}}~.

(10)

In particular, provided that
\kappa_{1}>0
, we immediately obtain that
R
is bigger (smaller) than 0 if and only if the second-order cumulant ratio
\kappa_{2}/\kappa_{1}
is bigger (smaller) than 1, which can be alternatively expressed as in Eqs. (
11
) and (
12
).

We would like to stress that the above relations hold for an arbitrary distribution of particles, without any assumptions on the underlying physics, provided that the corresponding uncorrelated system can be described by the Poisson distribution. In any such system the sign of
[\widetilde{g}(r,\Delta r)/\widetilde{g}_{0}(r,\Delta r)]-1
at
r\to 0
is the same as the sign of
(\kappa_{2}/\kappa)-1
. In particular, it follows that
\widetilde{g}(r,\Delta r)/\widetilde{g}_{0}(r,\Delta r)<1
for systems where a repulsive interaction dominates at short distances (leading to a distribution more uniform than that of an ideal gas), while
\widetilde{g}(r,\Delta r)/\widetilde{g}_{0}(r,\Delta r)>1
for systems where an attractive interaction dominates at short distances (which leads to a distribution that is less uniform than that of an ideal gas).

Chapter 25
A posteriori
application of the parallel ensembles method

Figure 1:
A posteriori
construction of the parallel ensembles mode for a system undergoing nuclear spinodal fragmentation (for details see Section
1
). The upper row corresponds to the initialization time, while the lower row corresponds to
t=100\ \textrm{fm}/c
. The left-most boxes in both rows show the full ensemble event, in which test particles have been divided into
N_{T}=4
groups, marked with different colors. The remaining boxes in both rows show the state of each of the parallel ensembles obtained by dividing the original full ensemble event into
N_{T}=4
separate events. It is evident that the parallel ensemble systems mirror the evolution of the full ensemble.

The version of
SMASH
that we used did not have the option to run in a parallel ensembles mode (this option has been recently added to
SMASH

[
199
]
and is currently being tested). However, for simulations with all collision and decay channels turned off (such as we study in Chapter
4
), the concept of parallel ensembles can be safely employed
a posteriori
, that is at the analysis stage. Specifically, in each event we divide the
N_{T}N_{B}
test particles obtained from a full ensemble
SMASH
simulation (where
N_{B}
is the baryon number evolved in the simulation and
N_{T}
is the number of test particles per particle) into
N_{T}
separate groups; this process is shown in Fig.
1
for
N_{T}=4
. We then treat these groups as separate events. Each of these
a posteriori
constructed events is governed by
P_{N_{B}}(N_{i})
(see Section
2
). We stress that for a
SMASH
simulation run in the full ensemble mode with
N_{\textrm{ev}}
events and
N_{T}
test particles per particle, the corresponding calculation in the parallel ensembles mode will be characterized by
N_{T}N_{\textrm{ev}}
events with
N_{T}=1
test particles per particle.

Chapter 26
The isothermal speed of sound at
\mu=0

We want to compute the
\mu=0
limit of Eq. (
8
), repeated here for convenience,

\displaystyle c_{T}^{2}=\left(\frac{\mu}{T}\frac{\kappa_{2}}{\kappa_{1}}+\left(\frac{d\ln\kappa_{1}}{d\ln T}\right)_{\mu}\right)^{-1}~.

(1)

We will use the fact that at
\mu=0
we have by symmetry, for all values of the temperature
T
,
\kappa_{1}=0
and
\kappa_{3}=0
, from which it follows that
\left({d\kappa_{1}}/{dT}\right)_{\mu=0}=0
. We will also use the l’Hospital rule, according to which the
x\to x_{0}
limit of the ratio of two functions
f(x)
and
g(x)
such that either
\lim_{x\to x_{0}}f(x)=\lim_{x\to x_{0}}g(x)=0
or
\lim_{x\to x_{0}}f(x)=\lim_{x\to x_{0}}g(x)=\infty
is given by

\displaystyle\lim_{x\to x_{0}}\frac{f(x)}{g(x)}=\lim_{x\to x_{0}}\frac{f^{\prime}(x)}{g^{\prime}(x)}~,

(2)

provided that
g^{\prime}(x)\neq 0~\forall~x\neq x_{0}
and the limit
\lim_{x\to x_{0}}\frac{f^{\prime}(x)}{g^{\prime}(x)}
exists. We denote the evoking of the l’Hospital rule with a subscript “LH”.

For the first term in Eq. (
1
), assuming that
\kappa_{2}\neq 0
(which is equivalent to assuming that there are finite fluctuations in the system), we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}~\lim_{\mu\to 0}\frac{\mu\kappa_{2}}{\kappa_{1}}=\bigg|_{\textrm{LH}}\frac{1}{T}~\lim_{\mu\to 0}\frac{\left(\frac{d{(\mu\kappa_{2})}}{d{\mu}}\right)_{T}}{\left(\frac{d\kappa_{1}}{d\mu}\right)_{T}}=\frac{\kappa_{2}}{T}\lim_{\mu\to 0}~\frac{\kappa_{2}+\mu\frac{\kappa_{3}}{T}}{\frac{\kappa_{2}}{T}}=1~.

(3)

Then we deal with the second term,

\displaystyle\lim_{\mu\to 0}~\frac{T}{\kappa_{1}}\left(\frac{d\kappa_{1}}{dT}\right)_{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigg|_{\textrm{LH}}\lim_{\mu\to 0}~\frac{T}{\left(\frac{d\kappa_{1}}{d\mu}\right)_{T}}\frac{d}{d\mu}\bigg|_{T}\left(\frac{d\kappa_{1}}{dT}\right)_{\mu}=\lim_{\mu\to 0}~\frac{T}{\left(\frac{d\kappa_{1}}{d\mu}\right)_{T}}\frac{d}{dT}\bigg|_{\mu}\left(\frac{d\kappa_{1}}{d\mu}\right)_{T}

(4)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\mu\to 0}~\frac{T}{\frac{\kappa_{2}}{T}}\frac{d}{dT}\bigg|_{\mu}\left(\frac{\kappa_{2}}{T}\right)=\lim_{\mu\to 0}~\left(\frac{d\ln\left(\frac{\kappa_{2}}{T}\right)}{d\ln T}\right)_{\mu}~.

Here the limit can be now taken simply by taking the value at
\mu=0
,

\displaystyle\left(\frac{d\ln\left(\frac{\kappa_{2}}{T}\right)}{d\ln T}\right)_{\mu=0}=\frac{T^{2}}{\kappa_{2}\big|_{\mu=0}}\left[\frac{1}{T}\left(\frac{d\kappa_{2}}{dT}\right)_{\mu=0}-\frac{\kappa_{2}\big|_{\mu=0}}{T^{2}}\right]=\frac{T}{\kappa_{2}\big|_{\mu=0}}\left(\frac{d\kappa_{2}}{dT}\right)_{\mu=0}-1~.

(5)

Altogether,

\displaystyle\lim_{\mu\to 0}c_{T}^{2}=\left(\frac{T}{\kappa_{2}\big|_{\mu=0}}\left(\frac{d\kappa_{2}}{dT}\right)_{\mu=0}\right)^{-1}=\left(\frac{d\ln\kappa_{2}}{d\ln T}\right)_{\mu=0}^{-1}~.

(6)
